Workbook 2 — Statistical learning and regularization: why the hacky bullshit works

Mysteries of Deep Learning, Iliad Intensive 2026

Statistical learning theory is the mathematics of learning a rule from finitely many examples and asking how well the rule does on examples it has not seen. Deep learning practice is full of tricks that look like hacks from the outside: weight decay, early stopping, dropout, data augmentation, “flat minima”, training straight through the point where the training loss hits zero. This workbook builds the classical theory in models simple enough to solve exactly, mostly linear regression, and uses it to say what each trick does and what it does not. The aim is that when you meet a claim about generalization, you can say which classical statement it borrows, and in which regime that statement is a theorem.

How to use it. There are four sections. Pick whatever interests you, in any order; nobody is expected to finish. Each section and subsection opens with a short pitch saying what question it answers, and restates the setup it needs, so you can start almost anywhere. §1.A–1.C are the common background; if the words “excess risk”, “bias–variance” and “ridge” are already familiar, skim them. Derivations are exercises: a group such as 1.D.3 has numbered parts, and “1.D.3.2” means part 2 of it. Where a result is quoted rather than derived, the text says so, and each subsection links the textbook sections or papers its derivations come from.

Worked solutions. Every problem has a worked solution folded directly after it under “Solution”; click to reveal it when you want it. Solutions are for everyone. Hints are folded under “Hint”.

Section What is in it
1. Classical statistical learning 1.A kinds of learning problem (supervised, unsupervised, self-supervised, …); risk, empirical risk minimization, and two different things called “generalization”. 1.B least squares, solved exactly. 1.C bias–variance. 1.D regularization: a survey of the techniques, then, in linear regression, ridge as a prior and as a constraint; L^1 and sparsity; ridge as a spectral filter, and early stopping; input noise and data augmentation; dropout; when flatness can matter, and the “sharp minima can generalize” reading. 1.E kernels and the kernel trick: kernel ridge regression, random features, Gaussian processes. 1.F information theory and rate–distortion; the information bottleneck; the debate over whether deep networks compress.
2. Doesn’t some theorem make this impossible? No free lunch; universal approximation; VC dimension and capacity bounds; Zhang et al.’s random-label experiments, which make class-wide bounds vacuous; PAC-Bayes, which bounds the function you actually found.
3. Overfitting and double descent Minimum-norm least squares across the interpolation threshold; why optimal ridge removes the peak; benign overfitting; which regime LLMs are in.
4. Bayes, MLE, and generalization measures One story about how much a fitted model loses on new data, to leading order: the sandwich covariance, AIC, TIC and BIC; a single fit versus the Bayes ensemble; whether gradient noise and curvature cancel, and what SGD’s noise does; which published measures actually predict generalization.

Notation used throughout. Each subsection restates what it uses; this is the common core.

  • A data point is z=(x,y): an input x and a target or label y. Data are drawn i.i.d. from an unknown distribution P. The training sample is S=(z_1,\dots,z_n), so n is the number of training examples.
  • A model is a family of predictors f_\theta with parameters \theta\in\mathbb R^p, so p is the number of parameters. A loss \ell(\theta;z) scores one prediction: for example the squared loss (y-f_\theta(x))^2, or the log loss -\log p_\theta(y\mid x) for a model that outputs probabilities.
  • The population risk (test loss) is R(\theta)=\mathbb E_{z\sim P}\,\ell(\theta;z). The empirical risk (training loss) is \hat R_n(\theta)=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\ell(\theta;z_i).
  • Empirical risk minimization (ERM) returns \hat\theta_n\in\arg\min_\theta\hat R_n(\theta). The population minimizer \theta^*\in\arg\min_\theta R(\theta) is the best the model can do with infinite data; it need not be the truth.
  • The running example is the linear model y=x^\top\beta+\varepsilon, with noise \varepsilon of mean 0 and variance \sigma^2, independent of x. Stacking the inputs as rows gives the design matrix X\in\mathbb R^{n\times p}, and the targets form y\in\mathbb R^n. The ratio \gamma=p/n matters in §3.
  • Ridge regression with penalty \lambda>0 is \hat\beta_\lambda=\arg\min_b\frac1n\|y-Xb\|^2+\lambda\|b\|^2=(X^\top X+n\lambda I)^{-1}X^\top y. The thin singular value decomposition X=\sum_k s_k u_kv_k^\top has singular values s_k>0 and orthonormal singular vectors u_k\in\mathbb R^n, v_k\in\mathbb R^p; directions v_k with small s_k are the ones the data barely determine.
  • Logarithms are natural, so information is in nats.

1. Classical statistical learning

This is the textbook theory of learning from examples, from before deep learning: what “generalization” means, how to compute it exactly in linear regression, how the error splits into bias and variance, what regularization does, how kernels make linear methods nonlinear, and one information-theoretic account of what a good representation is. Most of it is solvable in closed form, which is the point: it gives exact reference cases against which claims about deep networks can be checked. Everything later in the workbook uses the vocabulary of 1.A–1.C.

1.A. Learning problems, risks, and two kinds of generalization

The question. “Deep networks generalize well” is said about two different quantities, and papers often switch between them. This subsection sets up the framework and separates them.

Kinds of learning problem. The traditional breakdown is by what data the learner sees and what it is asked to do.

Paradigm Data Goal Examples
Supervised Pairs (x,y) Predict y from x: regression (real y) or classification (discrete y) Linear regression, image classification
Unsupervised Inputs x only Model the distribution p(x), or find structure in it: density estimation, clustering, dimensionality reduction PCA, k-means, mixture models, VAEs, diffusion models
Self-supervised Inputs x only, with targets manufactured from x itself Supervised learning on those targets, usually to learn a representation or a generative model Next-token prediction (LLM pretraining), masked-token prediction, contrastive learning
Semi-supervised A few labelled pairs, many unlabelled inputs Use the structure of p(x) to predict y better Pseudo-labelling, consistency training
Reinforcement States, actions and rewards from interacting with an environment Find a policy that maximizes expected reward Games, robotics, RL fine-tuning of LLMs

Two other distinctions cut across the table. A discriminative model learns p(y\mid x) or a predictor of y; a generative model learns p(x) or p(x,y) and can sample new data. In batch (offline) learning the whole dataset is fixed in advance; in online learning examples arrive one at a time, and in RL the learner’s own actions decide which data it sees.

Statistical learning theory is mostly a theory of the supervised, i.i.d., batch case, and so is this workbook. That covers more than it sounds. Density estimation with the log loss is the same framework with \ell(\theta;x)=-\log p_\theta(x) and no label. LLM pretraining is supervised prediction of each token from its context (WB3), though the tokens of one document are not i.i.d. RL breaks the framework more seriously, since the data distribution depends on the policy being learned.

Risks. In the supervised setting, data z=(x,y) are drawn i.i.d. from an unknown P, and a learning rule maps the training sample S=(z_1,\dots,z_n) to parameters \hat\theta_n. The quality of \hat\theta_n is its population risk R(\hat\theta_n)=\mathbb E_{z\sim P}\,\ell(\hat\theta_n;z), the expected loss on a fresh example. The learner can compute only the empirical risk \hat R_n(\theta)=\frac1n\sum_i \ell(\theta;z_i), the average loss on the training sample. The commonest rule is empirical risk minimization (ERM): \hat\theta_n\in\arg\min\hat R_n. The benchmark is the population minimizer \theta^*\in\arg\min R, the best parameter in the model.

Two quantities. For any learning rule, not only ERM, define the generalization gap and the excess risk:

\mathrm{gap}_n=R(\hat\theta_n)-\hat R_n(\hat\theta_n), \qquad \mathcal E_n=R(\hat\theta_n)-R(\theta^*).

The gap compares test loss with training loss: is the training loss an honest estimate of how the model will do? The excess risk compares the fitted model with the best one available: did the learner find a good model? Both are random through S. The exercises below show that either can be large while the other is zero; that for ERM the expected gap bounds the expected excess risk; and that a held-out set measures one of them but not the other. 1.B computes both exactly for least squares.

1.A.1. Gap without excess, excess without gap
  1. Construct a model class and a distribution in which ERM has excess risk exactly zero but a gap equal to the noise variance.
  2. Construct a learning rule with expected gap exactly zero but excess risk bounded away from zero.
  3. Show that for ERM, \mathbb E\,\mathcal E_n\le\mathbb E\,\mathrm{gap}_n, and that \mathcal E_n\le 2\sup_\theta|R(\theta)-\hat R_n(\theta)|.
  4. You have a held-out set of m fresh samples from P. Which of the two quantities can you estimate, and how accurately? What would you need in order to estimate the other?
Hint

For 1, use squared loss with a continuous input, and let the class contain functions that agree with the best predictor except on a finite set of points. For 2, the rule need not look at the data. For 3, telescope \mathcal E_n through \hat R_n(\hat\theta_n) and \hat R_n(\theta^*), and ask which terms depend on S in a way that matters.

Solution

1. Take x\sim\mathrm{Unif}[0,1] and y=f^*(x)+\varepsilon with \varepsilon\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma^2), under squared loss. Let the class be f^* together with every “patched” function f_T, where T is a finite set of labelled points with distinct inputs: f_T(x)=y if (x,y)\in T and f_T(x)=f^*(x) otherwise. Each f_T differs from f^* on a set of measure zero, so R(f_T)=R(f^*)=\sigma^2 and every member of the class is a population minimizer. ERM returns f_S, which has zero training loss because the x_i are almost surely distinct. Hence

\mathcal E_n=0,\qquad \mathrm{gap}_n=\sigma^2 .

Memorizing costs nothing here because the memorized inputs never recur. This is a caricature of benign overfitting; 3.D has a real version.

2. Take the rule that ignores the data and outputs a fixed \theta_0 with R(\theta_0)>R(\theta^*). Since \theta_0 does not depend on S, \mathbb E\hat R_n(\theta_0)=R(\theta_0), so \mathbb E\,\mathrm{gap}_n=0. Meanwhile \mathcal E_n=R(\theta_0)-R(\theta^*)>0 for every S. A small gap means the training loss is an honest estimate of the test loss, not that the model is good.

3. Write

\mathcal E_n=\underbrace{R(\hat\theta_n)-\hat R_n(\hat\theta_n)}_{\mathrm{gap}_n} +\underbrace{\hat R_n(\hat\theta_n)-\hat R_n(\theta^*)}_{\le 0\ \text{(ERM)}} +\underbrace{\hat R_n(\theta^*)-R(\theta^*)}_{\text{mean }0}.

The middle term is non-positive because \hat\theta_n minimizes \hat R_n. The last has mean zero because \theta^* does not depend on S. Taking expectations gives \mathbb E\,\mathcal E_n\le\mathbb E\,\mathrm{gap}_n. Bounding the first and third terms by \sup_\theta|R-\hat R_n| and dropping the middle one gives \mathcal E_n\le2\sup_\theta|R(\theta)-\hat R_n(\theta)|. This is the route every capacity bound takes (§2.C): control the worst gap over the whole class, and the excess risk follows.

4. Because \hat\theta_n is independent of the held-out data, the held-out mean loss is unbiased for R(\hat\theta_n), with standard error \mathrm{sd}(\ell)/\sqrt m. Subtracting the training loss estimates the gap. The excess risk also needs R(\theta^*), the best achievable loss in the class, which a held-out loss alone does not identify without further assumptions or knowledge. You can measure differences of excess risk between two fitted predictors in the same model class, since R(\theta^*) cancels. Different classes can have different optimum risks. More generally, a held-out set estimates the population loss of whatever predictor you evaluate on it, whether one network or an ensemble.

A sanity example with both quantities nonzero: for constant predictors on y\sim\mathcal N(\mu,\sigma^2), \hat\theta_n=\bar y, \mathbb E\,\mathcal E_n=\sigma^2/n and \mathbb E\,\mathrm{gap}_n=2\sigma^2/n.

1.B. Ordinary least squares, exactly

The question. How large are the gap and the excess risk in the simplest model where everything can be computed? Least squares is that model, and it is the reference case for most of this workbook: it gives the “parameter counting” answer, about \sigma^2/n of excess risk per parameter, and shows where it breaks down as p approaches n.

Setup. The linear model is y=x^\top\beta+\varepsilon, with x,\beta\in\mathbb R^p and noise \varepsilon of mean 0 and variance \sigma^2, independent of x. Stack the n training inputs as the rows of the design matrix X\in\mathbb R^{n\times p} and the targets as y\in\mathbb R^n, so y=X\beta+\varepsilon with \varepsilon\in\mathbb R^n. Here take \varepsilon\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma^2I_n), n>p, and X of full column rank (its columns are linearly independent). Ordinary least squares (OLS) is ERM with the squared loss, \hat R_n(b)=\frac1n\|y-Xb\|^2. For the population, take a fresh x\sim\mathcal N(0,I_p) with y=x^\top\beta+\varepsilon.

A tool used throughout: the SVD. Any X has a thin singular value decomposition X=U\,\mathrm{diag}(s_1,\dots,s_r)\,V^\top, where r is the rank, the columns u_k\in\mathbb R^n of U and v_k\in\mathbb R^p of V are orthonormal, and the singular values s_k>0. Then X^\top X=\sum_ks_k^2v_kv_k^\top: the v_k are the principal directions of the inputs, and s_k^2 measures how much the data vary along v_k (how strongly the data “see” that direction). Directions with small s_k are poorly determined by the data. Most results in 1.B–1.D are statements about what happens direction by direction in this basis.

Source: least squares and the optimism of the training error are in The Elements of Statistical Learning (Hastie, Tibshirani and Friedman), §3.2 and §7.4.

1.B.1. Mean, covariance and excess risk at a fixed design
  1. Derive \hat\beta=(X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top y, and show \mathbb E\hat\beta=\beta and \mathrm{Cov}\,\hat\beta=\sigma^2(X^\top X)^{-1}. Here expectations are over the noise, with X fixed.
  2. Show that R(b)=\sigma^2+\|b-\beta\|^2. Hence \theta^*=\beta, \mathcal E_n=\|\hat\beta-\beta\|^2, and \mathbb E[\mathcal E_n\mid X]=\sigma^2\,\mathrm{Tr}\,(X^\top X)^{-1}.
  3. Show that the expected training risk is \mathbb E[\hat R_n(\hat\beta)\mid X]=\sigma^2(n-p)/n.
Hint

For 3, the residual is (I-P_X)\varepsilon, where P_X=X(X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top is a rank-p orthogonal projection.

Solution

1. \nabla_b\hat R_n=-\frac2nX^\top(y-Xb)=0 gives the normal equations X^\top Xb=X^\top y. Since X^\top X is invertible, \hat\beta=(X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top y. Substituting y=X\beta+\varepsilon,

\hat\beta=\beta+(X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top\varepsilon,

so \mathbb E\hat\beta=\beta, and

\mathrm{Cov}\,\hat\beta=(X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top(\sigma^2I)X(X^\top X)^{-1}=\sigma^2(X^\top X)^{-1}.

2. R(b)=\mathbb E(x^\top\beta+\varepsilon-x^\top b)^2=\sigma^2+(\beta-b)^\top\mathbb E[xx^\top]\,(\beta-b)=\sigma^2+\|b-\beta\|^2, since \varepsilon is independent of x and \mathbb E xx^\top=I. So \theta^*=\beta, R(\theta^*)=\sigma^2 and \mathcal E_n=\|\hat\beta-\beta\|^2. Then

\mathbb E\|\hat\beta-\beta\|^2=\mathrm{Tr}\,\mathrm{Cov}\,\hat\beta=\sigma^2\,\mathrm{Tr}\,(X^\top X)^{-1}.

3. The residual is y-X\hat\beta=(I-P_X)y=(I-P_X)\varepsilon, because (I-P_X)X=0. So \mathbb E\|y-X\hat\beta\|^2=\sigma^2\,\mathrm{Tr}(I-P_X)=\sigma^2(n-p), and dividing by n gives \sigma^2(n-p)/n. The fit absorbs the noise along p directions, so the training loss underestimates \sigma^2.

1.B.2. Random design: parameter counting

Now let the rows of X be i.i.d. \mathcal N(0,I_p), so X^\top X is Wishart.

  1. Show that \mathbb E[(X^\top X)^{-1}]=I_p/(n-p-1) for n>p+1. Conclude that \mathbb E\,\mathcal E_n=\sigma^2p/(n-p-1).
  2. Compute the expected gap exactly, and show that for n\gg p the excess risk is \approx\sigma^2p/n while the gap is \approx2\sigma^2p/n. Where does the factor of two come from?
  3. What happens as p\to n-1? Which eigenvalues of X^\top X are responsible?
Hint

Rotation invariance shows that \mathbb E(X^\top X)^{-1} is a multiple of I. For a diagonal entry, the block-inverse (Schur complement) formula gives [(X^\top X)^{-1}]_{11}=1/\big(x_{\cdot1}^\top(I-P_{-1})x_{\cdot1}\big). Here x_{\cdot1}\in\mathbb R^n is the first column of X and P_{-1} projects onto the span of the other p-1 columns. What is the law of the denominator? You may use \mathbb E[1/\chi^2_k]=1/(k-2).

Solution

1. Proportional to I. For any orthogonal O\in\mathbb R^{p\times p}, XO has the same law as X, and ((XO)^\top XO)^{-1}=O^\top(X^\top X)^{-1}O. So M=\mathbb E(X^\top X)^{-1} satisfies M=O^\top MO for all O, which forces M=cI.

The constant. By the Schur complement, [(X^\top X)^{-1}]_{11}=1/\big(x_{\cdot1}^\top(I-P_{-1})x_{\cdot1}\big). Conditional on the other columns, I-P_{-1} is an orthogonal projection of rank n-p+1, and it is independent of x_{\cdot1}\sim\mathcal N(0,I_n). So the denominator is \chi^2_{n-p+1}. Next, \mathbb E[1/\chi^2_k]=\frac{\Gamma(k/2-1)}{2\Gamma(k/2)}=\frac1{k-2} for k>2, which gives c=1/(n-p-1). Combining with 1.B.1.2,

\mathbb E\,\mathcal E_n=\sigma^2\,\mathrm{Tr}\,\mathbb E(X^\top X)^{-1}=\frac{\sigma^2p}{n-p-1}.

2. \mathbb E R(\hat\beta)=\sigma^2+\sigma^2p/(n-p-1)=\sigma^2\frac{n-1}{n-p-1}, and by 1.B.1.3, \mathbb E\hat R_n(\hat\beta)=\sigma^2\frac{n-p}{n}. So

\mathbb E\,\mathrm{gap}_n=\sigma^2\Big[\frac{n-1}{n-p-1}-\frac{n-p}{n}\Big] \approx\sigma^2\Big[\Big(1+\frac pn\Big)-\Big(1-\frac pn\Big)\Big]=\frac{2\sigma^2p}{n}.

The test loss overestimates \sigma^2 by about \sigma^2p/n (the excess risk), and the training loss underestimates it by the same amount, because the fit has absorbed the noise along p directions. The gap counts both. This is the factor of two between “optimism” and “excess risk” that 4.A.2 derives in general (TIC vs \tfrac1{2n}\mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C)).

3. Each of the p coefficients is estimated with variance \approx\sigma^2/n, which is “parameter counting”. The exact correction n/(n-p-1) diverges as p\to n-1. The cause is the smallest eigenvalues of X^\top X/n. When p/n\to\gamma<1, its spectrum fills [(1-\sqrt\gamma)^2,(1+\sqrt\gamma)^2] (Marchenko–Pastur, quoted), and \mathrm{Tr}(X^\top X)^{-1}=\frac1n\sum_i 1/\lambda_i(X^\top X/n) is dominated by the eigenvalues near the lower edge, which tends to 0 as \gamma\to1. Noise along nearly degenerate directions of the design is amplified without limit. §3 (3.A) picks this up.

(All of these formulas are checked by Monte Carlo in checks/wb2a_check.py.)

When someone says deep nets “generalize mysteriously”, ask which quantity they mean. Zero training loss with a small test loss is a statement about the gap (and the test loss). A statement that more parameters do not hurt is about the excess risk, or about the test loss as a function of p.

1.C. Bias–variance

The question. Why can a model that fits its training data worse predict better? The classical answer: a learned predictor errs on new data for three separate reasons. The target is noisy (noise). The learning rule may be systematically off, on average over training sets (bias). And the prediction changes from one training set to another (variance). Flexible models have low bias and high variance; constrained models the reverse. Regularization (1.D) deliberately buys a little bias to remove a lot of variance.

What you get. 1.C.1 derives the exact decomposition for squared loss, shows that the same algebra compares a single posterior draw with the posterior average, and shows that it fails for 0–1 loss. 1.C.2 does ridge regression direction by direction: which directions carry the variance, why some ridge always beats none, and how to count “effective parameters”.

Source: ESL (The Elements of Statistical Learning, Hastie, Tibshirani and Friedman) §7.3 (the decomposition) and §3.4.1 (ridge in the singular-value basis, and its effective degrees of freedom).

1.C.1. Decompose, then decompose a posterior

Let y=f(x)+\varepsilon, where f is the true regression function, \mathbb E[\varepsilon\mid x]=0 and \mathrm{Var}[\varepsilon\mid x]=\sigma^2(x). A predictor \hat f_S is trained on a random sample S, independent of the test point. Fix a test input x; expectations are over S and the test noise.

  1. Show that \mathbb E_{S,\varepsilon}\big(\hat f_S(x)-y\big)^2=\sigma^2(x)+\big(\mathbb E_S\hat f_S(x)-f(x)\big)^2+\mathrm{Var}_S\hat f_S(x).
  2. Average K predictors trained on independent samples. Which terms change, and by how much?
  3. The same algebra with a different source of randomness. Let q be any distribution over parameters, for instance a posterior given S, with predictive mean \bar f(x)=\mathbb E_{\theta\sim q}f_\theta(x). Show that for squared loss \mathbb E_{\theta\sim q}\big(f_\theta(x)-y\big)^2=\big(\bar f(x)-y\big)^2+\mathrm{Var}_{\theta\sim q}f_\theta(x). Translate this into a statement comparing a single posterior draw with the posterior-predictive mean. (4.A.6 calls their errors the Gibbs and Bayes errors.)
  4. Does 1 hold for 0–1 loss? Give an example where more variance lowers the expected 0–1 error.
Solution

1. Write \hat f_S(x)-y=(\hat f_S-\mathbb E\hat f_S)+(\mathbb E\hat f_S-f)+(f-y) and square. The cross terms vanish in expectation. (\hat f_S-\mathbb E\hat f_S) has mean zero over S. (f-y)=-\varepsilon has mean zero and is independent of S. (\mathbb E\hat f_S-f) is a constant. What remains is variance, plus squared bias, plus \sigma^2(x).

2. The average of K independent copies has the same mean, hence the same bias, and variance \mathrm{Var}_S\hat f_S(x)/K. The noise term is untouched. (Real bagging resamples a single dataset, so the copies are correlated and the reduction is smaller.)

3. Expand f_\theta-y=(f_\theta-\bar f)+(\bar f-y) and take \mathbb E_{\theta\sim q} with x,y fixed. The cross term is 2(\bar f-y)\,\mathbb E_q[f_\theta-\bar f]=0. With q a posterior, the left side is the Gibbs loss at (x,y) and (\bar f-y)^2 is the Bayes-ensemble loss. Averaging over (x,y):

\text{Gibbs error}=\text{Bayes-ensemble error}+\mathbb E_x\,\mathrm{Var}_{\text{post}}f_\theta(x)\ \ge\ \text{Bayes-ensemble error},

with equality iff the posterior predictors agree for almost every input under the test distribution. For squared loss, ensembling never hurts a posterior draw. 4.A.6 finds that in the regular case the excess parts differ by a factor of two (p/n vs p/2n).

4. No: the 0–1 loss does not decompose additively. Take y=1 deterministically and predict \mathbf 1[\hat g_S(x)>0], where \hat g_S(x)\sim\mathcal N(\mu,v) with \mu<0, so the average prediction is on the wrong side. The error is \Pr[\hat g_S\le0]=\Phi(-\mu/\sqrt v), which decreases toward \tfrac12 as v grows. When the bias has the wrong sign, variance helps. “Variance always hurts” is a fact about squared loss.

1.C.2. Ridge in the eigenbasis: bias and variance per direction

Take the linear model y=X\beta+\varepsilon with a fixed n\times p design X and noise \varepsilon\sim(0,\sigma^2I_n), \sigma^2>0. For the comparisons with OLS below assume X has full column rank; the positive-penalty formulas also apply to rank-deficient designs. Ridge regression adds a squared-norm penalty to least squares:

\hat\beta_\lambda=\arg\min_b\ \tfrac1n\|y-Xb\|^2+\lambda\|b\|^2=(X^\top X+n\lambda I)^{-1}X^\top y .

Write X^\top X=V\,\mathrm{diag}(s_1^2,\dots,s_p^2)\,V^\top, where the s_k are the singular values of X and the v_k (columns of V) the principal directions of the inputs, and write \beta_k=v_k^\top\beta for the true coefficient along v_k. Write a=n\lambda for short. We track the parameter error \mathbb E\|\hat\beta_\lambda-\beta\|^2, which equals the excess population risk when test inputs are x\sim\mathcal N(0,I) (1.B.1.2).

  1. Show that, in coordinates v_k^\top\hat\beta_\lambda, \mathrm{bias}_k=-\frac{a}{s_k^2+a}\,\beta_k,\qquad \mathrm{var}_k=\sigma^2\frac{s_k^2}{(s_k^2+a)^2}, and that different coordinates are uncorrelated. Hence \mathbb E\|\hat\beta_\lambda-\beta\|^2=\sum_k\dfrac{a^2\beta_k^2+\sigma^2s_k^2}{(s_k^2+a)^2}.
  2. Which directions carry most of the variance at \lambda=0? Show that the derivative of the total error with respect to a at a=0 is strictly negative, so some ridge always beats OLS.
  3. Allow a separate penalty a_k in each direction. Show that the optimum is a_k=\sigma^2/\beta_k^2, independent of s_k, and that the minimal error in that direction is \big(s_k^2/\sigma^2+1/\beta_k^2\big)^{-1}. If \beta\sim\mathcal N(0,\tau^2I), show that the best common penalty is a=\sigma^2/\tau^2.
  4. Effective degrees of freedom. The fitted values are \hat y=S_\lambda y with S_\lambda=X(X^\top X+aI)^{-1}X^\top. Show that \mathrm{Tr}\,S_\lambda=\mathrm{df}(\lambda)=\sum_k s_k^2/(s_k^2+a). Then show that the in-sample optimism, \mathbb E\frac1n\|y'-\hat y\|^2-\mathbb E\frac1n\|y-\hat y\|^2 for fresh noise y'=X\beta+\varepsilon' at the same inputs, equals 2\sigma^2\,\mathrm{df}(\lambda)/n. Compare with 1.B.2.
Hint

For 1, \mathbb E\hat\beta_\lambda=(X^\top X+aI)^{-1}X^\top X\beta, and the covariance is \sigma^2(X^\top X+aI)^{-1}X^\top X(X^\top X+aI)^{-1}. All of these matrices are diagonal in V. For 3, differentiate f(a)=(a^2\beta^2+\sigma^2s^2)/(s^2+a)^2; the numerator of f' factors. For 4, expand both squared norms around X\beta. The only surviving difference is \mathbb E\langle\varepsilon,S_\lambda\varepsilon\rangle.

Solution

1. \mathbb E\hat\beta_\lambda=(X^\top X+aI)^{-1}X^\top X\beta, so in the V basis coordinate k is \frac{s_k^2}{s_k^2+a}\beta_k and the bias is \big(\frac{s_k^2}{s_k^2+a}-1\big)\beta_k=-\frac{a}{s_k^2+a}\beta_k. The covariance is \sigma^2(X^\top X+aI)^{-1}X^\top X(X^\top X+aI)^{-1}=V\,\mathrm{diag}\!\big(\sigma^2s_k^2/(s_k^2+a)^2\big)V^\top, which is diagonal in V, so the coordinates are uncorrelated. Summing squared bias and variance over k gives the total.

2. At a=0, \mathrm{var}_k=\sigma^2/s_k^2, so the weakest directions (small s_k) dominate. That is the same \mathrm{Tr}(X^\top X)^{-1} as in 1.B.1.2. Write f_k(a)=\frac{a^2\beta_k^2+\sigma^2s_k^2}{(s_k^2+a)^2}. Then

f_k'(a)=\frac{2a\beta_k^2(s_k^2+a)-2(a^2\beta_k^2+\sigma^2s_k^2)}{(s_k^2+a)^3}=\frac{2s_k^2(a\beta_k^2-\sigma^2)}{(s_k^2+a)^3}.

At a=0 this is -2\sigma^2/s_k^4<0 for every k, so a small amount of ridge lowers the total error, for every \beta. This is the observation of Hoerl and Kennard (1970).

3. By the factored derivative, f_k decreases for a<\sigma^2/\beta_k^2 and increases after, so a_k^*=\sigma^2/\beta_k^2, which does not involve s_k. Substituting,

f_k(a_k^*)=\frac{\sigma^2(\sigma^2+s_k^2\beta_k^2)/\beta_k^2}{(s_k^2\beta_k^2+\sigma^2)^2/\beta_k^4}=\frac{\sigma^2\beta_k^2}{s_k^2\beta_k^2+\sigma^2}=\Big(\frac{s_k^2}{\sigma^2}+\frac1{\beta_k^2}\Big)^{-1}.

If \beta_k=0, the optimum is the limit a_k\to\infty, with zero error. This has the form of a posterior variance: data precision plus prior precision. If \beta\sim\mathcal N(0,\tau^2I), then \mathbb E\beta_k^2=\tau^2, and \mathbb E_\beta f_k(a)=\frac{a^2\tau^2+\sigma^2s_k^2}{(s_k^2+a)^2} is minimized at a=\sigma^2/\tau^2 for every k at once. So the best common penalty is n\lambda=\sigma^2/\tau^2. 1.D.1 gets the same value as the MAP prior, and 3.C.1.2 as the optimal \lambda^*=\sigma^2\gamma/r^2 for random designs, where \gamma=p/n, r^2=\|\beta\|^2 and \tau^2=r^2/p.

4. With the thin SVD X=U\,\mathrm{diag}(s_k)V^\top, S_\lambda=U\,\mathrm{diag}\big(s_k^2/(s_k^2+a)\big)U^\top, so \mathrm{Tr}\,S_\lambda=\sum_k s_k^2/(s_k^2+a)\in[0,p]. Let \mu=X\beta. Then

\|y'-\hat y\|^2=\|\varepsilon'\|^2+\|\mu-\hat y\|^2+2\langle\varepsilon',\mu-\hat y\rangle,\qquad \|y-\hat y\|^2=\|\varepsilon\|^2+\|\mu-\hat y\|^2+2\langle\varepsilon,\mu-\hat y\rangle .

The first cross term has mean 0 because \varepsilon' is independent of \hat y. In the second, \mu-\hat y=(I-S_\lambda)\mu-S_\lambda\varepsilon, so \mathbb E\langle\varepsilon,\mu-\hat y\rangle=-\sigma^2\,\mathrm{Tr}\,S_\lambda. The difference is 2\sigma^2\,\mathrm{Tr}\,S_\lambda, and dividing by n gives 2\sigma^2\mathrm{df}(\lambda)/n. At \lambda=0, \mathrm{df}=p, which has the same leading 2\sigma^2p/n value as 1.B.2. Here it is exact for new labels at the training inputs; 1.B.2 evaluates fresh random inputs, so its finite-sample gap differs. Ridge replaces “count the parameters” with “count the directions the data can resolve against the penalty.”

The textbook U-shaped curve, where test error falls with model size and then rises, is 1.C.2 with the penalty replaced by the number of features. §3 shows the U is not the whole story even in least squares.

1.D. Regularization

Every network that is trained in practice is regularized, usually by several things at once: weight decay, data augmentation, dropout, early stopping, label smoothing, and choices nobody designed as regularizers, such as the optimizer and the architecture. This subsection asks what each of these does to the learned function, and which of them are the same thing in different clothes. It first surveys the techniques, with pointers to the standard textbook chapter, and then derives the main mechanisms exactly in linear regression. There each technique turns into a precise statement about which directions of the data get shrunk, and by how much.

What counts as regularization. Goodfellow, Bengio and Courville (Deep Learning, 2016, §5.2.2) define it as “any modification we make to a learning algorithm that is intended to reduce its generalization error but not its training error”. Their chapter 7 opens with the same idea: strategies “designed to reduce the test error, possibly at the expense of increased training error”. The definition is about purpose, not form. Penalties on the weights count, but so do changes to the data, the optimizer, the architecture, and averaging several models.

Why it is needed. There are three reasons, from mild to severe.

  • Variance. An estimate fitted to n noisy examples is itself noisy, and the noise is worst in the directions the data barely pin down. For least squares at a fixed design, the parameter error is \mathbb E\|\hat\beta_{\mathrm{OLS}}-\beta\|^2=\sigma^2\mathrm{Tr}(X^\top X)^{-1}=\sigma^2\sum_k s_k^{-2}, where the s_k are the singular values of the design (defined below), so the weakest directions dominate. Shrinking those directions adds a little bias and removes a lot of variance. For ridge, a small enough penalty lowers the error for every true \beta (Hoerl and Kennard, 1970).
  • Ill-posedness. If p>n, or more generally if X is rank deficient, the training loss has a whole affine subspace of minimizers. The data cannot choose among them, so something else has to. This was Tikhonov’s motivation for ill-posed inverse problems, which is why ridge is also called Tikhonov regularization.
  • Deep learning lives at p\gtrsim n. Networks routinely have more parameters than training examples and can fit random labels. Which of the many zero-training-loss solutions you get is decided entirely by the regularizers, explicit and implicit.

The toolbox. The table lists the techniques in common use, with one line on how each works. “GBC” is Goodfellow, Bengio and Courville’s chapter 7, Regularization for Deep Learning, free online; the section links jump to the right page. The last column says where this subsection derives the mechanism in a linear model.

Technique What it does GBC Here
L^2 penalty (“weight decay”) Add \lambda\lVert\theta\rVert_2^2 to the loss. Shrinks every weight toward 0, most strongly in directions the data constrain weakly. §7.1.1 1.D.1, 1.D.2, 1.D.4
L^1 penalty Add \lambda\lVert\theta\rVert_1. Sets many weights exactly to 0, so it selects features as well as shrinking them. §7.1.2 1.D.3
Norm constraints Keep \lVert w\rVert\le c (often per unit, “max-norm”) by projecting after each step: the hard form of a penalty. §7.2 1.D.2
Choosing among many minimizers When the problem is underdetermined, pick one solution, e.g. the minimum-norm one X^+y, which is the \lambda\to0 limit of ridge. §7.3 1.D.4
Data augmentation Train on transformed copies (flips, crops, shifts) whose label should not change. Builds in an invariance. §7.4 1.D.5
Noise injection Add noise to inputs, hidden units or weights during training. For a linear model, input noise is exactly an L^2 penalty; for a nonlinear one, it acts like a smoothness penalty only approximately. §7.5 1.D.5
Label smoothing With K classes, train toward soft targets \epsilon/(K-1) and 1-\epsilon instead of 0 and 1, so the loss stops rewarding ever-larger logits. §7.5.1
Semi-supervised and multitask learning Share a representation with unlabelled data or other tasks, whose data then constrain the shared parameters. §7.6–7.7
Early stopping Stop when validation loss stops improving. For a quadratic loss, roughly equivalent to L^2. §7.8 1.D.4
Parameter tying and sharing Force groups of weights to be equal (a convolution uses the same filter at every position), which builds in a symmetry. §7.9
Sparse representations Penalize the activations rather than the weights, e.g. L^1 on hidden units. §7.10
Bagging and ensembles Train several models (on resampled data, or from different seeds) and average them. Averaging cuts variance. §7.11
Dropout Zero random units during training. A cheap approximation to bagging many subnetworks; in a linear model, L^2 in a data-dependent metric. §7.12 1.D.6
Adversarial training Train on worst-case perturbations of each input within a small ball, which makes f locally flat in input space. §7.13
Tangent propagation Penalize the derivative of f along directions of known invariance: the infinitesimal version of augmentation. §7.14
Decoupled weight decay (AdamW) With plain SGD, an L^2 penalty and shrinking the weights by a fixed factor each step are the same. With Adam they are not, because Adam divides the penalty’s gradient by a per-coordinate scale. AdamW applies the decay directly. Loshchilov and Hutter (2019)
Implicit regularizers Not designed as regularizers, but they act like them: which minimizer gradient descent finds, minibatch noise (GBC §8.1.3), batch normalization (GBC §8.7.1; Ioffe and Szegedy (2015) report it can replace dropout), and the architecture itself. 1.D.4

What this subsection does. It works in linear regression, where every claim can be checked exactly.

  • 1.D.1 Ridge as a Gaussian prior: the MAP estimate and the posterior, and why “penalty = log prior” depends on the parametrization.
  • 1.D.2 Ridge as a norm constraint, with the Lagrange multiplier as the exchange rate.
  • 1.D.3 L^1 and sparsity: soft thresholding, and why exact zeros appear.
  • 1.D.4 Ridge as a spectral filter on the data’s singular directions; early stopping as a similar, but not identical, filter; gradient descent’s choice of minimizer.
  • 1.D.5 Input noise and augmentation: for a linear model input noise is exactly ridge; for a flexible model the input-gradient penalty matches the noisy objective to leading order but not its minimizer; augmentation by a symmetry penalizes the non-invariant part of the weights.
  • 1.D.6 Dropout (optional): in a linear model it is ridge in a data-dependent metric.
  • 1.D.7 Flatness: when the sharpness of a minimum can matter for generalization, and why any sharpness claim needs a metric.
  • 1.D.8 A reading: Dinh et al., “Sharp minima can generalize”.

Setup used throughout 1.D. There are n training examples with inputs x_i\in\mathbb R^p and targets y_i\in\mathbb R. Stack the inputs as the rows of the design matrix X\in\mathbb R^{n\times p} and the targets as y\in\mathbb R^n. The data follow the linear model y=X\beta+\varepsilon, where \beta\in\mathbb R^p is the true coefficient vector and the noise \varepsilon has mean 0 and covariance \sigma^2I_n. The design is treated as fixed unless a part says otherwise. Ridge regression with penalty \lambda>0 is

\hat\beta_\lambda=\arg\min_b\ \frac1n\|y-Xb\|^2+\lambda\|b\|^2=(X^\top X+n\lambda I)^{-1}X^\top y ,

and \lambda=0 (with X of full column rank) is ordinary least squares (OLS). Write the thin singular value decomposition as X=U\,\mathrm{diag}(s_k)\,V^\top, with singular values s_k\ge0, left singular vectors u_k\in\mathbb R^n (columns of U) and right singular vectors v_k\in\mathbb R^p (columns of V). Then X^\top X=V\,\mathrm{diag}(s_k^2)\,V^\top, and a direction v_k with small s_k is one the data barely constrain. When x\sim\mathcal N(0,I_p), the parameter error \mathbb E\|\hat\beta-\beta\|^2 equals the excess test risk, which is why the exercises track it.

For reference (books): Deep Learning, Goodfellow, Bengio and Courville (2016; free online), ch. 7, intro and §7.1, §7.8, §7.12; The Elements of Statistical Learning, Hastie, Tibshirani and Friedman (2nd ed. 2009; free PDF), §3.4.1–3.4.3.

1.D.1. Ridge as a prior: MAP, posterior, and a warning

Why. “Weight decay is a Gaussian prior” is the most common justification of the most common regularizer. This exercise checks the claim exactly in linear regression and finds where it stops being a statement about the learned function.

Setup. The linear model y=X\beta+\varepsilon with a fixed design X\in\mathbb R^{n\times p} and noise \varepsilon\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma^2I_n); ridge regression is \hat\beta_\lambda=(X^\top X+n\lambda I)^{-1}X^\top y, the minimizer of \frac1n\|y-Xb\|^2+\lambda\|b\|^2. MAP (maximum a posteriori) is the mode of the posterior density.

Source: ESL §3.4.1 notes that ridge is the posterior mode and mean under a Gaussian prior.

  1. Take the prior \beta\sim\mathcal N(0,\tau^2I) and the likelihood y\mid\beta\sim\mathcal N(X\beta,\sigma^2I). Show that the MAP estimate is ridge with \lambda=\sigma^2/(n\tau^2). Show that the posterior is \mathcal N\big(\hat\beta_\lambda,\ \sigma^2(X^\top X+n\lambda I)^{-1}\big), so the posterior mean equals the MAP here.
  2. Compare the posterior covariance with the sampling covariance of \hat\beta_\lambda over the noise at fixed \beta, which is \sigma^2(X^\top X+n\lambda I)^{-1}X^\top X(X^\top X+n\lambda I)^{-1} (1.C.2). Which is larger, and why do they differ?
  3. MAP depends on the parametrization. Suppose a posterior on \beta>0 has density \pi(\beta)\propto\beta e^{-\beta}. Find its mode. Now write \beta=e^u, find the mode of the density of u, and map it back. Which of the mode, mean and median is invariant under monotone reparametrization? What does this mean for “weight decay is a prior”?
Solution

1. The negative log posterior is \frac1{2\sigma^2}\|y-X\beta\|^2+\frac1{2\tau^2}\|\beta\|^2+\text{const}. Multiplying by 2\sigma^2/n gives \frac1n\|y-X\beta\|^2+\frac{\sigma^2}{n\tau^2}\|\beta\|^2, the ridge objective with \lambda=\sigma^2/(n\tau^2). The log posterior is quadratic in \beta, so the posterior is Gaussian. Its precision is X^\top X/\sigma^2+I/\tau^2=(X^\top X+n\lambda I)/\sigma^2, and its mean (which is also the mode) is \hat\beta_\lambda.

2. Write G=X^\top X and a=n\lambda. The posterior covariance is \sigma^2(G+aI)^{-1} and the sampling covariance is \sigma^2G(G+aI)^{-2}. Their difference is \sigma^2a(G+aI)^{-2}\succeq0, so the posterior is wider. The sampling covariance ignores the bias. The posterior covariance is the bias–variance mean squared error averaged over the prior: along a singular direction v_k of X with singular value s (and \mathbb E\beta_k^2=\tau^2 under the prior), using 1.C.2.1 at a=\sigma^2/\tau^2,

\frac{a^2\tau^2+\sigma^2s^2}{(s^2+a)^2}=\frac{\sigma^2(a+s^2)}{(s^2+a)^2}=\frac{\sigma^2}{s^2+a}.

3. \frac{d}{d\beta}\log(\beta e^{-\beta})=1/\beta-1=0, so the mode is \beta=1. The density of u=\log\beta is \pi(e^u)e^u\propto e^{2u-e^u}, and \frac{d}{du}(2u-e^u)=0 gives e^u=2, i.e. \beta=2. Same posterior, different “MAP”. The mean is not invariant under nonlinear maps either. The median is invariant under monotone maps.

So the identification “penalty = negative log prior” holds in a chosen parametrization. Weight decay is a Gaussian prior on the weights, which in a network induces some prior on functions that depends on the architecture. The MAP estimate under it is not an invariant summary of the posterior.

1.D.2. Ridge as a constraint: the Lagrange multiplier

Why. The second reading of a penalty: minimize the training loss over a ball \|b\|^2\le c of allowed parameters. Small balls contain few functions, which is the “capacity” explanation of why regularized models generalize. This exercise shows the penalty and the ball are the same estimator, and asks what the capacity explanation then predicts.

Take the linear model of 1.D, with design X\in\mathbb R^{n\times p} and targets y\in\mathbb R^n, and ridge \hat\beta_\lambda=(X^\top X+n\lambda I)^{-1}X^\top y. X^+ is the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse, so X^+y is the least-squares solution of smallest norm.

  1. For c>0, show that a minimizer of \hat R_n(b)=\frac1n\|y-Xb\|^2 subject to \|b\|^2\le c is \hat\beta_\lambda, where \lambda is the KKT (Lagrange) multiplier. Interpret \hat\beta_0=X^+y when the design is rank deficient.
  2. Show that c(\lambda)=\|\hat\beta_\lambda\|^2 is strictly decreasing when X^\top y\neq0. Hence \lambda(c) is well defined for c<\|X^+y\|^2, and \lambda=0 for c\ge\|X^+y\|^2.
  3. In this view, what explains why the constrained estimator generalizes? Which objection from §2 (the random-label experiments of 2.D) applies to that explanation?
Hint

For 1, write the Lagrangian \hat R_n(b)+\lambda(\|b\|^2-c) with \lambda\ge0, set its gradient to zero, and use complementary slackness. For 2, write \|\hat\beta_\lambda\|^2 in the SVD of X and look at each term as a function of \lambda. For 3, recall 1.A.1.3 and §2.C; the Rademacher complexity of a norm ball of linear predictors is in UML §26.2.

Solution

1. The problem is convex, and Slater’s condition holds for c>0. The Lagrangian is \hat R_n(b)+\lambda(\|b\|^2-c) with \lambda\ge0. Stationarity gives -\frac2nX^\top(y-Xb)+2\lambda b=0, i.e. b=(X^\top X+n\lambda I)^{-1}X^\top y=\hat\beta_\lambda for \lambda>0. For \lambda=0, use the minimum-norm least-squares solution X^+y; other feasible least-squares solutions may also minimize when X is rank deficient. Complementary slackness says either \lambda=0 (the constraint need not be strictly slack) or \|\hat\beta_\lambda\|^2=c.

2. In the SVD, \|\hat\beta_\lambda\|^2=\sum_k\frac{s_k^2(u_k^\top y)^2}{(s_k^2+n\lambda)^2}. Each term is non-increasing in \lambda, and at least one is strictly decreasing when X^\top y\ne0. As \lambda\downarrow0 the norm rises to \|X^+y\|^2, the minimum-norm least-squares solution, and as \lambda\to\infty it falls to 0. So the map from c to \lambda is a decreasing bijection on (0,\|X^+y\|^2). Tighter constraints need bigger multipliers.

3. The explanation is capacity. The norm ball \{x\mapsto x^\top b:\|b\|\le B\} has Rademacher complexity of order B\sqrt{\mathbb E\|x\|^2/n} (quoted), which controls prediction complexity. To turn this into a squared-loss gap bound, additionally control the input norms and labels (or suitable tails), so the loss is Lipschitz on the allowed prediction range. Small enough complexity then gives an excess-risk bound via 1.A.1.3. This inherits two problems. The norm depends on the parametrization (1.D.1.3, 1.D.7), and Zhang et al.’s objection applies: nets with weight decay still fit random labels, so the constraint that is actually active is not small.

1.D.3. L1 and sparsity

Replace the squared norm in the ridge penalty by the sum of absolute values, and the estimator starts setting coefficients exactly to zero: it selects features and shrinks them at once. This is the lasso (Tibshirani, 1996). This group shows why one penalty gives exact zeros and the other never does, and what the usual Bayesian reading of the lasso is worth.

Setup: the linear model y=X\beta+\varepsilon with X\in\mathbb R^{n\times p}, noise variance \sigma^2, and ridge \hat\beta_\lambda=\arg\min_b\frac1n\|y-Xb\|^2+\lambda\|b\|^2, as in the 1.D setup. The lasso is

\hat\beta^{\mathrm{lasso}}=\arg\min_b\ \frac1{2n}\|y-Xb\|^2+\lambda\|b\|_1,\qquad \|b\|_1=\sum_j|b_j| .

The factor \tfrac12 is a convention that makes the threshold below come out as \lambda. In general the lasso has no closed form. Parts 1, 2 and 4 use an orthonormal design, X^\top X=nI_p (which needs n\ge p), where it does. Then the OLS estimate is z=X^\top y/n.

  1. With X^\top X=nI, show that each estimator acts on each coordinate z_j separately:

    • ridge gives z_j/(1+\lambda);
    • the lasso gives the soft threshold \mathrm{sign}(z_j)\,(|z_j|-\lambda)_+, where (t)_+=\max(t,0);
    • best-subset selection with an L^0 penalty, \arg\min_b\frac1{2n}\|y-Xb\|^2+\frac{\lambda^2}2\|b\|_0 with \|b\|_0 the number of nonzero entries, gives the hard threshold z_j\,\mathbf 1[|z_j|>\lambda].

    Sketch the three as functions of z_j. Which of them is biased for large coefficients, and by how much?

  2. Give the coefficients independent Laplace priors with density \frac1{2\tau}e^{-|\beta_j|/\tau} (scale \tau>0), and take the likelihood y\mid\beta\sim\mathcal N(X\beta,\sigma^2I), for general X. Show that the MAP estimate is the lasso with \lambda=\sigma^2/(n\tau).

  3. For general X, show that \hat b minimizes the lasso objective if and only if, for every j, \tfrac1n\,x_{\cdot j}^\top(y-X\hat b)=\lambda\,\mathrm{sign}(\hat b_j)\ \text{ if }\hat b_j\ne0,\qquad \big|\tfrac1n\,x_{\cdot j}^\top(y-X\hat b)\big|\le\lambda\ \text{ if }\hat b_j=0, where x_{\cdot j} is the j-th column of X. In words: a feature stays out until its correlation with the residual reaches \lambda. Use this to explain why the lasso gives \hat b_j=0 for a whole open set of data y, while ridge with \lambda>0 gives \hat\beta_{\lambda,j}=0 only on a set of probability zero. Draw the constrained version (minimize the loss subject to \|b\|_1\le c) in two dimensions and say where the zeros come from.

  4. A caveat about the Bayesian reading. In the orthonormal case, show that the posterior of part 2 factorizes over coordinates, with \pi(\beta_j\mid y)\propto\exp\!\big(-\frac{n}{2\sigma^2}(\beta_j-z_j)^2-|\beta_j|/\tau\big). Show that the posterior mean of \beta_j has the sign of z_j and is never exactly zero unless z_j=0. Is a draw from the Laplace prior ever sparse? What does this say about “the lasso is Bayesian inference under a sparsity prior”?

Hint

For 1, expand \frac1{2n}\|y-Xb\|^2=\frac1{2n}\|y\|^2-b^\top z+\frac12\|b\|^2, which is \frac12\|b-z\|^2 plus a constant. For the scalar lasso problem \frac12(b-z)^2+\lambda|b|, try b>0 and b<0 separately, and otherwise b=0. For 3, the objective is convex, so \hat b is a minimizer iff 0 lies in its subdifferential; the subdifferential of |b| is \{\mathrm{sign}(b)\} for b\ne0 and the interval [-1,1] at b=0. For 4, compare the posterior density at \beta and at -\beta.

Solution

1. With X^\top X=nI and z=X^\top y/n, \frac1{2n}\|y-Xb\|^2=\frac12\|b-z\|^2+\text{const}, and every penalty here is a sum over coordinates, so each problem splits into scalar problems in (b_j,z_j). Drop the index j.

  • Ridge. \frac1n\|y-Xb\|^2+\lambda\|b\|^2=\|b-z\|^2+\lambda\|b\|^2+\text{const}, minimized at b=z/(1+\lambda). This is also (X^\top X+n\lambda I)^{-1}X^\top y=(nI+n\lambda I)^{-1}nz.
  • Lasso. Minimize h(b)=\frac12(b-z)^2+\lambda|b|. On b>0, h'(b)=b-z+\lambda vanishes at b=z-\lambda, which lies in b>0 iff z>\lambda. On b<0 the stationary point is b=z+\lambda, valid iff z<-\lambda. If |z|\le\lambda, then h'<0 on b<0 and h'>0 on b>0, so the minimum is at the kink, b=0. Together, b=\mathrm{sign}(z)(|z|-\lambda)_+.
  • L^0. A coordinate set to 0 costs \frac12z^2. A nonzero coordinate pays the penalty \frac{\lambda^2}2 whatever its size, so it might as well be b=z, costing \frac{\lambda^2}2. Keep it iff z^2>\lambda^2: b=z\,\mathbf 1[|z|>\lambda]. (Best subset of a fixed size M likewise keeps the M largest |z_j|.)

The sketches: ridge is a line through the origin with slope 1/(1+\lambda). Soft thresholding is 0 on [-\lambda,\lambda] and then runs parallel to the diagonal, shifted toward zero by \lambda. Hard thresholding is 0 on [-\lambda,\lambda] and then jumps onto the diagonal. For large |z|, ridge pulls the estimate toward zero by \frac{\lambda}{1+\lambda}|z|, which grows in proportion to the coefficient; the lasso pulls it in by the constant \lambda; hard thresholding is unbiased for large coefficients but discontinuous at the threshold, so a small change in the data can switch a coefficient between 0 and \pm\lambda. The lasso sits between the other two: continuous like ridge, sparse like subset selection. This is ESL’s Table 3.4.

2. The negative log posterior is \frac1{2\sigma^2}\|y-X\beta\|^2+\frac1\tau\|\beta\|_1+\text{const}. Multiplying by \sigma^2/n gives \frac1{2n}\|y-X\beta\|^2+\frac{\sigma^2}{n\tau}\|\beta\|_1, the lasso objective with \lambda=\sigma^2/(n\tau). As with ridge and the Gaussian prior (1.D.1), “penalty = negative log prior” holds in the parametrization you chose.

3. The objective is convex, so \hat b minimizes it iff 0\in-\frac1nX^\top(y-X\hat b)+\lambda\,\partial\|\hat b\|_1. The subdifferential of \|b\|_1 is the product over j of \{\mathrm{sign}(b_j)\} when b_j\ne0 and [-1,1] when b_j=0. Reading off coordinate j gives exactly the two stated conditions.

Why zeros are robust for the lasso. The condition for \hat b_j=0 is an inequality, which typically holds strictly. In the orthonormal case it reads |z_j|\le\lambda: a whole interval of z_j, which has positive probability under any continuous noise. For general X, the residual y-X\hat b is the same for every lasso solution and depends continuously on y, and a coordinate whose inequality is strict must be zero in every solution (a nonzero \hat b_j would need equality). So if the inequality holds strictly at some y, it still holds after a small change of y, and the same coordinates stay at zero on an open set of y. Ridge is different. \hat\beta_\lambda=(X^\top X+n\lambda I)^{-1}X^\top y is linear in y, so \hat\beta_{\lambda,j}=0 is one linear equation in y: a hyperplane, of probability zero under continuous noise (unless that row of the matrix vanishes). In the orthonormal case, z_j/(1+\lambda)=0 only at z_j=0.

The picture. In the constrained form, the level sets of \|y-Xb\|^2 are ellipses centred at the OLS solution, and the solution is where the smallest ellipse touches the ball \{\|b\|_1\le c\}. In two dimensions that ball is a diamond with corners on the axes. An ellipse growing from a generic centre often first touches a corner, where one coordinate is exactly 0; it does so for a whole region of centres. The L^2 ball is round, so the touching point lies on an axis only if the centre is specially placed. In p dimensions the L^1 ball has corners and low-dimensional faces on every coordinate subspace, which is where sparse solutions live. This is ESL’s Figure 3.11.

4. With X^\top X=nI, \|y-X\beta\|^2=n\|\beta-z\|^2+\text{const}, so the posterior is a product of the stated one-dimensional densities. Take z_j>0 and drop the index. For \beta>0, \frac{\pi(\beta\mid y)}{\pi(-\beta\mid y)}=\exp\!\Big(-\frac{n}{2\sigma^2}\big[(\beta-z)^2-(\beta+z)^2\big]\Big)=\exp\!\Big(\frac{2n\beta z}{\sigma^2}\Big)>1 , so \mathbb E[\beta\mid y]=\int_0^\infty\beta\,\big[\pi(\beta\mid y)-\pi(-\beta\mid y)\big]\,d\beta>0. By symmetry the mean is negative for z<0, and it is zero only at z=0. The same comparison gives \Pr(\beta>0\mid y)>\tfrac12, so the posterior median is not zero either. The posterior mean is the Bayes estimate under squared loss, and it is never sparse; only the mode is.

A draw from the Laplace prior is nonzero with probability one, so the prior does not believe in sparsity at all. Exact zeros come from taking the mode of a density with a kink at 0. The lasso is better understood as a convex surrogate for best-subset selection (part 1) than as Bayesian inference. If you really believe many coefficients are exactly zero, the matching prior puts a point mass at zero (a “spike-and-slab” prior). Its posterior probability that \beta_j=0 is then meaningful, but its posterior mean is still not sparse.

1.D.4. Ridge as a spectral filter, and early stopping

Why. The third reading, and the most useful for deep learning: ridge acts on the data’s principal directions v_k one at a time, keeping the well-determined ones (large singular value s_k) and shrinking the poorly determined ones. Gradient descent stopped early does something similar for free, which is one sense in which “early stopping is regularization”. The exercise compares the two filters exactly and finds the implicit bias of gradient descent when it runs to convergence.

Setup. Design X\in\mathbb R^{n\times p}, targets y\in\mathbb R^n, ridge \hat\beta_\lambda=(X^\top X+n\lambda I)^{-1}X^\top y. The thin SVD is X=\sum_ks_ku_kv_k^\top, with singular values s_k>0 and orthonormal u_k\in\mathbb R^n, v_k\in\mathbb R^p. A filter is a set of factors \phi_k\in[0,1] saying how much of each direction an estimator keeps.

  1. Show that \hat\beta_\lambda=\sum_{k:\,s_k>0}\phi_k\,\dfrac{u_k^\top y}{s_k}\,v_k with \phi_k^{\mathrm{ridge}}=\dfrac{s_k^2}{s_k^2+n\lambda}. The factor (u_k^\top y)/s_k is the unregularized coefficient.
  2. Run gradient flow \dot\beta=-\nabla L(\beta) on L(\beta)=\frac1{2n}\|X\beta-y\|^2, starting from \beta(0)=0. Show that \beta(t) has the same form with \phi_k^{\mathrm{GF}}(t)=1-e^{-s_k^2t/n}. For gradient descent with step \eta and T steps, show \phi_k=1-(1-\eta s_k^2/n)^T.
  3. Show that the two filters cross \tfrac12 at the same s^2 if and only if t=\ln2/\lambda. With that matching, write both in terms of x=s^2/(n\lambda), and compare them for x\ll1 and for x\gg1. Which filter passes more of the weak directions? Which shrinks the strong directions more?
  4. Let p>n. Show that gradient flow from \beta(0)=0 converges to the minimum-norm interpolant X^+y. What is the limit from a nonzero initialization \beta_0?
Hint

Work in the coordinates b_k=v_k^\top\beta, where X^\top X is diagonal and X^\top y=\sum_ks_k(u_k^\top y)v_k; gradient flow then decouples into one scalar linear ODE per k. For 3, set each filter equal to \tfrac12 and solve for s^2. For 4, in which subspace does \dot\beta always lie?

Further reading:

A continuous-time view of early stopping for least squares regression, Alnur Ali, J. Zico Kolter, Ryan J. Tibshirani (AISTATS 2019) Importance ●●○○○ Length ●●●○○

Compares the risks of gradient flow and ridge along the whole path, under the calibration t=1/\lambda.

Solution

1. X^\top y=\sum_k s_k(u_k^\top y)v_k and (X^\top X+n\lambda I)^{-1}v_k=v_k/(s_k^2+n\lambda), so \hat\beta_\lambda=\sum_k\frac{s_k(u_k^\top y)}{s_k^2+n\lambda}v_k=\sum_k\frac{s_k^2}{s_k^2+n\lambda}\cdot\frac{u_k^\top y}{s_k}v_k.

2. \nabla L=\frac1n(X^\top X\beta-X^\top y). In coordinates b_k=v_k^\top\beta,

\dot b_k=-\frac{s_k^2}{n}b_k+\frac{s_k}{n}u_k^\top y,

with fixed point (u_k^\top y)/s_k. From b_k(0)=0 the solution is b_k(t)=(1-e^{-s_k^2t/n})(u_k^\top y)/s_k. For gradient descent, b_k^{(T)}-b_k^\infty=(1-\eta s_k^2/n)^T(b_k^{(0)}-b_k^\infty), which gives the stated filter. Directions with s_k=0 never move. Discrete gradient descent converges on the positive eigendirections when 0<\eta<2n/s_{\max}^2; the filter is non-oscillatory for \eta\le n/s_{\max}^2.

3. Ridge equals \tfrac12 at s^2=n\lambda, and flow equals \tfrac12 at s^2t/n=\ln2, i.e. at s^2=n\ln2/t. These coincide iff t=\ln2/\lambda. With this matching, ridge is x/(1+x) and flow is 1-e^{-x\ln2}=1-2^{-x}.

  • Weak directions, x\ll1: ridge \approx x and flow \approx x\ln2, so ridge passes about 1/\ln2\approx1.44 times more of each weak direction.
  • Strong directions, x\gg1: ridge leaves a shrinkage of 1-\phi\approx1/x, only polynomially small, while flow leaves 2^{-x}, exponentially small. So ridge shrinks the strong directions more.

Gradient flow is the sharper, more step-like filter. The largest disagreements are flow above ridge by about 0.138 (near x\approx4.3) and ridge above flow by about 0.044 (near x\approx0.36). With the naive matching t=1/\lambda, flow becomes 1-e^{-x} and its half-point moves to x=\ln2. “Early stopping is ridge” is right in outline (keep the strong directions, drop the weak ones) and wrong in detail. WB5 §1 derives how the polynomial tail of the ridge filter affects learning-curve exponents.

4. \dot\beta\in\mathrm{row}(X), so \beta(t)\in\mathrm{row}(X) for all t. For p>n with X of full row rank, all s_k>0 for k\le n, so by 2 the flow converges to \sum_{k\le n}\frac{u_k^\top y}{s_k}v_k=X^+y. That is the unique interpolant in the row space, and so the minimum-norm interpolant. From \beta_0, the row-space component converges to the same place and the null-space component never moves: the limit is X^+y+(I-X^+X)\beta_0. The implicit bias is “minimum distance from the initialization”.

▶ Interactive figure: spectral_filters.html. Use panel A only (panel B belongs to WB5). Press “match half-points” and read the difference strip: where do the filters disagree, and which passes more of the weak tail? Then set t = 1/λ by hand and see the ln 2 offset.

1.D.5. Input noise and data augmentation as penalties

Two common regularizers change the data rather than the loss: adding noise to the inputs, and training on transformed copies of each example (mirror images, crops, shifts) whose labels should not change. In a linear model each one becomes an explicit penalty you can write down. Gaussian input noise is exactly ridge. Augmentation by a symmetry penalizes the part of the weights that is not invariant, and when every transformation is used equally often that penalty becomes a hard constraint. For a nonlinear model, Bishop (1995) argued that small input noise is equivalent to adding a penalty on the input gradient, s^2\,\mathbb E\|\nabla_xf\|^2 (a Tikhonov penalty, i.e. a penalty on derivatives of f). Parts 2 and 3 check this: the two objectives agree to order s^2 near the regression function, but their minimizers differ at that same order.

Setup: training pairs (x_i,y_i), i=1,\dots,n, with x_i\in\mathbb R^p, squared loss, and X\in\mathbb R^{n\times p} the matrix with rows x_i^\top. A linear predictor is f(x)=\beta^\top x. Input noise replaces x_i by \tilde x_i=x_i+\xi_i, where the \xi_i are i.i.d. with mean 0 and covariance s^2I_p, independent of the data. We study the training loss averaged over the noise, which is what drawing fresh noise at every step approximates over many epochs.

  1. Show that, exactly, \mathbb E_\xi\,\frac1n\sum_i(y_i-\tilde x_i^\top\beta)^2=\frac1n\|y-X\beta\|^2+s^2\|\beta\|^2 , so the minimizer is ridge with \lambda=s^2. What penalty does noise with covariance \Sigma_\xi give? (The dropout exercise, 1.D.6, multiplies the inputs by noise instead, and gets a penalty in a data-dependent metric.)
  2. Now let f be a smooth nonlinear function of x (its parameters are suppressed), and let \xi\sim\mathcal N(0,s^2I_p). For one example, write r=y-f(x) for the residual, g=\nabla_xf(x) for the input gradient and \Delta f=\mathrm{Tr}\,\nabla_x^2f(x) for the Laplacian. Show that \mathbb E_\xi\big(y-f(x+\xi)\big)^2=r^2+s^2\big(\|g\|^2-r\,\Delta f\big)+O(s^4). Check that part 1 is the linear case. Which of the two s^2 terms is a penalty, and which is not?
  3. Bishop’s argument, and where it stops. Assume enough data that sums over examples can be replaced by expectations over (x,y), and let m(x)=\mathbb E[y\mid x] be the regression function.
    • (a) Show that the averaged second term is -s^2\,\mathbb E_x\big[(m(x)-f(x))\,\Delta f(x)\big]. It vanishes at f=m and is O(s^4) when f-m=O(s^2). Bishop concluded that this term can be dropped: to order s^2, the noisy objective and the Tikhonov objective \mathbb E(y-f(x))^2+s^2\,\mathbb E\|\nabla_xf\|^2 are minimized by the same function.
    • (b) Test this where everything is solvable. Let x be uniform on the circle [0,2\pi), with the noise added modulo 2\pi, let y=m(x)+\varepsilon, and minimize over all smooth periodic f. Show that the noisy objective is minimized by f(t)=\mathbb E[m(x)\mid x+\xi=t], which here is m convolved with a Gaussian of variance s^2: on the Fourier mode e^{ikx} it multiplies by e^{-s^2k^2/2}. Show that the Tikhonov objective \mathbb E(y-f(x))^2+s^2\,\mathbb E f'(x)^2 is minimized by the multiplier 1/(1+s^2k^2). Expand both to order s^2. Do they agree? What did the argument in (a) miss?
  4. Augmentation by a mirror symmetry. Let S be an orthogonal involution, S^\top=S and S^2=I; for example, reversing the order of the pixels in each row of an image (a left–right mirror). Let P=\frac12(I+S) and Q=\frac12(I-S). Augmentation trains on both (x_i,y_i) and (Sx_i,y_i). Show that \frac1{2n}\sum_i\Big[(y_i-\beta^\top x_i)^2+(y_i-\beta^\top Sx_i)^2\Big]=\frac1n\|y-XP\beta\|^2+\frac1n\|XQ\beta\|^2 . Deduce that if XQ is injective on the range of Q (enough data), the minimizer has Q\hat\beta=0, so the learned predictor is exactly mirror-invariant, and \hat\beta is the least-squares fit to the symmetrized inputs Px_i. What does this cost if the true \beta has Q\beta\ne0?
  5. Partial augmentation. Now mirror each example with probability \alpha\in[0,\tfrac12) and keep it otherwise. Work with the population risk, under an input distribution that is mirror-symmetric (Sx has the same distribution as x); the labels need not be. Let \Sigma=\mathbb E[xx^\top], assumed invertible. Show that
    • (a) P\Sigma Q=0;
    • (b) the minimizer of the augmented risk has the same P\beta as the minimizer of the clean risk, and Q\beta multiplied by 1-2\alpha;
    • (c) this is also the minimizer of the clean risk plus \kappa\,\beta^\top Q\Sigma Q\beta with \kappa=2\alpha/(1-2\alpha): ridge on the non-invariant part only, in the data’s own metric. What happens as \alpha\to\tfrac12?
Hint

For 1, write \tilde x_i^\top\beta=x_i^\top\beta+\xi_i^\top\beta and use \mathbb E(a-Z)^2=a^2+\mathrm{Var}\,Z for a mean-zero Z. For 2, Taylor expand f(x+\xi) to third order; odd moments of \xi vanish, and \mathbb E\,\xi^\top A\xi=s^2\mathrm{Tr}A. For 3(b), substitute t=x+\xi: t is again uniform and independent of \xi, and x=t-\xi. For the Tikhonov objective, expand f and m in Fourier series and use Parseval; the problem separates by frequency. For 4 and 5, write \beta=\beta_P+\beta_Q with \beta_P=P\beta, \beta_Q=Q\beta. Then \beta^\top x=\beta_P^\top x+\beta_Q^\top x and \beta^\top Sx=\beta_P^\top x-\beta_Q^\top x, because S\beta_P=\beta_P and S\beta_Q=-\beta_Q.

Solution

1. For one example, y_i-\tilde x_i^\top\beta=(y_i-x_i^\top\beta)-\xi_i^\top\beta, and \xi_i^\top\beta has mean 0 and variance \beta^\top(s^2I)\beta=s^2\|\beta\|^2. So \mathbb E_\xi(y_i-\tilde x_i^\top\beta)^2=(y_i-x_i^\top\beta)^2+s^2\|\beta\|^2, with no approximation, and only the first two moments of the noise matter. Averaging over i gives the claim, which is the ridge objective with \lambda=s^2. Its minimizer is (X^\top X+ns^2I)^{-1}X^\top y. With noise covariance \Sigma_\xi the penalty is \beta^\top\Sigma_\xi\beta, a “generalized ridge”: noise along a direction penalizes weight along that direction.

2. Taylor expand: f(x+\xi)=f+g^\top\xi+\frac12\xi^\top H\xi+\frac16T[\xi,\xi,\xi]+O(|\xi|^4), with H=\nabla_x^2f and T the third-derivative tensor. Then \big(y-f(x+\xi)\big)^2=r^2-2r\,g^\top\xi-r\,\xi^\top H\xi-\tfrac13r\,T[\xi,\xi,\xi]+(g^\top\xi)^2+(g^\top\xi)(\xi^\top H\xi)+O(|\xi|^4). Take expectations. Terms with an odd number of \xi’s vanish. \mathbb E(g^\top\xi)^2=s^2\|g\|^2 and \mathbb E\,\xi^\top H\xi=s^2\mathrm{Tr}H=s^2\Delta f. The remainder has expectation O(s^4). This gives the stated formula. For linear f, g=\beta and H=0, and the expansion is exact, recovering part 1.

The term s^2\|g\|^2 is a penalty: nonnegative, and it rewards functions that change slowly with the input. The term -s^2r\,\Delta f has no sign. It can be lowered by curving f so that \Delta f has the same sign as the residual, so on its own it is not a regularizer.

3. (a) \mathbb E_{x,y}[r\,\Delta f]=\mathbb E_x\big[\mathbb E[y-f(x)\mid x]\,\Delta f(x)\big]=\mathbb E_x\big[(m-f)\Delta f\big]. This is zero at f=m, and if f-m=O(s^2) it is O(s^2), so the term s^2\,\mathbb E[r\Delta f] is O(s^4).

(b) Noise. The noisy objective is \mathbb E_{x,\varepsilon,\xi}\big(m(x)+\varepsilon-f(x+\xi)\big)^2=\sigma^2+\mathbb E_{t,\xi}\big(m(t-\xi)-f(t)\big)^2, writing \sigma^2 for the variance of \varepsilon. Here t=x+\xi is uniform on the circle and independent of \xi, since x is uniform and independent of \xi. For each t the minimizing f(t) is the mean, f(t)=\mathbb E_\xi\,m(t-\xi)=\mathbb E[m(x)\mid x+\xi=t], which is m convolved with the wrapped Gaussian. The Fourier coefficients of a Gaussian of variance s^2 are e^{-s^2k^2/2}, so \hat f_k=e^{-s^2k^2/2}\hat m_k.

Tikhonov. By Parseval, with coefficients normalized so that \mathbb E|f(x)|^2=\sum_k|\hat f_k|^2, the objective is \sigma^2+\sum_k\big(|\hat m_k-\hat f_k|^2+s^2k^2|\hat f_k|^2\big). Each term is minimized at \hat f_k=\hat m_k/(1+s^2k^2).

Comparison. To order s^2, e^{-s^2k^2/2}\approx1-\frac12s^2k^2 and 1/(1+s^2k^2)\approx1-s^2k^2. Since multiplying \hat m_k by -k^2 is taking a second derivative, f_{\text{noise}}=m+\tfrac{s^2}2\,m''+O(s^4),\qquad f_{\text{Tikhonov}}=m+s^2\,m''+O(s^4). They differ at order s^2, the same order as the regularization effect itself: the Tikhonov penalty with coefficient s^2 smooths twice as much as the noise does.

What (a) missed. The dropped term is O(s^4) in value near m, but its gradient with respect to f is O(s^2), the same order as the gradient of s^2\,\mathbb E f'^2, so dropping it moves the minimizer at leading order. Keeping it fixes this. On the circle, integrating by parts gives -\mathbb E[(m-f)f'']=\mathbb E[(m'-f')f'], so the full second-order objective is \sigma^2+\mathbb E(m-f)^2+s^2\,\mathbb E[f'^2]+s^2\,\mathbb E[(m'-f')f']=\sigma^2+\mathbb E(m-f)^2+s^2\,\mathbb E[m'f'] , whose minimizer satisfies -2(m-f)-s^2m''=0, i.e. f=m+\frac{s^2}2m'', in agreement with the exact answer. The s^2 part of this objective is linear in f, not a penalty at all.

So the precise statements are these.

  • Linear model: input noise with covariance s^2I is ridge with \lambda=s^2, exactly (part 1).
  • Flexible model, plenty of data: noise training converges to the smoothed regression function \mathbb E[m(x)\mid x+\xi], the “target smoothing” picture of Reed, Marks and Oh (1995).
  • What Bishop’s argument does establish: for every f within O(s^2) of m, the noisy objective and the Tikhonov objective differ by O(s^4); and both minimizers are m+O(s^2).
  • What it does not: the O(s^2) correction to m, which is the whole regularization effect, differs. On the uniform circle the Tikhonov minimizer moves twice as far, so there a penalty with coefficient s^2/2 instead of s^2 would match to order s^2. With a non-uniform input density \rho(x) even that fails: to order s^2, \mathbb E[m(x)\mid x+\xi]=m+s^2\big(\tfrac12\Delta m+\nabla\log\rho\cdot\nabla m\big), whereas the Tikhonov minimizer with coefficient c is m+c\,\big(\Delta m+\nabla\log\rho\cdot\nabla m\big) to first order in c, and no single c matches both terms. The perturbative weight correction in Bishop’s §3 is computed from the reduced penalty, so it inherits the same discrepancy.

4. Write \beta_P=P\beta, \beta_Q=Q\beta. P and Q are symmetric, P^2=P, Q^2=Q, PQ=0 and P+Q=I (all from S^2=I), so they are complementary orthogonal projections onto the symmetric vectors (Sv=v) and the antisymmetric vectors (Sv=-v). Since S\beta_P=\beta_P and S\beta_Q=-\beta_Q, \beta^\top Sx=(S\beta)^\top x=\beta_P^\top x-\beta_Q^\top x. With a_i=y_i-\beta_P^\top x_i and c_i=\beta_Q^\top x_i, the two losses are (a_i-c_i)^2 and (a_i+c_i)^2, whose average is a_i^2+c_i^2. Since \beta_P^\top x_i=(XP\beta)_i and c_i=(XQ\beta)_i, this is the claim.

The fit term depends only on P\beta and the penalty only on Q\beta, and the penalty is minimized, at 0, exactly when XQ\beta=0. If XQ is injective on the range of Q, that forces Q\hat\beta=0. Then f(Sx)=\hat\beta^\top Sx=\hat\beta^\top x=f(x), and P\hat\beta minimizes \|y-XP\beta\|^2: least squares on the symmetrized inputs Px_i. This is a hard constraint, not a trade-off. The antisymmetric part of \beta earns nothing in the fit, because its contributions to an example and to its mirror image cancel on average, so any penalty on it drives it to zero.

The same computation works for any finite group G of orthogonal matrices (shifts, rotations by multiples of 90°, and so on) when each example is replaced by its whole orbit. Let P=\frac1{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}g. Then P is the orthogonal projection onto the vectors fixed by every g, and \frac1{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}(y-\beta^\top gx)^2=(y-\beta^\top Px)^2+\frac1{|G|}\sum_{g\in G}\big(\beta^\top(g-P)x\big)^2 , a mean plus a variance over the orbit. The variance term depends only on (I-P)\beta and vanishes for invariant \beta. This is the linear-regression case of the view of Chen, Dobriban and Lee (2020), that augmentation is averaging over the orbits of a group.

The cost. If the truth has \beta_Q\ne0, the augmented estimator can never learn \beta_Q^\top x, however much data it sees. For mirror-symmetric inputs (part 5) the excess risk is at least \beta_Q^\top\Sigma\beta_Q. Augmentation is a bet that the symmetry holds: good for photographs of cats, bad for telling “b” from “d”.

5. (a) Mirror symmetry of the inputs gives S\Sigma S=\mathbb E[Sx\,(Sx)^\top]=\Sigma, so S\Sigma=\Sigma S. Then \Sigma commutes with P and Q, and P\Sigma Q=\Sigma PQ=0.

(b) With a=y-\beta_P^\top x and c=\beta_Q^\top x as in 4, the augmented loss of one example averages to (1-\alpha)(a-c)^2+\alpha(a+c)^2=a^2+c^2-2(1-2\alpha)\,ac. Now \mathbb E[ac]=\mathbb E[yx]^\top\beta_Q-\beta_P^\top\Sigma\beta_Q=\mathbb E[yx]^\top\beta_Q by (a). Writing \rho=1-2\alpha and h=\mathbb E[yx], the augmented risk is \mathbb E\big(y-\beta_P^\top x\big)^2+\beta_Q^\top\Sigma\beta_Q-2\rho\,h^\top\beta_Q . The clean risk is the case \rho=1. Both separate into a \beta_P part, which is the same, and a \beta_Q part. Minimizing over \beta_Q in the range of Q gives Q\Sigma Q\,\beta_Q=\rho\,Qh, so \beta_Q=\rho\,\beta_Q^{\mathrm{clean}}.

(c) Adding \kappa\,\beta_Q^\top\Sigma\beta_Q to the clean risk gives (1+\kappa)\,Q\Sigma Q\,\beta_Q=Qh, so \beta_Q=\beta_Q^{\mathrm{clean}}/(1+\kappa) and leaves \beta_P alone. This matches (b) iff 1/(1+\kappa)=1-2\alpha, i.e. \kappa=2\alpha/(1-2\alpha). As \alpha\to\tfrac12, \kappa\to\infty and the non-invariant part is removed entirely, which is part 4. Partial augmentation is a soft version of the same symmetry constraint: ridge on exactly the directions the symmetry says should not matter.

1.D.6. Dropout as adaptive L^2 (optional)

Why. Dropout randomly zeroes units during training. In a linear model, averaging over the dropout noise gives an explicit penalty, which shows what dropout regularizes and in which metric.

Setup. Linear predictor f(x)=\beta^\top x, training pairs (x_i,y_i) with x_i\in\mathbb R^p the rows of X\in\mathbb R^{n\times p}, squared loss. Drop inputs independently: \tilde x_{ij}=x_{ij}\xi_{ij}/q with \xi_{ij}\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(q), where q is the keep probability. The 1/q keeps \mathbb E\tilde x_{ij}=x_{ij}. Part 3 is the main observation of Wager, Wang and Liang (2013).

  1. Show that \mathbb E_\xi\frac1n\sum_i(y_i-\tilde x_i^\top\beta)^2=\frac1n\|y-X\beta\|^2+\frac{1-q}{q}\sum_j\beta_j^2\,\frac{\|x_{\cdot j}\|^2}{n}, where x_{\cdot j} is the j-th column of X.
  2. Write the penalty as \beta^\top\Gamma\beta. Show that it is invariant under rescaling a feature (x_{\cdot j}\to cx_{\cdot j}, \beta_j\to\beta_j/c), while the ridge penalty is not. Which standard procedure is it equivalent to?
  3. Take a generalized linear model with per-example loss A(x^\top\beta)-y\,x^\top\beta (for logistic regression, A(\eta)=\log(1+e^\eta)). Expand to second order in the dropout noise and show that the expected penalty is approximately \frac{1-q}{2q}\cdot\frac1n\sum_iA''(x_i^\top\beta)\sum_jx_{ij}^2\beta_j^2. In what sense is this “adaptive”?
Hint

Write \tilde x_i^\top\beta=x_i^\top\beta+\sum_jx_{ij}\beta_j(\xi_{ij}/q-1). The noise has mean zero and \mathrm{Var}(\xi/q)=(1-q)/q.

Solution

1. Let \eta_i=x_i^\top\beta and \delta_i=\tilde x_i^\top\beta-\eta_i=\sum_jx_{ij}\beta_j(\xi_{ij}/q-1). Then \mathbb E\delta_i=0 and, by independence, \mathrm{Var}\,\delta_i=\frac{1-q}{q}\sum_jx_{ij}^2\beta_j^2. So \mathbb E(y_i-\eta_i-\delta_i)^2=(y_i-\eta_i)^2+\mathrm{Var}\,\delta_i. Averaging over i gives the penalty \frac{1-q}{q}\sum_j\beta_j^2\|x_{\cdot j}\|^2/n.

2. \Gamma=\frac{1-q}{q}\,\mathrm{diag}(X^\top X/n). Under x_{\cdot j}\to cx_{\cdot j}, \beta_j\to\beta_j/c, the product \beta_j^2\|x_{\cdot j}\|^2 is unchanged, while ridge’s \beta_j^2 changes by 1/c^2. Dropout on inputs is ridge after standardizing each feature by its root mean square: ridge in the metric \mathrm{diag}(\hat\Sigma).

3. \mathbb E\tilde x_i^\top\beta=\eta_i, so the linear term -y\,\tilde x_i^\top\beta is unchanged in expectation. A second-order expansion gives \mathbb E A(\eta_i+\delta_i)\approx A(\eta_i)+\tfrac12A''(\eta_i)\mathrm{Var}\,\delta_i, so the penalty is \frac1n\sum_i\tfrac12A''(\eta_i)\frac{1-q}{q}\sum_jx_{ij}^2\beta_j^2. For logistic regression A''(\eta)=\hat p(1-\hat p), the Fisher information of the example. The penalty is ridge in the metric of the diagonal Fisher information. Confidently classified examples contribute little, and features that matter on uncertain examples are penalized most. “Adaptive” because the metric depends on the current \beta. For squared loss written as \tfrac12(y-\eta)^2, A''=1, which recovers 1 up to the factor \tfrac12 of that convention.

The general lesson: many “hacky” regularizers are quadratic penalties in disguise, in some metric. The question to ask is “which metric?”

1.D.7. Why flatness could matter, and why sharpness needs a metric

Why. A popular heuristic says that minima in flat regions of the loss landscape generalize better than sharp ones, and several training methods (e.g. sharpness-aware minimization) aim for flatness. Here is the exact local statement behind the heuristic, and the reason the naive version cannot be right.

Setup. Near a minimum \theta^* of the population risk R, write H=\nabla^2R(\theta^*) for the Hessian (the curvature: large eigenvalues are sharp directions, small ones flat). A trained parameter \hat\theta=\theta^*+\delta is random through the training data (and the optimizer); write \Sigma=\mathbb E\,\delta\delta^\top for its spread around \theta^*. The per-sample gradient covariance at the optimum, C=\mathrm{Cov}_z[\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta^*;z)], measures how much single examples disagree about which way is downhill; it reappears in §4.

  1. Let \theta^* minimize R, with H=\nabla^2R(\theta^*), and let the trained parameter be \hat\theta=\theta^*+\delta with \mathbb E\,\delta\delta^\top=\Sigma. Show that \mathbb E R(\hat\theta)-R(\theta^*)\approx\tfrac12\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma). Check that this is exact for OLS with test inputs x\sim\mathcal N(0,I_p), where R(b)=\sigma^2+\|b-\beta\|^2 and \Sigma=\sigma^2(X^\top X)^{-1} (1.B.1).
  2. Suppose \hat\theta is ERM in a regular model with \Sigma\approx\frac1nH^{-1}CH^{-1} (the “sandwich” covariance, derived in 4.A.1), where C is the covariance of the per-sample gradients at \theta^*. Show that \tfrac12\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma)=\frac1{2n}\mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C), and that if C=cH this equals cp/(2n), whatever the curvature. What has to be true of \Sigma for “flatter is better” to hold?
  3. Dinh et al.’s rescaling. Take the two-layer scalar linear network f(x)=w_2w_1x with R(w)=\mathbb E(y-w_2w_1x)^2 and a=\mathbb E x^2>0. Assume c^*=\mathbb E[xy]/a\ne0; the minima are the hyperbola w_1w_2=c^*. Compute the Hessian at a minimum, show that \mathrm{Tr}\,H=2a(w_1^2+w_2^2) and that one eigenvalue is zero. Under (w_1,w_2)\to(\alpha w_1,w_2/\alpha) the function does not change. Show that \mathrm{Tr}\,H takes every value in [4a|c^*|,\infty). Why does the same trick work in deep ReLU networks?
  4. Let \theta=g(\varphi) be a smooth reparametrization with Jacobian J at the minimum, and let \delta transform as a tangent vector. Show that \mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma) is invariant while \mathrm{Tr}\,H is not. What does a meaningful flatness claim have to specify?
Hint

For 1, the gradient vanishes at \theta^*, and \mathbb E\,\delta^\top H\delta=\mathrm{Tr}(H\,\mathbb E\,\delta\delta^\top). For 3, write R=a(w_1w_2-c^*)^2+\text{const}. For 4, at a critical point the Hessian transforms as J^\top HJ, because the term involving the second derivative of g multiplies \nabla R=0.

Solution

1. R(\theta^*+\delta)=R(\theta^*)+\nabla R(\theta^*)^\top\delta+\tfrac12\delta^\top H\delta+O(\|\delta\|^3). The gradient term vanishes, and \mathbb E\,\delta^\top H\delta=\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma). Here \Sigma is the second moment about \theta^*, so it includes any bias. For OLS, R(b)=\sigma^2+\|b-\beta\|^2 is exactly quadratic with H=2I, and \Sigma=\sigma^2(X^\top X)^{-1}, so \tfrac12\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma)=\sigma^2\mathrm{Tr}(X^\top X)^{-1} exactly, as in 1.B.1.2.

2. \tfrac12\mathrm{Tr}\big(H\cdot\tfrac1nH^{-1}CH^{-1}\big)=\tfrac1{2n}\mathrm{Tr}(CH^{-1}). If C=cH, this is cp/(2n). The curvature cancels: a sharp direction is pinned down more tightly by the data (\Sigma\propto H^{-1}), and this exactly offsets the larger cost of a given error there. “Flatter is better” needs \Sigma that does not shrink in proportion as H grows. One case is a perturbation scale set by something other than the data’s curvature, such as SGD noise at a fixed learning rate: if the gradient noise scales as C\propto H^{g}, 4.B.3 finds \Sigma\propto H^{g-1} (it writes the exponent as \gamma). So “flat minima generalize” is a claim about H and \Sigma jointly.

3. Write u=w_1w_2, so R=a(u-c^*)^2+\text{const}. Then \partial_{w_1}R=2a(u-c^*)w_2, and at a minimum (u=c^*),

H=2a\begin{pmatrix}w_2^2&w_1w_2\\ w_1w_2&w_1^2\end{pmatrix}=2a\,vv^\top,\qquad v=(w_2,w_1)^\top .

The terms proportional to u-c^* vanish there. So H has rank one, with eigenvalue 2a(w_1^2+w_2^2)=\mathrm{Tr}\,H along v and eigenvalue 0 along (w_1,-w_2), the direction tangent to the hyperbola. Under the rescaling, \mathrm{Tr}\,H=2a(\alpha^2w_1^2+w_2^2/\alpha^2)\ge4a|w_1w_2|=4a|c^*| by AM–GM, with equality at \alpha^2=|w_2/w_1|, and it is unbounded as \alpha\to0 or \infty. Every value in [4a|c^*|,\infty) is attained by the same function. Weight decay would pick the balanced representative. For ReLU networks, \mathrm{ReLU}(\alpha z)=\alpha\,\mathrm{ReLU}(z) for \alpha>0, so multiplying one layer by \alpha and the next by 1/\alpha leaves the network function unchanged. Dinh et al. use this to make any minimum arbitrarily sharp.

4. At a critical point, H_\varphi=J^\top HJ. To first order \delta_\theta=J\delta_\varphi, so \Sigma_\varphi=J^{-1}\Sigma J^{-\top}. Then \mathrm{Tr}(H_\varphi\Sigma_\varphi)=\mathrm{Tr}(J^\top HJJ^{-1}\Sigma J^{-\top})=\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma), which is invariant, whereas \mathrm{Tr}\,H_\varphi=\mathrm{Tr}(J^\top HJ) depends on J. A meaningful flatness claim has to say in what metric the perturbations are measured: either the distribution \Sigma of perturbations that actually occurs, or an invariant geometry such as the Fisher (output-space) metric. Raw \mathrm{Tr}\,H in weight space is a property of the parametrization, not of the function.

1.D.8. Reading: sharp minima can generalize
