Sharp minima can generalize for deep nets, Laurent Dinh, Razvan Pascanu, Samy Bengio, Yoshua Bengio (ICML 2017) Importance ●●●●● Length ●●●○○

Because of the rescaling symmetries of ReLU networks, a minimum can be made arbitrarily sharp under several standard sharpness definitions without changing the function computed. So flatness in weight space cannot, on its own, explain generalization. Read §2–§4: the definitions of flatness (volume ε-flatness, Hessian-based measures, ε-sharpness), the non-negative homogeneity of rectifiers, and the construction you just did in 1.D.7.3, generalized to deep rectifier networks. §5 (reparametrizing the model and the input) is short and makes the same point more generally.

Question.

Dinh et al. is often cited as “flatness is meaningless”. Using 1.D.7.4, say precisely what the paper shows and what it leaves open. Name one flatness-like quantity it does not touch.

Solution

The paper shows that weight-space sharpness measures (\mathrm{Tr}\,H, the top eigenvalue, \epsilon-sharpness in a Euclidean ball, flat volume) are not functions of the network function, because a symmetry of the parametrization changes them arbitrarily. So none of them can be a sufficient statistic for generalization, and an empirical correlation between them and test error depends on how parametrizations happen to be distributed in practice. It leaves open quantities that are invariant under the symmetry or carry their own metric:

  • \mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma) with the \Sigma that training actually produces;
  • sharpness measured in the Fisher or output-space metric;
  • normalized or scale-invariant sharpness;
  • the volume of a function-space neighbourhood (the posterior mass of a function), which is what PAC-Bayes and Bayesian accounts use.

It also leaves open whether SGD, from standard initializations, tends to land in parametrizations where raw sharpness happens to track one of these. That is an empirical question about the training procedure, not about the geometry.

Where this leaves “the hacky bullshit”. In linear models, weight decay, early stopping, input noise and dropout are all ways of choosing a filter on the data’s principal directions, each in its own metric; L1 is the exception that selects coordinates instead of shrinking directions. The “prior” reading and the “capacity constraint” reading of the same penalty make different predictions about networks trained on random labels (2.D). Flatness enters through \Sigma and H together, and only once a metric is fixed; PAC-Bayes (2.E) is one way of fixing it.

1.E. Kernels and the kernel trick

The question. Linear regression has a complete theory: closed-form estimators, exact bias and variance, and regularization as a filter on the data’s principal directions. Can we keep all of that and still fit nonlinear functions, even ones from an infinite-dimensional family? Yes: replace the input x by a feature vector \phi(x) and do linear regression on the features. The kernel trick is the observation that ridge regression on features needs only the inner products k(x,x')=\phi(x)^\top\phi(x'), which can often be computed cheaply even when \phi(x) has astronomically many, or infinitely many, coordinates.

Why it matters here. Kernel methods were the leading nonparametric methods before deep learning. They are the cleanest setting in which a model with more parameters than data points, fit to zero training error, can be solved exactly: the ridgeless limit of kernel ridge regression is a minimum-norm interpolant, the object behind double descent and benign overfitting in section 3 of this workbook (3.B, 3.D). And they are the main bridge from linear theory to neural networks: a network linearized around its initialization is kernel regression with the neural tangent kernel, and a network with a frozen random first layer approximates a kernel method (end of this subsection). WB5 §1 derives scaling-law learning curves for ridge regression with a power-law spectrum, which covers kernel and random-feature models.

What you get. 1.E.1: ridge regression in dual form, the representer theorem, and the ridgeless limit as a minimum-norm interpolant. 1.E.2: the polynomial and Gaussian kernels, and random features. 1.E.3: Gaussian-process regression, whose posterior mean is kernel ridge regression.

Setup. Training data are pairs (x_1,y_1),\dots,(x_n,y_n). The inputs x_i lie in any set \mathcal X (vectors in \mathbb R^d, but also strings or graphs), and the labels form a vector y\in\mathbb R^n. A feature map \phi:\mathcal X\to\mathbb R^D sends each input to D features, where D may be much larger than n or infinite (then \mathbb R^D means the square-summable sequences; nothing below changes). The model is linear in the features, f_w(x)=w^\top\phi(x). The feature design matrix \Phi\in\mathbb R^{n\times D} has rows \phi(x_i)^\top. Ridge regression on the features is

\hat w_\lambda=\arg\min_w\ \tfrac1n\|y-\Phi w\|^2+\lambda\|w\|^2=(\Phi^\top\Phi+n\lambda I_D)^{-1}\Phi^\top y ,\qquad \lambda>0 .

The kernel of the feature map is k(x,x')=\phi(x)^\top\phi(x'). The kernel matrix (Gram matrix) is K=\Phi\Phi^\top\in\mathbb R^{n\times n}, with entries K_{ij}=k(x_i,x_j). For a test input x, write k_x=\Phi\phi(x)=\big(k(x,x_1),\dots,k(x,x_n)\big)^\top. Take the thin SVD \Phi=U\,\mathrm{diag}(s_1,\dots,s_r)\,V^\top, where r is the rank of \Phi, the singular values are s_k>0, and the left and right singular vectors are u_k\in\mathbb R^n and v_k\in\mathbb R^D. Then K=\sum_ks_k^2u_ku_k^\top: the nonzero eigenvalues of the kernel matrix are the squared singular values of the feature design. In this basis ridge is a filter (1.D.4.1, with \Phi in place of the design matrix X): it multiplies the least-squares coefficient along the k-th direction by s_k^2/(s_k^2+n\lambda), keeping directions with s_k^2\gg n\lambda and suppressing those with s_k^2\ll n\lambda.

Which functions are kernels. A symmetric function k:\mathcal X\times\mathcal X\to\mathbb R is a positive-definite kernel if every Gram matrix [k(z_a,z_b)]_{a,b\le N}, for every finite set of points z_1,\dots,z_N, is positive semidefinite. Any k=\phi^\top\phi is one, because c^\top Kc=\|\Phi^\top c\|^2\ge0. The converse is what makes kernels useful (Moore–Aronszajn; stated, not proved): every positive-definite kernel is an inner product of features in some Hilbert space. So one may pick k directly, without ever writing down \phi. The functions the model can represent, f_w, form the reproducing-kernel Hilbert space (RKHS) \mathcal H_k, with norm \|f\|_{\mathcal H}=\min\{\|w\|:f_w=f\}. So “small \|w\|” and “small RKHS norm” are the same penalty. Spectrally (Mercer’s theorem, stated): if the integral operator of k under the input distribution has eigenvalues \mu_j and eigenfunctions e_j, then \|\sum_jc_je_j\|_{\mathcal H}^2=\sum_jc_j^2/\mu_j. The norm charges heavily for components along small-eigenvalue eigenfunctions, which for smooth kernels such as the Gaussian kernel below are the rapidly varying ones. With many training inputs, the top eigenvalues of K/n approximate the top \mu_j.

1.E.1. Ridge in dual form, the representer theorem, and the ridgeless limit
  1. Push-through. For a>0, show that (\Phi^\top\Phi+aI_D)^{-1}\Phi^\top=\Phi^\top(\Phi\Phi^\top+aI_n)^{-1}. Deduce that \hat w_\lambda=\Phi^\top\hat\alpha with \hat\alpha=(K+n\lambda I_n)^{-1}y, and that the prediction at any input x is \hat f_\lambda(x)=\sum_{i=1}^n\hat\alpha_i\,k(x,x_i)=k_x^\top(K+n\lambda I_n)^{-1}y . Suppose evaluating k costs c operations. Compare the costs of fitting and of predicting in the primal form (a D\times D solve) and the dual form (an n\times n solve). When is each cheaper?
  2. Representer theorem. Let \mathcal L:\mathbb R^n\to\mathbb R be any loss that depends on the parameters only through the training predictions \Phi w, and minimize J(w)=\mathcal L(\Phi w)+\lambda\|w\|^2. Show that every minimizer has the form \Phi^\top\alpha for some \alpha\in\mathbb R^n, and rewrite J in terms of \alpha and K only. For the squared loss \mathcal L(v)=\frac1n\|y-v\|^2, check that \hat\alpha from part 1 minimizes the rewritten objective.
  3. Ridgeless limit. Assume K is invertible. This needs n\le D; for the Gaussian kernel of 1.E.2 it holds whenever the x_i are distinct. Show that as \lambda\to0, \hat f_\lambda(x)\to k_x^\top K^{-1}y, that this limit interpolates the training data, and that \hat w_0=\Phi^\top K^{-1}y is the minimum-norm interpolant: it minimizes \|w\| subject to \Phi w=y, with \|\hat w_0\|^2=y^\top K^{-1}y.
  4. What the interpolant pays for. Show that the fitted values and the norm of the fit are \hat y=\Phi\hat w_\lambda=\sum_k\frac{s_k^2}{s_k^2+n\lambda}\,(u_k^\top y)\,u_k,\qquad \|\hat w_\lambda\|^2=\sum_k\frac{s_k^2\,(u_k^\top y)^2}{(s_k^2+n\lambda)^2} . In the setting of part 3, let y_i=f^*(x_i)+\varepsilon_i, where f^*=f_{w^*} and the noise is independent with mean 0 and variance \sigma^2. Show that \mathbb E\|\hat w_0\|^2=\sum_k(v_k^\top w^*)^2+\sigma^2\,\mathrm{Tr}\,K^{-1}. Which part of y makes the interpolant’s norm large when K has tiny eigenvalues, as kernel matrices of smooth kernels do?
Hint

For 1, check that \Phi^\top(\Phi\Phi^\top+aI)=(\Phi^\top\Phi+aI)\Phi^\top, then multiply by inverses on each side. For 2, write w=\Phi^\top\alpha+w_\perp with w_\perp orthogonal to every \phi(x_i), so \Phi w_\perp=0, and use Pythagoras. For 3, any interpolant is \hat w_0+w' with \Phi w'=0; show that w'\perp\hat w_0. For 4, K(K+aI)^{-1}=\sum_k\frac{s_k^2}{s_k^2+a}u_ku_k^\top, and f^* evaluated at the training inputs is \Phi w^*, whose component along u_k is s_k\,v_k^\top w^*.

Solution

1. Both sides of \Phi^\top(\Phi\Phi^\top+aI_n)=\Phi^\top\Phi\Phi^\top+a\Phi^\top=(\Phi^\top\Phi+aI_D)\Phi^\top agree. For a>0 both bracketed matrices are positive definite, hence invertible. Multiplying on the left by (\Phi^\top\Phi+aI_D)^{-1} and on the right by (\Phi\Phi^\top+aI_n)^{-1} gives the identity. With a=n\lambda, \hat w_\lambda=\Phi^\top(K+n\lambda I)^{-1}y=\Phi^\top\hat\alpha, and \hat f_\lambda(x)=\phi(x)^\top\Phi^\top\hat\alpha=k_x^\top\hat\alpha.

Costs, counting arithmetic operations:

fit predict one x
primal form \Phi^\top\Phi: O(nD^2); solve: O(D^3) compute \phi(x), then O(D)
dual form K: O(n^2c); solve: O(n^3) O(nc)

The dual form wins when D\gg n, and it is the only option when D=\infty; the primal form wins when n\gg D. For large n the O(n^3) solve and the O(n^2) memory for K are the bottleneck of kernel methods, which is what random features (1.E.2.3) address.

2. Split w=w_\parallel+w_\perp, where w_\parallel=\Phi^\top\alpha lies in the span of \phi(x_1),\dots,\phi(x_n) and w_\perp is orthogonal to it. Each entry of \Phi w_\perp is \phi(x_i)^\top w_\perp=0, so \mathcal L(\Phi w)=\mathcal L(\Phi w_\parallel), while \|w\|^2=\|w_\parallel\|^2+\|w_\perp\|^2. Hence J(w)=J(w_\parallel)+\lambda\|w_\perp\|^2, and any w with w_\perp\neq0 is strictly beaten by w_\parallel. Substituting \Phi w=\Phi\Phi^\top\alpha=K\alpha and \|w\|^2=\alpha^\top K\alpha,

J=\mathcal L(K\alpha)+\lambda\,\alpha^\top K\alpha .

The infinite-dimensional problem has become an n-dimensional one that needs only kernel values. The same proof works for any regularizer that is a nondecreasing function of \|w\| (with “a minimizer” in place of “every minimizer” when it is not strictly increasing).

For the squared loss, the gradient in \alpha is -\frac2nK(y-K\alpha)+2\lambda K\alpha=\frac2nK\big[(K+n\lambda I)\alpha-y\big], which vanishes at \hat\alpha. The objective is a convex quadratic in \alpha, so a stationary point is a minimizer. (If K is singular, other minimizers \hat\alpha+v with Kv=0 exist, but \|\Phi^\top v\|^2=v^\top Kv=0, so they all give the same w.)

3. If K is invertible, (K+n\lambda I)^{-1}\to K^{-1}, so \hat f_\lambda(x)\to k_x^\top K^{-1}y. At the training inputs, \Phi\hat w_0=\Phi\Phi^\top K^{-1}y=y, so the limit interpolates. Any other interpolant is \hat w_0+w' with \Phi w'=0, and \hat w_0^\top w'=y^\top K^{-1}\Phi w'=0, so \|\hat w_0+w'\|^2=\|\hat w_0\|^2+\|w'\|^2\ge\|\hat w_0\|^2. Finally \|\hat w_0\|^2=y^\top K^{-1}\Phi\Phi^\top K^{-1}y=y^\top K^{-1}y. In RKHS language: among all functions in \mathcal H_k that fit the data exactly, k_x^\top K^{-1}y has the smallest RKHS norm. Here \hat w_0=\Phi^+y (Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse): the same minimum-norm interpolant that gradient descent from zero finds in linear regression with more parameters than data (1.D.4.4, 3.B.1).

4. On the span of the u_k, K(K+aI)^{-1} acts as s_k^2/(s_k^2+a), and it is zero on the orthogonal complement. So \hat y=K\hat\alpha=K(K+n\lambda I)^{-1}y is as stated, and \|\hat w_\lambda\|^2=\hat\alpha^\top K\hat\alpha=\sum_ks_k^2(u_k^\top y)^2/(s_k^2+n\lambda)^2. This is the primal ridge filter of 1.D.4, as it must be: it is the same estimator.

At \lambda=0, \|\hat w_0\|^2=\sum_k(u_k^\top y)^2/s_k^2. Write y=\Phi w^*+\varepsilon. Since u_k^\top\Phi w^*=s_kv_k^\top w^*, and \mathbb E(u_k^\top\varepsilon)^2=\sigma^2 in every direction,

\mathbb E\|\hat w_0\|^2=\sum_k(v_k^\top w^*)^2+\sigma^2\sum_k\frac1{s_k^2}=\|P_{\rm row}w^*\|^2+\sigma^2\,\mathrm{Tr}\,K^{-1},

where P_{\rm row} projects onto the span of the \phi(x_i). The signal contributes at most \|w^*\|^2, however small the eigenvalues. The noise contributes \sigma^2/s_k^2 per direction, which explodes along the smallest eigenvalues of K: the interpolant fits the noise using exactly the directions the kernel considers least plausible (largest RKHS cost), and pays for it in norm. Ridge suppresses those directions instead. Whether the interpolant’s noise fit actually hurts test error depends on how many small eigenvalues there are and how they spread; that is benign overfitting, 3.D.

1.E.2. Two kernels and random features

Why. The kernel trick pays off when k is cheap and \phi is huge. The two standard kernels on \mathbb R^d show both extremes: the polynomial kernel k(x,x')=(1+x^\top x')^m of degree m\in\{1,2,\dots\}, with finitely but astronomically many features, and the Gaussian (or RBF) kernel k(x,x')=\exp\!\big(-\|x-x'\|^2/(2\ell^2)\big) with length scale \ell>0, with infinitely many. Going the other way, random features approximate a kernel by a finite, randomly drawn \phi, which is a two-layer network with a frozen first layer.

The Gaussian kernel is strictly positive definite: its Gram matrix is invertible for any set of distinct points (stated). So ridgeless Gaussian kernel regression can interpolate any labels whatsoever, including pure noise. When many inputs lie within a length scale of each other, though, K has very small eigenvalues and is badly conditioned.

  1. Polynomial kernel. Expand (1+x^\top x')^m by the multinomial theorem and write it as \phi(x)^\top\phi(x'), where the coordinates of \phi(x) are scaled monomials \prod_ix_i^{j_i} of total degree at most m. Show that there are \binom{d+m}{m} of them. Evaluate this for d=784 (the pixels of an MNIST digit) with m=3 and m=5, and compare with the O(d) cost of evaluating the kernel.
  2. Gaussian kernel in one dimension. Factor e^{-(x-x')^2/(2\ell^2)} and Taylor-expand the cross term to show that k(x,x')=\sum_{j=0}^\infty\phi_j(x)\phi_j(x') with \phi_j(x)=e^{-x^2/(2\ell^2)}\,\frac{(x/\ell)^j}{\sqrt{j!}} . So the Gaussian kernel is an inner product of infinitely many features. For |x|\lesssim\ell, how large is \phi_j(x) for large j? Using \|f\|_{\mathcal H}=\min\{\|w\|:f_w=f\}, what does the RKHS norm charge for a function that needs a large degree-j component?
  3. Random Fourier features (Rahimi and Recht, 2007). Let w\sim\mathcal N(0,\ell^{-2}I_d) and b\sim\mathrm{Unif}[0,2\pi], independent. Show that \mathbb E\big[2\cos(w^\top x+b)\cos(w^\top x'+b)\big]=e^{-\|x-x'\|^2/(2\ell^2)} . Draw m independent pairs (w_j,b_j) and set z(x)=\sqrt{2/m}\,\big(\cos(w_j^\top x+b_j)\big)_{j=1}^m. Then z(x)^\top z(x') is an average of m independent unbiased estimates of the Gaussian kernel; each has variance at most 1 (stated), so the error is at most about 1/\sqrt m. For n=10^6 training points, compare the cost of ridge regression on m=10^4 random features with that of exact kernel ridge regression.
Hint

For 1, (1+\sum_{i=1}^dx_ix_i')^m=\sum_{j_0+j_1+\dots+j_d=m}\frac{m!}{j_0!\,j_1!\cdots j_d!}\prod_{i\ge1}(x_ix_i')^{j_i}; count the tuples (j_0,\dots,j_d) by stars and bars. For 2, -(x-x')^2=-x^2-x'^2+2xx'. For 3, use 2\cos A\cos B=\cos(A-B)+\cos(A+B), note that \mathbb E_b\cos(c+2b)=0 for every constant c, and use the Gaussian characteristic function \mathbb E\,e^{iw^\top\delta}=e^{-\|\delta\|^2/(2\ell^2)}.

Solution

1. By the multinomial theorem, with j_0=m-\sum_{i\ge1}j_i,

(1+x^\top x')^m=\sum_{j_0+\dots+j_d=m}\frac{m!}{j_0!\,j_1!\cdots j_d!}\prod_{i=1}^d x_i^{j_i}\,x_i'^{\,j_i}=\phi(x)^\top\phi(x'),\qquad \phi_{(j_1,\dots,j_d)}(x)=\sqrt{\frac{m!}{j_0!\,j_1!\cdots j_d!}}\ \prod_{i=1}^dx_i^{j_i} .

The coordinates are indexed by tuples (j_0,\dots,j_d) of d+1 non-negative integers summing to m. Stars and bars gives \binom{m+d}{d}=\binom{d+m}{m}. For d=784: about 8.1\times10^7 features at m=3 and 2.5\times10^{12} at m=5. The kernel costs one 784-dimensional inner product and a power. So polynomial-kernel ridge is ridge over all polynomials of degree at most m (each monomial weighted by the square root of its multinomial coefficient), at the price of an inner product.

2. e^{-(x-x')^2/(2\ell^2)}=e^{-x^2/(2\ell^2)}\,e^{-x'^2/(2\ell^2)}\,e^{xx'/\ell^2}, and e^{xx'/\ell^2}=\sum_{j\ge0}\frac{(x/\ell)^j}{\sqrt{j!}}\cdot\frac{(x'/\ell)^j}{\sqrt{j!}}. Multiplying in the two Gaussian factors gives the stated \phi_j. For |x|\le\ell, |\phi_j(x)|\le1/\sqrt{j!}, which is super-exponentially small. Every f_w has the form f_w(x)=e^{-x^2/(2\ell^2)}\sum_jw_j\,\frac{(x/\ell)^j}{\sqrt{j!}}, so the coefficient of x^j in the power series of e^{x^2/(2\ell^2)}f_w(x) is w_j/(\ell^j\sqrt{j!}), and power series are unique, so this fixes w. Making that coefficient of order one costs w_j^2\sim j!\,\ell^{2j}: the RKHS norm grows super-exponentially with the degree a function needs. Gaussian-kernel ridge is therefore a strong smoothness prior, which charges enormously for variation on scales below \ell. (Example 16.2 of Shalev-Shwartz and Ben-David, reading below, does this expansion with \ell=1.)

3. Write A=w^\top x+b and B=w^\top x'+b. Then 2\cos A\cos B=\cos\big(w^\top(x-x')\big)+\cos\big(w^\top(x+x')+2b\big). Averaging over b with w fixed kills the second term. The first is the real part of e^{iw^\top\delta} with \delta=x-x', and for w\sim\mathcal N(0,\ell^{-2}I), \mathbb E\,e^{iw^\top\delta}=e^{-\|\delta\|^2/(2\ell^2)}. So \mathbb E[2\cos A\cos B]=k(x,x'), and z(x)^\top z(x')=\frac1m\sum_j2\cos(w_j^\top x+b_j)\cos(w_j^\top x'+b_j) averages m independent copies. (The single-term variance is 1-k^2+\tfrac12k^4\in[\tfrac12,1].)

Cost at n=10^6: exact kernel ridge needs K, which has 10^{12} entries (8 TB in double precision), and an O(n^3)=10^{18} solve. Ridge on m=10^4 random features needs Z^\top Z in O(nm^2)=10^{14} operations (it can be accumulated in batches) and an O(m^3)=10^{12} solve.

The map x\mapsto z(x) is the hidden layer of a two-layer network with cosine activations and frozen random weights; ridge on z trains only the readout, and as m\to\infty it converges to Gaussian kernel ridge regression. For finite m it is a model in its own right: in high-dimensional limits, ridgeless regression on m random features has a test-error peak near m\approx n, like the interpolation peak that 3.A–3.C derive for linear least squares (Mei and Montanari, 2019). The same construction works for any continuous shift-invariant kernel with k(0)=1, sampling w from its Fourier transform, which is a probability distribution by Bochner’s theorem (stated; Theorem 1 of Rahimi and Recht).

1.E.3. Gaussian-process regression is kernel ridge regression

Why. Ridge regression is the posterior mean of a Bayesian linear model (1.D.1). The kernel version of that statement is Gaussian-process regression: put a prior directly on functions, with the kernel as covariance, and the posterior mean is kernel ridge regression. The Bayesian view also supplies error bars, and shows what they cannot see.

A Gaussian process f\sim\mathrm{GP}(0,k) is a random function such that, for any finite set of inputs z_1,\dots,z_N, the vector \big(f(z_1),\dots,f(z_N)\big) is jointly Gaussian with mean 0 and covariance matrix [k(z_a,z_b)]_{a,b}. The kernel is now a covariance function; positive definiteness is exactly the condition that makes every one of these matrices a valid covariance. Notation as in the setup: K is the kernel matrix of the training inputs and k_x=(k(x,x_1),\dots,k(x,x_n))^\top.

  1. Weight-space view. Take finite D, and let f(x)=w^\top\phi(x) with prior w\sim\mathcal N(0,\tau^2I_D), where \tau>0. Show that f\sim\mathrm{GP}(0,\tau^2k). (For D=\infty this construction fails, since independent \mathcal N(0,\tau^2) coordinates have infinite norm; the GP is then defined directly as above, and its draws are almost surely not in the RKHS. The GP prior and the RKHS penalty give the same predictor but describe different function classes.)
  2. Posterior. Observe y_i=f(x_i)+\varepsilon_i with \varepsilon\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma^2I_n) independent of f. Show that, given y, f(x) is Gaussian with \mathbb E[f(x)\mid y]=k_x^\top\Big(K+\frac{\sigma^2}{\tau^2}I_n\Big)^{-1}y,\qquad \mathrm{Var}[f(x)\mid y]=\tau^2\Big(k(x,x)-k_x^\top\Big(K+\frac{\sigma^2}{\tau^2}I_n\Big)^{-1}k_x\Big). Conclude that the posterior mean is the kernel ridge prediction with n\lambda=\sigma^2/\tau^2. For finite D, check the variance against the weight-space posterior of 1.D.1.1 (with \Phi in place of X), w\mid y\sim\mathcal N\big(\hat w_\lambda,\ \sigma^2(\Phi^\top\Phi+n\lambda I)^{-1}\big).
  3. Error bars. The posterior variance does not depend on y. Show that it is at most the prior variance \tau^2k(x,x); that at a training input it tends to 0 as \sigma\to0 (assume K invertible); and that for the Gaussian kernel it returns to the prior variance \tau^2 far from all training inputs. What can this error bar not detect?
Hint

For 2, use Gaussian conditioning: if (a,b) is jointly Gaussian with mean zero, then a\mid b\sim\mathcal N\big(\Sigma_{ab}\Sigma_{bb}^{-1}b,\ \Sigma_{aa}-\Sigma_{ab}\Sigma_{bb}^{-1}\Sigma_{ba}\big). Here a=f(x) and b=y. For the weight-space check, use (\Phi^\top\Phi+cI)^{-1}=c^{-1}\big(I-\Phi^\top(K+cI)^{-1}\Phi\big), which follows from 1.E.1.1. For 3, at a training input x_j, k_{x_j} is the j-th column of K.

Solution

1. For inputs z_1,\dots,z_N, the vector (f(z_a))_a=(\phi(z_a)^\top w)_a is a linear image of the Gaussian vector w, hence jointly Gaussian with mean zero. Its covariance is \mathbb E[f(z_a)f(z_b)]=\phi(z_a)^\top\mathbb E[ww^\top]\phi(z_b)=\tau^2k(z_a,z_b).

2. (f(x),y) is jointly Gaussian with \mathrm{Cov}(y)=\tau^2K+\sigma^2I, \mathrm{Cov}(f(x),y)=\tau^2k_x^\top and \mathrm{Var}\,f(x)=\tau^2k(x,x). Conditioning gives

\mathbb E[f(x)\mid y]=\tau^2k_x^\top(\tau^2K+\sigma^2I)^{-1}y=k_x^\top\Big(K+\frac{\sigma^2}{\tau^2}I\Big)^{-1}y,

\mathrm{Var}[f(x)\mid y]=\tau^2k(x,x)-\tau^4k_x^\top(\tau^2K+\sigma^2I)^{-1}k_x=\tau^2\Big(k(x,x)-k_x^\top\Big(K+\frac{\sigma^2}{\tau^2}I\Big)^{-1}k_x\Big).

The mean is k_x^\top(K+n\lambda I)^{-1}y with n\lambda=\sigma^2/\tau^2, the same calibration as ridge-as-MAP in 1.D.1.1, as it should be: the two are the same model.

Weight-space check: write c=n\lambda=\sigma^2/\tau^2. The posterior variance of f(x)=\phi(x)^\top w is

\sigma^2\phi(x)^\top(\Phi^\top\Phi+cI)^{-1}\phi(x)=\frac{\sigma^2}{c}\Big(\phi(x)^\top\phi(x)-\phi(x)^\top\Phi^\top(K+cI)^{-1}\Phi\phi(x)\Big)=\tau^2\Big(k(x,x)-k_x^\top(K+cI)^{-1}k_x\Big).

The identity in the hint holds because (\Phi^\top\Phi+cI)\big(I-\Phi^\top(K+cI)^{-1}\Phi\big)=\Phi^\top\Phi+cI-\Phi^\top(K+cI)(K+cI)^{-1}\Phi=cI.

The two traditions use the same predictor but tune it differently: kernel ridge usually picks \lambda by cross-validation, Gaussian-process regression picks \sigma,\tau and the kernel’s own parameters (such as \ell) by maximizing the marginal likelihood of y.

3. (K+cI)^{-1} is positive definite, so the subtracted term is non-negative and the posterior variance is at most \tau^2k(x,x). At a training input, k_{x_j} is the j-th column of K, so K^{-1}k_{x_j}=\mathbf 1_j, the j-th standard basis vector, and as \sigma\to0 the variance tends to \tau^2(K_{jj}-K_{jj})=0. For the Gaussian kernel, k_x\to0 as x moves away from all training inputs, so the variance returns to \tau^2k(x,x)=\tau^2.

Because the variance does not depend on y, it cannot detect that the model is wrong: a kernel with the wrong length scale, the wrong noise level, or a target that is not a plausible draw from the prior gives equally confident error bars. They are calibrated only if the prior is right. In practice y enters only through the hyperparameters fitted by marginal likelihood.

Where this leads. Linearize a network f(x;\theta) around its initialization \theta_0: f(x;\theta)\approx f(x;\theta_0)+\nabla_\theta f(x;\theta_0)^\top(\theta-\theta_0). This is a linear model with features \phi(x)=\nabla_\theta f(x;\theta_0) and kernel \Theta(x,x')=\nabla_\theta f(x;\theta_0)^\top\nabla_\theta f(x';\theta_0), the neural tangent kernel (Jacot, Gabriel and Hongler, 2018). Gradient flow on squared loss for a linear model that can interpolate the data converges to the interpolant closest to its starting point (1.D.4.4), so in this linearized (“lazy” or “kernel”) regime the trained network predicts f(x;\theta_0) plus ridgeless kernel regression of the residuals y_i-f(x_i;\theta_0) with kernel \Theta. Separately, a randomly initialized fully connected network, with independent weights whose variance scales as one over the layer width, becomes a Gaussian process as the widths go to infinity (Lee et al., 2018), so 1.E.3 also describes exact Bayesian inference in infinitely wide networks. Much of the debate about why deep networks generalize is about how far real networks are from this kernel regime. The Training Dynamics materials take up the linearized network and the lazy-versus-rich distinction; the infinite-width NTK theory is on the Physics of Deep Learning day.

For reference (books): Understanding Machine Learning: From Theory to Algorithms, Shalev-Shwartz and Ben-David (2014; free PDF), ch. 16, §16.1–16.2; Gaussian Processes for Machine Learning, Rasmussen and Williams (2006; free online), ch. 2, §2.1–2.3; ch. 6, §6.1–6.2.

1.F. Information bottleneck and rate–distortion

The question. A common intuition about learned representations is that a good one is a compressed summary of the input: it throws away what is irrelevant to the task and keeps what is relevant. On this view the last hidden layer of an image classifier should have forgotten the lighting and the background and remembered the object. The intuition is appealing, but it says nothing definite until “compressed” and “relevant” are given a meaning. This subsection gives them one, using information theory, and then looks at a debate over whether deep networks actually compress during training.

Rate–distortion theory is Shannon’s theory of lossy compression (1959). Suppose a source X may be described only approximately, with average error at most D under a chosen distortion measure d. What is the fewest nats per symbol that such a description can use? The answer, R(D), is the solution of a mutual-information minimization. Its optimal encoders have a Boltzmann form, with the distortion as energy and an inverse temperature \beta. 1.F.1 works through the two textbook sources (a biased coin and a Gaussian) and the general solution.

The information bottleneck (IB, 1.F.2) applies the same idea with the distortion set by a task: a representation T of X should keep the information about a target Y and discard the rest. It turns out to be a rate–distortion problem whose distortion measure depends on the encoder being optimized.

The information plane. Do trained networks actually compress in this sense? Shwartz-Ziv and Tishby (2017) reported that hidden layers first fit the labels and then compress, and connected the compression to generalization. The exchange that followed turned on what mutual information measures when a layer is a deterministic function of its input. 1.F.3–1.F.6 derive the points at issue in small models; 1.F.7 has the papers.

What is here. A primer (below) defines every information-theoretic quantity used in the subsection; skip it if you know Cover and Thomas ch. 2. 1.F.1 and 1.F.2 are classical information theory. The information-plane material (1.F.3–1.F.7) needs only the primer, so you can go straight there if the deep-learning debate is what interests you.

Notation for the whole subsection. X is the input, with distribution p(x); Y is the target or label; T is a representation of X, produced by an encoder p(t\mid x), a conditional distribution. A deterministic encoder T=f(X), such as a hidden layer of a network, is the special case where p(t\mid x) puts all its mass on f(x). Because T is computed from X alone (plus, possibly, independent noise), the three variables form a Markov chain Y\to X\to T, defined below.

Information-theory primer. All logarithms are natural, so information is measured in nats; 1 nat =1/\log 2\approx1.44 bits.

Entropy. For a discrete random variable X with probability mass function p(x), H(X)=-\sum_xp(x)\log p(x)=\mathbb E\big[-\log p(X)\big]. It satisfies 0\le H(X)\le\log|\mathcal X|, where \mathcal X is the set of values. It is 0 iff X is deterministic and maximal iff X is uniform. Operationally, H(X) is the minimum average number of nats per symbol needed to encode a long i.i.d. sequence of copies of X without loss (Shannon’s source-coding theorem). The entropy of a Bernoulli(q) variable is the binary entropy H_b(q)=-q\log q-(1-q)\log(1-q), which increases on [0,\tfrac12] from 0 to \log2 and is symmetric about \tfrac12.

Joint and conditional entropy. H(X,Y) is the entropy of the pair. The conditional entropy is H(Y\mid X)=-\sum_{x,y}p(x,y)\log p(y\mid x)=\sum_xp(x)\,H(Y\mid X=x), the average uncertainty left in Y once X is known. Chain rule: H(X,Y)=H(X)+H(Y\mid X). Conditioning does not increase entropy on average: H(Y\mid X)\le H(Y). If T=f(X) then H(T\mid X)=0.

Relative entropy. For distributions p,q on the same set, \mathrm{KL}(p\,\|\,q)=\sum_xp(x)\log\frac{p(x)}{q(x)}\ \ge0, with equality iff p=q (Gibbs’ inequality, which is Jensen’s inequality applied to -\log). It is not symmetric, and it is +\infty if p puts mass where q does not.

Mutual information. The information that X and Y carry about each other has several equivalent forms: I(X;Y)=\mathrm{KL}\big(p(x,y)\,\|\,p(x)p(y)\big)=H(X)-H(X\mid Y)=H(Y)-H(Y\mid X) =H(X)+H(Y)-H(X,Y). It is symmetric, nonnegative, zero iff X and Y are independent, and I(X;X)=H(X). A fifth form is often the most useful one: I(X;Y)=\sum_xp(x)\,\mathrm{KL}\big(p(y\mid x)\,\|\,p(y)\big), the average amount by which learning x moves the distribution of Y. Conditional mutual information is I(X;Y\mid Z)=H(X\mid Z)-H(X\mid Y,Z)\ge0, and the chain rule for information is I(X;Y,Z)=I(X;Z)+I(X;Y\mid Z).

Differential entropy. For a random vector X with density f on \mathbb R^d, h(X)=-\int f\log f. It is not the limit of discrete entropies: quantizing X to cubes of side \Delta gives an entropy of about h(X)-d\log\Delta, which diverges as \Delta\to0. Unlike H, h can be negative and depends on units: h(aX)=h(X)+d\log|a|, and more generally h(AX)=h(X)+\log|\det A|. It is unchanged by translation, h(X+c)=h(X). For a Gaussian, h\big(\mathcal N(\mu,\sigma^2)\big)=\tfrac12\log(2\pi e\sigma^2),\qquad h\big(\mathcal N(\mu,\Sigma)\big)=\tfrac12\log\det(2\pi e\,\Sigma). Maximum entropy: among all densities with covariance \Sigma, the Gaussian has the largest h. In one dimension, any W with \mathbb EW^2\le s has h(W)\le\tfrac12\log(2\pi e s). Conditional differential entropy h(X\mid Y) is defined as in the discrete case.

Mutual information for continuous variables. When densities exist, I(X;Y)=h(X)-h(X\mid Y)=\mathrm{KL}\big(p(x,y)\,\|\,p(x)p(y)\big). Unlike h, mutual information does not depend on units: it is unchanged by any invertible transformation of X, or of Y, separately. It equals the supremum of the discrete mutual information between quantizations of X and of Y. It can be +\infty; for example I(X;X)=\infty when X has a density.

Markov chains and data processing. Y\to X\to T means p(y,x,t)=p(y)\,p(x\mid y)\,p(t\mid x): given X, the variable T carries no further information about Y, i.e. I(Y;T\mid X)=0. This holds whenever T is computed from X alone, possibly with independent noise. The chain is reversible: T\to X\to Y holds too. The data-processing inequality says I(Y;T)\le I(Y;X): processing X cannot create information about Y. Proof: expand I(Y;X,T) by the chain rule in two orders, I(Y;X)+I(Y;T\mid X)=I(Y;T)+I(Y;X\mid T), and use I(Y;T\mid X)=0 and I(Y;X\mid T)\ge0. Applied to the reversed chain, it also gives I(T;Y)\le I(T;X). So the information-plane point \big(I(X;T),I(T;Y)\big) of any representation satisfies I(T;Y)\le\min\big(I(X;T),\,I(X;Y)\big).

For reference (books): Elements of Information Theory, 2nd ed., Cover and Thomas (2006), ch. 2; ch. 10, §10.1–10.3 and §10.8.

1.F.1. Rate–distortion warm-ups

Why. Before compressing for a task (1.F.2), compress for fidelity. The two textbook sources show exactly what an optimal lossy description keeps and what it throws away, and the general solution (parts 6–8) has the Boltzmann form that the information bottleneck inherits.

Setup. A source emits X\sim p(x). An encoder p(t\mid x) represents it by T, which takes values in a reproduction alphabet \mathcal T (often the same set as the values of X). A distortion measure d(x,t)\ge0 says how bad it is to represent x by t: Hamming distortion d(x,t)=\mathbf 1[x\ne t] for bits, squared error d(x,t)=(x-t)^2 for real numbers. The rate–distortion function is R(D)=\min_{p(t\mid x):\ \mathbb E\,d(X,T)\le D}\ I(X;T), with the expectation over p(x)\,p(t\mid x). I(X;T) is how many nats about X the representation keeps, so R(D) is the least that any representation accurate to D on average must keep.

What R(D) means (Shannon 1959; Cover and Thomas ch. 10; quoted, not proved here). Code long i.i.d. blocks X_1,\dots,X_n jointly, and ask for an average per-symbol distortion of at most D. Then any rate above R(D) nats per symbol is achievable as n\to\infty, and no rate below R(D) is. So R(D) is the minimum number of nats per symbol needed for lossy coding at distortion D. A single symbol cannot be coded at a fractional rate; the theorem is about blocks. For Hamming distortion, R(0)=H(X), the lossless limit.

Two facts you may use freely. R(D) is non-increasing, because a larger D allows more encoders. It is convex, because I(X;T) is a convex function of p(t\mid x) at fixed p(x) while \mathbb E\,d is linear in it, so mixing two encoders gives at most the mixed rate at the mixed distortion. Also, R(D)=0 once D\ge D_{\max}=\min_t\mathbb E\,d(X,t), since a constant representation achieves D_{\max}.

  1. Bernoulli source, lower bound. Let X\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p) with p\le\tfrac12, \mathcal T=\{0,1\}, and Hamming distortion. Show that every encoder with \mathbb E\,d(X,T)\le D, where D\le p, has I(X;T)\ge H_b(p)-H_b(D).
  2. Bernoulli source, achievability. Build the joint law backwards: T\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(r), Z\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(D) independent of T, and X=T\oplus Z (addition mod 2). Find the r that makes X\sim\mathrm{Bernoulli}(p) and check that it lies in [0,1] for 0\le D\le p. Show that this joint law has \mathbb E\,d=D and I(X;T)=H_b(p)-H_b(D). Conclude that R(D)=H_b(p)-H_b(D) for 0\le D\le p and R(D)=0 for D\ge p.
  3. Take p=\tfrac12 and work in bits. What are R(0) and R'(D) as D\to0, and what does the slope mean? How much rate does tolerating 1% bit errors save, compared with lossless coding?
  4. Gaussian source, lower bound. Let X\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma^2), \mathcal T=\mathbb R, and squared-error distortion. Show that every encoder with \mathbb E(X-T)^2\le D\le\sigma^2 has I(X;T)\ge\tfrac12\log(\sigma^2/D).
  5. Gaussian source, achievability. Let T\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma^2-D) and Z\sim\mathcal N(0,D) be independent, and X=T+Z. Show that this achieves the bound, so R(D)=\tfrac12\log(\sigma^2/D) for D\le\sigma^2 and 0 beyond. Find the forward encoder p(t\mid x). Invert to D(R) in bits: how much does each extra bit reduce the mean squared error?
Hint

For 1, write I(X;T)=H(X)-H(X\mid T). Given T, the variables X and X\oplus T determine each other, so H(X\mid T)=H(X\oplus T\mid T); then drop the conditioning. Note \Pr[X\oplus T=1]=\mathbb E\,d. For 4, the same argument with X-T in place of X\oplus T, plus the maximum-entropy property of the Gaussian. For 5, (X,T) is jointly Gaussian; condition T on X by the usual Gaussian formulas.

Solution

1. I(X;T)=H(X)-H(X\mid T)=H_b(p)-H(X\mid T). Given T=t, the map x\mapsto x\oplus t is a bijection, so H(X\mid T)=H(X\oplus T\mid T)\le H(X\oplus T). The variable X\oplus T is Bernoulli with parameter \Pr[X\ne T]=\mathbb E\,d\le D\le p\le\tfrac12, and H_b is increasing on [0,\tfrac12], so H(X\oplus T)=H_b(\mathbb E\,d)\le H_b(D). Hence I(X;T)\ge H_b(p)-H_b(D).

2. \Pr[X=1]=r(1-D)+(1-r)D=D+r(1-2D), so r=\dfrac{p-D}{1-2D} (for D<\tfrac12). It is \ge0 because D\le p, and \le1 because p-D\le1-2D is p+D\le1. The distortion is \Pr[X\ne T]=\Pr[Z=1]=D. Since Z is independent of T, H(X\mid T)=H(T\oplus Z\mid T)=H(Z)=H_b(D), so I(X;T)=H_b(p)-H_b(D). The encoder is the forward conditional p(t\mid x)=p(t)p(x\mid t)/p(x); only the joint law matters for I and \mathbb E\,d. Together with 1, this gives R(D)=H_b(p)-H_b(D) on [0,p]. For D\ge p, the constant T=0 has \mathbb E\,d=\Pr[X=1]=p\le D and I=0.

3. In bits, R(D)=1-H_2(D) on [0,\tfrac12], where H_2 is binary entropy in bits. R(0)=1 bit: zero distortion forces T to determine X, so I(X;T)=H(X), the lossless rate. The slope is R'(D)=-H_b'(D)=-\log\frac{1-D}{D}\to-\infty as D\to0 (in nats; divide by \log2 for bits): the first errors you tolerate save the most rate. At D=0.01, R=1-H_2(0.01)\approx1-0.081=0.919 bits, so 1% bit errors save about 8% of the rate. At D=0.1 the rate is already down to 0.531 bits.

4. I(X;T)=h(X)-h(X\mid T). Translation does not change differential entropy, so h(X\mid T)=h(X-T\mid T)\le h(X-T). The variable X-T has \mathbb E(X-T)^2\le D, so by maximum entropy h(X-T)\le\tfrac12\log(2\pi eD). With h(X)=\tfrac12\log(2\pi e\sigma^2), this gives I(X;T)\ge\tfrac12\log(\sigma^2/D).

5. X=T+Z is Gaussian with variance (\sigma^2-D)+D=\sigma^2, as required. \mathbb E(X-T)^2=\mathbb EZ^2=D, and I(X;T)=h(X)-h(X\mid T)=h(X)-h(Z)=\tfrac12\log(\sigma^2/D). With 4, this is R(D). For D\ge\sigma^2, T=0 gives distortion \sigma^2 at zero rate.

Forward encoder: \mathrm{Cov}(X,T)=\mathrm{Var}\,T=\sigma^2-D, so T\mid X=x\ \sim\ \mathcal N\Big(\big(1-\tfrac{D}{\sigma^2}\big)x,\ \ D\big(1-\tfrac{D}{\sigma^2}\big)\Big). The optimal encoder shrinks x toward zero by the factor 1-D/\sigma^2 and adds independent Gaussian noise. In bits, R=\tfrac12\log_2(\sigma^2/D), i.e. D(R)=\sigma^2\,2^{-2R}: each extra bit divides the mean squared error by 4 (about 6 dB per bit).

Note that this optimal single-symbol encoder is noisy, and its I(X;T) is finite. A noiseless invertible encoder such as T=X would have I(X;T)=\infty. The block codes that achieve R(D) in the operational sense are deterministic quantizers with finitely many codewords, so their mutual information is finite too. The question of what I(X;T) means for a deterministic map with continuous output returns from 1.F.3 on.

The Lagrangian form. Because R(D) is convex, the constrained problem can be traded for an unconstrained one: for \beta\ge0, minimize \mathcal F[p(t\mid x)]=I(X;T)+\beta\,\mathbb E\,d(X,T) over encoders, where I(X;T) is computed with the marginal p(t)=\sum_xp(x)\,p(t\mid x). Each \beta picks out one point (D_\beta,R_\beta) of the curve. Part 6 is the standard characterization of the optimal encoder (Cover and Thomas §10.7); 1.F.2.3 reuses it for the information bottleneck. Parts 7–8 interpret it and turn it into an algorithm. Part 9 is optional.

  1. Boltzmann form. Show that at a stationary point of \mathcal F, p(t\mid x)=\frac{p(t)\,e^{-\beta d(x,t)}}{Z(x,\beta)},\qquad Z(x,\beta)=\sum_tp(t)\,e^{-\beta d(x,t)} . Show also that at such a point I(X;T)+\beta\,\mathbb E\,d=-\mathbb E_X\log Z(X,\beta). Interpret d, \beta, p(t) and \log Z in statistical-mechanics terms, and describe the encoder as \beta\to0 and \beta\to\infty. Why is this not yet a closed-form solution?
  2. \beta is the slope. Let F(\beta)=R_\beta+\beta D_\beta be the minimum value of \mathcal F. Using the envelope theorem, F'(\beta)=D_\beta, show that along the curve dR/dD=-\beta. Check this against parts 2 and 5: write the test channels found there in Boltzmann form, read off \beta, and compare with -R'(D). What is the optimal encoder when \beta is below \log\frac{1-p}{p} (Bernoulli) or \frac1{2\sigma^2} (Gaussian)?
  3. Alternating minimization (Blahut–Arimoto). For any distribution q(t), show that \sum_xp(x)\,\mathrm{KL}\big(p(\cdot\mid x)\,\|\,q\big)=I(X;T)+\mathrm{KL}(p_T\,\|\,q), where p_T is the marginal of T. Conclude that minimizing \mathcal F is the same as minimizing G(p(t\mid x),q)=\sum_xp(x)\,\mathrm{KL}\big(p(\cdot\mid x)\,\|\,q\big)+\beta\,\mathbb E\,d over encoder and q jointly. Show that minimizing G over the encoder at fixed q gives p(t\mid x)\propto q(t)e^{-\beta d(x,t)}, and over q at fixed encoder gives q=p_T. Why does alternating these two steps never increase G?
  4. (Optional) Reverse water-filling. Let X=(X_1,\dots,X_k) have independent components X_i\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma_i^2), with d(x,t)=\|x-t\|^2. Show that R(D)=\sum_i\tfrac12\log\frac{\sigma_i^2}{D_i},\qquad D_i=\min(\theta,\sigma_i^2),\quad\text{with }\theta\text{ fixed by }\textstyle\sum_iD_i=D . Which components are described, and which are dropped? Why does the same answer hold for a correlated Gaussian X\sim\mathcal N(0,\Sigma), with \sigma_i^2 replaced by the eigenvalues of \Sigma? Compare the optimal encoder with principal component analysis, and with ridge regression, which multiplies the least-squares coefficient along the k-th principal direction of the inputs by s_k^2/(s_k^2+n\lambda) (here s_k^2 is the k-th eigenvalue of X^\top X for an n\times p design matrix, and \lambda the ridge penalty).
Hint

For 6, differentiate I(X;T)=\sum_{x,t}p(x)p(t\mid x)\log\frac{p(t\mid x)}{p(t)} with respect to p(t\mid x), remembering that p(t) depends on it; the terms from differentiating p(t) cancel, leaving p(x)\log\frac{p(t\mid x)}{p(t)}. Add a multiplier \lambda(x) for \sum_tp(t\mid x)=1. For 7, in the Bernoulli case p(x\mid t)=(1-D)\,e^{-\beta d(x,t)} with e^{-\beta}=D/(1-D). For 9, first show I(X;T)\ge\sum_iI(X_i;T_i) using independence of the X_i and “conditioning reduces entropy”; then apply part 4 to each component and minimize over the allocation D_i.

Solution

6. Write I(X;T)=\sum_{x,t}p(x)p(t\mid x)\big[\log p(t\mid x)-\log p(t)\big] with p(t)=\sum_{x'}p(x')p(t\mid x'). Then \frac{\partial I}{\partial p(t\mid x)}=p(x)\log\frac{p(t\mid x)}{p(t)}+p(x)-\sum_{x'}p(x')p(t\mid x')\,\frac{p(x)}{p(t)} =p(x)\log\frac{p(t\mid x)}{p(t)}, and \partial(\beta\,\mathbb E\,d)/\partial p(t\mid x)=\beta\,p(x)\,d(x,t). With a multiplier \lambda(x) for normalization, stationarity reads p(x)\big[\log\frac{p(t\mid x)}{p(t)}+\beta d(x,t)\big]+\lambda(x)=0, so p(t\mid x)=p(t)e^{-\beta d(x,t)}\times(\text{a function of }x), and normalizing gives Z(x,\beta). Substituting \log\frac{p(t\mid x)}{p(t)}=-\beta d(x,t)-\log Z(x,\beta) into I gives I(X;T)=-\beta\,\mathbb E\,d-\mathbb E\log Z(X,\beta), which is the stated identity.

For each input x, the encoder is a Boltzmann distribution over the “states” t, with energy d(x,t), inverse temperature \beta, and a prior weight p(t) playing the role of a degeneracy. -\log Z(x,\beta) is \beta times a free energy, and \mathcal F at the optimum is its average over the quenched input x. As \beta\to0 (high temperature), p(t\mid x)\to p(t): T is independent of X and the rate is zero. As \beta\to\infty (zero temperature), p(t\mid x) concentrates on \arg\min_td(x,t) among the t with p(t)>0: a deterministic nearest-neighbour quantizer. It is not a closed form because p(t) on the right is the marginal of the encoder on the left; the equation must be solved self-consistently (part 8).

7. At the minimizing encoder, changing \beta changes \mathcal F only through its explicit \beta, because the encoder is stationary; so F'(\beta)=D_\beta. Then R_\beta=F(\beta)-\beta D_\beta gives dR_\beta/d\beta=D_\beta-D_\beta-\beta\,dD_\beta/d\beta, i.e. dR/dD=-\beta. So \beta is the exchange rate between rate and distortion, in nats per unit of distortion, and the convexity of R(D) says that it falls as D grows.

Bernoulli. In part 2, p(x\mid t)=1-D if x=t and D otherwise, i.e. p(x\mid t)=(1-D)e^{-\beta d(x,t)} with \beta=\log\frac{1-D}{D}. Then p(t\mid x)=p(t)p(x\mid t)/p(x)\propto p(t)e^{-\beta d(x,t)}, the Boltzmann form. And -R'(D)=H_b'(D)=\log\frac{1-D}{D}. They agree.

Gaussian. In part 5, p(x\mid t)\propto e^{-(x-t)^2/(2D)}, so p(t\mid x)\propto p(t)e^{-\beta(x-t)^2} with \beta=\frac1{2D}, and -R'(D)=\frac1{2D}. They agree.

Low \beta. As D rises to D_{\max} (which is p or \sigma^2), the slope \beta=-R'(D) falls to \beta_c=\log\frac{1-p}{p} or \frac1{2\sigma^2}. For \beta\le\beta_c, convexity gives R'(D)+\beta\le0 for all D<D_{\max}, and R=0 beyond, so R(D)+\beta D is minimized at D=D_{\max}: the optimal encoder is a constant, T carries no information, and R_\beta=0. Below a critical inverse temperature the representation is trivial, a phase transition. For a fair coin, \beta_c=0.

8. \sum_xp(x)\sum_tp(t\mid x)\log\frac{p(t\mid x)}{q(t)} =\sum_{x,t}p(x,t)\Big[\log\frac{p(t\mid x)}{p(t)}+\log\frac{p(t)}{q(t)}\Big] =I(X;T)+\mathrm{KL}(p_T\,\|\,q). This is at least I(X;T), with equality iff q=p_T. So \min_{\text{enc}}\mathcal F=\min_{\text{enc}}\min_qG.

At fixed q, G splits over x, and for each x we minimize \sum_tp(t\mid x)\big[\log\frac{p(t\mid x)}{q(t)}+\beta d(x,t)\big] =\mathrm{KL}\big(p(\cdot\mid x)\,\big\|\,q\,e^{-\beta d(x,\cdot)}/Z_q(x)\big)-\log Z_q(x), with Z_q(x)=\sum_tq(t)e^{-\beta d(x,t)}. This is smallest when the KL term vanishes, at p(t\mid x)=q(t)e^{-\beta d(x,t)}/Z_q(x) (the Gibbs variational principle). At fixed encoder, only \mathrm{KL}(p_T\|q) depends on q, so q=p_T. Each step minimizes G exactly over one argument, so G cannot increase, and it is bounded below by \min\mathcal F. That the iteration converges to the global minimum uses the joint convexity of G in (encoder, q) (Csiszár and Tusnády 1984; quoted). This is the Blahut–Arimoto algorithm (Blahut 1972; Arimoto 1972 for the channel-capacity version; Cover and Thomas §10.8). In practice: put t on a grid, start from a uniform q, and iterate. checks/wb2_new_ratedistortion_check.py does this and recovers H_b(p)-H_b(D) to machine precision and \tfrac12\log(\sigma^2/D) on a grid to better than 10^{-3} relative error, with \beta=\log\frac{1-D}{D} and \beta=\frac1{2D} respectively.

9. Lower bound. With independent components, h(X)=\sum_ih(X_i), and by the chain rule and “conditioning reduces entropy”, h(X\mid T)=\sum_ih(X_i\mid T,X_1,\dots,X_{i-1})\le\sum_ih(X_i\mid T_i). So I(X;T)\ge\sum_iI(X_i;T_i). By part 4, I(X_i;T_i)\ge\tfrac12\log^+(\sigma_i^2/D_i) where D_i=\mathbb E(X_i-T_i)^2 and \log^+u=\max(\log u,0). Hence R(D) is at least the minimum of \sum_i\tfrac12\log^+(\sigma_i^2/D_i) subject to \sum_iD_i\le D.

Allocation. Taking D_i>\sigma_i^2 wastes distortion budget, so take D_i\le\sigma_i^2. Minimize \sum_i\tfrac12\log(\sigma_i^2/D_i)+\mu\big(\sum_iD_i-D\big): for each i with D_i<\sigma_i^2, -\frac1{2D_i}+\mu=0, so all such D_i equal a common level \theta=\frac1{2\mu}. Components with \sigma_i^2\le\theta sit at their bound D_i=\sigma_i^2. So D_i=\min(\theta,\sigma_i^2).

Achievability. Use the part-5 test channel independently on each component with \sigma_i^2>\theta, at distortion \theta, and set T_i=0 for the rest. Then I(X;T)=\sum_iI(X_i;T_i), and the bound is met.

Picture. Think of \theta as a water level. Components whose variance is below it are not described at all (T_i=0, error \sigma_i^2). Components above it are all described to the same error \theta, at rate \tfrac12\log(\sigma_i^2/\theta), with forward encoder T_i=(1-\theta/\sigma_i^2)X_i+\text{noise}. For example, with \sigma^2=(4,2,1,0.3) and D=1.8, the level is \theta=0.5, the fourth component is dropped, and R=\tfrac12\log(8\cdot4\cdot2)=3\log2 nats.

Correlated Gaussian. Rotate to the eigenbasis, X'=U^\top X with \Sigma=U\Lambda U^\top, and use T'=U^\top T. Mutual information is invariant under invertible maps of each variable, and squared error is invariant under the same rotation of x and t, so the problem becomes the independent one with variances \Lambda. The optimal encoder keeps the principal components with eigenvalue above \theta, shrinks each by 1-\theta/\lambda_k and adds noise, and discards the rest: PCA truncation at a level set by the distortion budget.

Comparison with ridge. Both are filters on principal directions that favour high-variance directions, and both have a level below which directions are suppressed: n\lambda for ridge, \theta here. Ridge’s filter s_k^2/(s_k^2+n\lambda)=1/(1+n\lambda/s_k^2) is smooth and never exactly zero. The rate–distortion filter \max(0,1-\theta/\lambda_k) is exactly zero below the level. Far above their levels the two agree to first order: 1-n\lambda/s_k^2 against 1-\theta/\lambda_k. The analogy is loose: ridge filters a regression coefficient using the input covariance, while rate–distortion filters the source itself. What the two share is that low-variance directions are where a fixed budget (of information, or of signal-to-noise ratio) buys the least.

1.F.2. The IB is rate–distortion with a learned distortion

Why. Rate–distortion (1.F.1) compresses a source X into a representation T subject to a distortion measure d(x,t) chosen in advance. The information bottleneck (IB) of Tishby, Pereira and Bialek (1999) replaces the distortion by a task: keep whatever information about X is relevant to a second variable Y (a label), and discard the rest. Given the joint law p(x,y), it seeks the encoder p(t\mid x) solving

\min_{p(t\mid x)}\ I(X;T)-\beta\,I(T;Y),\qquad \beta>0,

where T is generated from X alone, so Y\to X\to T is a Markov chain. Small \beta favours compression; large \beta favours relevance. This exercise shows the IB is a rate–distortion problem whose distortion is a KL divergence between predictions of Y, and that its optimal encoder has the same Boltzmann form as in 1.F.1.6.

  1. Define p(y\mid t)=\sum_xp(y\mid x)\,p(x\mid t) and d(x,t)=\mathrm{KL}\big(p(y\mid x)\,\|\,p(y\mid t)\big). Show that \mathbb E\,d(X,T)=I(X;Y)-I(T;Y), so the IB Lagrangian is the rate–distortion Lagrangian I(X;T)+\beta\,\mathbb E\,d(X,T) of 1.F.1 up to a constant.
  2. Why is this not an ordinary rate–distortion problem?
  3. Show that at a stationary point, p(t\mid x)=\dfrac{p(t)}{Z(x,\beta)}\exp\!\big(-\beta\,\mathrm{KL}(p(y\mid x)\,\|\,p(y\mid t))\big), i.e. 1.F.1.6 with d=\mathrm{KL}. Because d now depends on the encoder, this needs one extra check: that the encoder-dependence of d contributes nothing to the derivative of \mathbb E\,d.
Hint

For 3, differentiate \sum_{x',t'}p(x')p(t'\mid x')\,d(x',t') with respect to p(t\mid x). Differentiating the weight p(t'\mid x') gives p(x)\,d(x,t), as in 1.F.1.6. In the remaining term only \log p(y\mid t) depends on the encoder; collect it as -\sum_yp(t,y)\,\partial\log p(y\mid t) and use \sum_yp(y\mid t)=1.

Solution

1. With Y\to X\to T, the joint is p(x)p(y\mid x)p(t\mid x). Then \mathbb E\,d=\sum_{x,t,y}p(x,t)\,p(y\mid x)\log\frac{p(y\mid x)}{p(y\mid t)} =-H(Y\mid X)+H(Y\mid T)=I(X;Y)-I(T;Y), using p(x,t)p(y\mid x)=p(x,y,t), so that p(y\mid t) as defined is the true conditional. Hence I(X;T)-\beta I(T;Y)=I(X;T)+\beta\,\mathbb E\,d-\beta I(X;Y), and the last term does not depend on the encoder.

2. The distortion depends on the encoder itself, through p(y\mid t). In rate–distortion theory d is fixed in advance. So the IB equations must be solved self-consistently: alternate the Boltzmann update for p(t\mid x) with recomputing p(t) and p(y\mid t), as in Blahut–Arimoto (1.F.1.8). Unlike there, the IB objective is not convex in the encoder, so the iteration finds a local optimum.

3. Write \mathbb E\,d=\sum_{x',t'}p(x')p(t'\mid x')\,d(x',t'). Differentiating the explicit weight p(t\mid x) gives p(x)\,d(x,t). The rest is \sum_{x'}p(x')p(t\mid x')\sum_yp(y\mid x')\Big(-\frac{\partial\log p(y\mid t)}{\partial p(t\mid x)}\Big) =-\sum_yp(t,y)\,\frac{\partial\log p(y\mid t)}{\partial p(t\mid x)} =-p(t)\,\frac{\partial}{\partial p(t\mid x)}\sum_yp(y\mid t)=0, because \sum_yp(y\mid t)=1 for every encoder (only the t'=t terms depend on p(t\mid x) through p(y\mid t')). So \partial\,\mathbb E\,d/\partial p(t\mid x)=p(x)\,d(x,t), exactly as if d were fixed, and the stationarity condition of 1.F.1.6, p(x)\big[\log\frac{p(t\mid x)}{p(t)}+\beta d(x,t)\big]+\lambda(x)=0, gives the stated form with Z(x,\beta) the normalizer.

At large \beta, inputs x are sent to the representations t whose prediction p(y\mid t) is closest to their own p(y\mid x): the IB clusters inputs by what they say about Y.

The information plane: did deep networks compress?

Why this is here. Do trained networks actually compress in the IB sense? Shwartz-Ziv and Tishby (2017) said yes, and that this is why they generalize. It was one of the most discussed theories of deep learning, and the debate that followed is a compact lesson in what mutual information can and cannot measure about a network. The exercises 1.F.3–1.F.6 derive the three facts the debate turned on; 1.F.7 has the papers. Entropy, mutual information and the data-processing inequality are defined in the primer at the start of 1.F.

Setting. X is the network input, Y the label, and T=f(X) the activations of one hidden layer, a deterministic function of X; T is random only because X is. Because T depends on Y only through X, the variables form a Markov chain Y\to X\to T, so I(T;Y)\le I(X;Y). The information plane plots each layer as the point \big(I(X;T),\,I(T;Y)\big) and follows it through training. In IB terms (1.F.2), moving left means compressing and moving up means keeping label information.

Tishby’s claim (Shwartz-Ziv and Tishby 2017). Training has two phases. In a short fitting phase both coordinates rise. In a long compression phase I(X;T) falls while I(T;Y) holds. The compression is driven by the noise-dominated, diffusive part of SGD, and it is why networks generalize. Their estimator binned the activations of a \tanh network into 30 equal bins on [-1,1], with X uniform over 4096 inputs.

The critique (Saxe et al. 2018). Compression appears with double-sided saturating units like \tanh, and not with ReLU or linear units. Networks that do not compress still generalize. The same curves appear under full-batch gradient descent, so SGD noise is not their cause. There is also a conceptual problem, which 1.F.3 makes precise: for a deterministic network the quantity being plotted is constant or infinite.

A third reading (Goldfeld et al. 2019). Add a little Gaussian noise to each layer, so that I(X;T) is finite and depends on the weights. Then compression does occur, and it is driven by the clustering of same-class representations. The binned estimates track the same clustering.

What the exercises show. For a deterministic network, I(X;T) is fixed by the input distribution as long as the layer is injective on a discrete input set, and infinite if the activations are continuously distributed (1.F.3), so it cannot “fall”. What a binned estimate measures instead is the entropy of a quantization of T: saturating \tanh units make it rise and then fall, while for ReLU units it grows or stays constant depending on how the bins are chosen (1.F.4). Once Gaussian noise is added to the layer, I(X;T) is finite and counts distinguishable cells at the noise scale (1.F.5). In a two-class model, at fixed label information I(T;Y), it falls exactly when the within-class spread, measured in units of the noise, shrinks (1.F.6).

1.F.3. A deterministic network has nothing to compress

Why. The information-plane plots show I(X;T) falling during training. Before asking why, ask what I(X;T) is when T is a deterministic function of X, as it is in an ordinary network.

  1. Let X be discrete and T=f(X) for a deterministic f. Show that I(X;T)=H(T), and that I(X;T)=H(X) if f is injective on the support of X.
  2. In Shwartz-Ziv and Tishby’s setup, X is uniform over 4096 inputs. What is I(X;T) for any layer that maps distinct inputs to distinct activation vectors? With 32-bit floats, can two distinct inputs still be mapped to exactly the same activation vector, and what would cause it?
  3. Now let X have a density on \mathbb R^d, and suppose T=f(X) has an atomless distribution, i.e. \Pr[T=t]=0 for every t (true, for example, if f is smooth and injective). Show that I(X;T)=\infty.
  4. Conclude: which changes to the weights can change I(X;T) in cases 1 and 3?
Hint

For 3, let q_\Delta quantize T to cubes of side \Delta. Since q_\Delta(T) is a function of X, part 1 and data processing give I(X;T)\ge I(X;q_\Delta(T))=H(q_\Delta(T)). Then use H(Q)\ge-\log\max_k\Pr[Q=k].

Solution

1. I(X;T)=H(T)-H(T\mid X), and H(T\mid X)=0 because T is determined by X. So I(X;T)=H(T)=H(f(X)). If f is injective on the support, f(X) is a relabelling of X, so H(f(X))=H(X).

2. H(X)=\log4096=12 bits \approx8.32 nats, for every such layer, at every step of training, as long as the layer stays injective on the 4096 inputs. Exact collisions are possible but need a mechanism: units that are exactly zero on whole regions of input (dead ReLUs), or saturated \tanh units that round to exactly \pm1 in floating point, can map distinct inputs to the same vector. Without such collisions, nothing in I(X;T) changes, whatever a binned estimate does.

3. Let q_\Delta map T to the cube of side \Delta that contains it. q_\Delta(T) is a deterministic function of X, so by 1, I(X;q_\Delta(T))=H(q_\Delta(T)). Since X\to T\to q_\Delta(T), data processing gives I(X;T)\ge H(q_\Delta(T))\ge-\log\max_k\Pr[q_\Delta(T)=k].

It remains to show that m(\Delta):=\sup_{\text{cubes }Q\text{ of side }\Delta}\Pr[T\in Q]\to0. Suppose not: then there are \epsilon>0 and cubes Q_j with sides \Delta_j\to0 and \Pr[T\in Q_j]\ge\epsilon. By tightness, the Q_j eventually lie in a compact set, so a subsequence of their centres converges to some t_0. Every ball around t_0 then has probability at least \epsilon, so \Pr[T=t_0]\ge\epsilon, contradicting atomlessness. Hence H(q_\Delta(T))\to\infty and I(X;T)=\infty.

(Equivalently, when T has a density, h(T\mid X)=-\infty because T given X is a point mass.)

4. In case 3, none: I(X;T)=\infty for every weight setting that keeps T atomless. In case 1, only exact collisions, where two distinct inputs are mapped to exactly the same activation vector, can lower H(f(X)). Such collisions are possible, for example with dead ReLUs. The constancy conclusion requires injectivity on the discrete input support; the infinite-information conclusion requires atomlessness. Under those conditions, movement in a finite plotted estimate does not describe a change in the exact mutual information.

1.F.4. What the binned estimator measures: saturation looks like compression

Why. If the true I(X;T) cannot fall, something else produced the published curves. The estimator is the place to look.

The binned estimator replaces T by \hat T=\mathrm{bin}(T), its bin index, and reports I(X;\hat T). Since \hat T is a function of X, 1.F.3.1 says this is just H(\mathrm{bin}(T)), the entropy of a quantization.

Take one unit with standard normal pre-activation Z and pre-activation scale a>0, the weight norm. Training typically grows a.

  1. T=\tanh(aZ), binned into K equal bins on [-1,1], with K even. Show that H(\mathrm{bin}(T))\to\log2 as a\to\infty. What is the limit as a\to0? How does it change if K is odd?
  2. Sketch H(\mathrm{bin}(T)) as a function of a for K=30. Explain why steadily growing weights alone produce a “fitting phase” followed by a “compression phase”.
  3. T=\mathrm{ReLU}(aZ)=\max(0,aZ), binned with a fixed width \Delta: [0,\Delta),[\Delta,2\Delta),\dots. Show that as a\to\infty, H(\mathrm{bin}(T))=\log2+\tfrac12\Big(h(|Z|)+\log\frac a\Delta\Big)+o(1),\qquad h(|Z|)=\tfrac12\log\frac{\pi e}2 . So the estimate grows without bound, like \tfrac12\log a.
  4. Many implementations instead bin ReLU activations into K equal bins on [0,\max T], the observed range. What happens to the estimate as a grows?
  5. In one sentence: what quantity is the binned estimator measuring?
Hint

For 1, the bin edges are e_j=-1+2j/K; compute \Pr[T\ge1-2/K]. For 3, condition on the sign of Z, and use the standard fact that for a nice density, H(\mathrm{bin}_\delta(W))=h(W)-\log\delta+o(1) as \delta\to0. Here the effective bin width for Z is \delta=\Delta/a. For 4, ReLU is positively homogeneous.

Solution

1. The top bin is [1-2/K,1], and \Pr[T\ge1-2/K]=\Pr\big[Z\ge\operatorname{artanh}(1-2/K)/a\big]\to\Pr[Z>0]=\tfrac12 as a\to\infty. The bottom bin likewise tends to \tfrac12, so every other bin tends to 0. Entropy is continuous on the finite simplex, so H\to\log2: the unit has become a sign function, and the estimate counts two cells.

As a\to0, T\approx aZ concentrates at 0. With K even, 0 is a bin edge, so the two bins adjacent to 0 each get mass \to\tfrac12 and H\to\log2 again. With K odd, the middle bin straddles 0, takes all the mass, and H\to0.

2. For K=30, exact bin probabilities give (from checks/wb2b_check.py):

a 0.1 0.5 0.9 2 5 20 100 10^4
H(\mathrm{bin}\tanh(aZ)) (nats) 1.83 3.21 3.40 (max) 2.88 1.92 1.10 0.80 0.69

At small a the activations occupy a few central bins. As a grows they spread over more bins, so the estimate rises (“fitting”). Past a\approx1 they pile up in the two saturated end bins, so it falls toward \log2 (“compression”). Monotone weight growth produces the two-phase curve with no change in what the network distinguishes: T stays an injective function of Z throughout.

3. Let S=\mathbf 1[Z>0]. All mass with Z\le0 sits at T=0, in bin 0, together with \Pr[0<Z<\Delta/a]\to0. Up to that vanishing overlap, S is a function of the bin, so H(\mathrm{bin}\,T)=H(S)+\Pr[S=1]\,H(\mathrm{bin}\,T\mid S=1)+o(1) =\log2+\tfrac12H\big(\mathrm{bin}_{\Delta/a}(|Z|)\big)+o(1). Given Z>0, Z has the density of |Z|, and binning aZ at width \Delta is binning Z at width \delta=\Delta/a\to0. So H(\mathrm{bin}_\delta|Z|)=h(|Z|)-\log\delta+o(1). The half-normal has h(|Z|)=\tfrac12\log(2\pi e)-\log2=\tfrac12\log(\pi e/2), which gives the formula. Check: with \Delta=1/15 and a=100 the formula gives 4.713 nats and the exact value is 4.710; at a=1000, 5.864 against 5.864. The ReLU estimate keeps growing, with no compression phase. This is the pattern Saxe et al. report.

4. \max(0,aZ)/\max_i(0,aZ_i) does not depend on a, so the binned estimate is exactly constant in a. The answer to 3 versus 4 is decided by a choice of bins, not by the network.

5. It measures how many cells of size \Delta the data occupy, i.e. the spread of the representation measured at a resolution fixed by the analyst. That is a geometric quantity. For a fixed binning rule it is a property of the distribution of T, but it is not generally equal to the unquantized I(X;T). The distribution and geometry of T can change even while the latter stays constant.

1.F.5. With noise, mutual information is finite: the Gaussian channel

Why. 1.F.3 showed that noise-free I(X;T) is degenerate. Goldfeld et al. make it meaningful by adding noise to the layer: T=f(X)+\sigma\varepsilon with \varepsilon\sim\mathcal N(0,I_d) independent of X. Now h(T\mid X)=h(\sigma\varepsilon)=\frac d2\log(2\pi e\sigma^2) is finite, and I(X;T)=h(T)-\frac d2\log(2\pi e\sigma^2) depends on f. Differential entropy and the Gaussian channel are in Cover and Thomas, Elements of Information Theory, ch. 8–9.

  1. Suppose f(X)\sim\mathcal N(\mu,S). Show that I(X;T)=\tfrac12\log\det(I+S/\sigma^2)=\sum_k\tfrac12\log(1+s_k/\sigma^2), where s_k are the eigenvalues of S.
  2. Show that for any f(X) with covariance S, I(X;T)\le\tfrac12\log\det(I+S/\sigma^2).
  3. Interpret \tfrac12\log(1+s_k/\sigma^2) in terms of the number of distinguishable cells of size \sigma along the k-th principal direction. Compare with 1.F.4.5.
Hint

h(T\mid X)=h(\sigma\varepsilon) is the same for every f. For 2, among all densities with a given covariance, the Gaussian has the largest differential entropy.

Solution

1. T\sim\mathcal N(\mu,S+\sigma^2I), so h(T)=\tfrac12\log\det\big(2\pi e(S+\sigma^2I)\big) and h(T\mid X)=\tfrac12\log\det(2\pi e\sigma^2I). Subtracting gives \tfrac12\log\det(I+S/\sigma^2). Diagonalizing S gives the sum.

2. h(T\mid X) is the same for every f. T has covariance S+\sigma^2I, and the Gaussian maximizes differential entropy at fixed covariance, so h(T)\le\tfrac12\log\det(2\pi e(S+\sigma^2I)).

3. \tfrac12\log(1+s_k/\sigma^2)\approx\log(\sqrt{s_k}/\sigma) when s_k\gg\sigma^2. This is the log of the number of noise-width cells that the signal’s spread covers in that direction. The noisy mutual information counts cells of size \sigma, exactly as the binned estimate counts cells of size \Delta. In both, the analyst’s choice of scale is what makes the number finite.

1.F.6. With noise, compression is clustering: a two-class model

Why. Once I(X;T) is finite, what does a decrease in it mean geometrically? In the simplest model with a label, it means that same-class representations cluster, and the IB objective of 1.F.2 becomes an explicit penalty on within-class spread.

Let Y\in\{\pm1\} be uniform, and let X=(Y,U) with U\sim\mathcal N(0,I_d) independent of Y, so the label is a function of the input. The layer computes f(X)=Y\,m+W^{1/2}U , so m\in\mathbb R^d sets the class means \pm m (between-class covariance mm^\top) and W is the within-class covariance. Then T=f(X)+\sigma\varepsilon.

  1. Show that I(X;T)=I(T;Y)+I(X;T\mid Y), and that I(X;T\mid Y)=\tfrac12\log\det(I+W/\sigma^2).
  2. Let \Sigma_T=W+\sigma^2I and \rho^2=m^\top\Sigma_T^{-1}m (the squared separation of the class means, measured in the metric of the within-class-plus-noise covariance). Show that I(T;Y) depends on the model only through \rho^2, and that I(T;Y)=\log2-\mathbb E\log\!\big(1+e^{-L}\big),\qquad L\sim\mathcal N(2\rho^2,\,4\rho^2). Show that this increases with \rho^2 and tends to \log2.
  3. Now shrink the within-class covariance, W\to\alpha W with \alpha\downarrow0, keeping m and \sigma fixed. Describe the path of the point \big(I(X;T),I(T;Y)\big). What are its limits?
  4. What happens to I(X;T) if you rescale the whole representation, f\to cf, and leave \sigma fixed? What does this say about the role of \sigma?
  5. Back to the IB. Write the IB objective of 1.F.2, I(X;T)-\beta I(T;Y) with \beta>1, for this model in terms of W and I(T;Y). Seen as a regularizer on the layer, what does it penalize and what does it reward?
Hint

For 1, use the chain rule on I(X,Y;T) and the fact that Y is a function of X. Given Y, f(X) is Gaussian, so 1.F.5.1 applies. For 2, the posterior log-odds L=\log\frac{p(T\mid Y=+1)}{p(T\mid Y=-1)} is linear in T. For monotonicity, show that a channel with smaller \rho^2 is the channel with larger \rho^2 followed by extra noise.

Solution

1. Y is a function of X, so I(X;T)=I(X,Y;T)=I(Y;T)+I(X;T\mid Y) by the chain rule. Given Y=y, f(X)=ym+W^{1/2}U\sim\mathcal N(ym,W), so by 1.F.5.1, I(X;T\mid Y=y)=\tfrac12\log\det(I+W/\sigma^2) for both values of y.

2. Given Y=y, T\sim\mathcal N(ym,\Sigma_T). The log-likelihood ratio is L=\log\frac{p(T\mid+)}{p(T\mid-)}=2m^\top\Sigma_T^{-1}T. Given Y=+1, L has mean 2m^\top\Sigma_T^{-1}m=2\rho^2 and variance 4m^\top\Sigma_T^{-1}\Sigma_T\Sigma_T^{-1}m=4\rho^2. The posterior is \Pr[Y=+1\mid T]=1/(1+e^{-L}), so given Y=+1, -\log\Pr[Y\mid T]=\log(1+e^{-L}), and the case Y=-1 is the mirror image. Hence I(T;Y)=H(Y)-H(Y\mid T)=\log2-\mathbb E\log(1+e^{-L}), which depends only on \rho^2. (Since L is a sufficient statistic, I(T;Y)=I(L;Y).)

Monotonicity: L/(2\rho)=Y\rho+G with G\sim\mathcal N(0,1). For \rho'<\rho, Y\rho'+G'\overset{\text{law}}{=}\frac{\rho'}{\rho}(Y\rho+G)+\sqrt{1-\rho'^2/\rho^2}\,G'', so the \rho' channel is the \rho channel followed by independent noise. By data processing, I is non-decreasing in \rho. As \rho\to\infty, L\to+\infty given Y=+1, so I\to\log2=H(Y).

3. With W\to\alpha W: I(X;T\mid Y)=\tfrac12\log\det(I+\alpha W/\sigma^2)\downarrow0, while \rho^2=m^\top(\alpha W+\sigma^2I)^{-1}m\uparrow\|m\|^2/\sigma^2, so I(T;Y) is nondecreasing. The point moves left and up (weakly: a coordinate can stay fixed in degenerate cases such as W=0). To justify the leftward movement, for \alpha_2>\alpha_1 the marginal law of T_{\alpha_2} is that of T_{\alpha_1} plus independent Gaussian noise with covariance (\alpha_2-\alpha_1)W. Adding independent noise cannot decrease differential entropy here, while h(T\mid X) stays fixed. Thus I(X;T) is nondecreasing in \alpha. In the limit I(X;T)=I(T;Y): the layer carries no information about X beyond what it carries about Y. A numerical example from checks/wb2b_check.py, with m=e_1, W=\mathrm{diag}(0.6,1,1), \sigma=0.5 (nats; Monte Carlo agrees with the formulas to \le0.004):

\alpha 1 0.3 0.1 0.01
I(X;T) 2.59 1.59 1.04 0.68
I(T;Y) 0.37 0.53 0.60 0.63

In this model, compression in the noisy-network sense is within-class clustering. The conditional information I(X;T\mid Y)=\tfrac12\log\det(I+W/\sigma^2) measures within-class spread in units of \sigma. The drop in total I(X;T) is the drop in this conditional term minus the gain in label information I(T;Y); it is not equal to the conditional term alone.

4. f\to cf multiplies W by c^2 and m by c, which is the same as \sigma\to\sigma/c. I(X;T) grows without bound as c\to\infty if W has a positive eigenvalue. If W=0, the signal contains only the binary label and the information is bounded by \log2 (approaching it if m\ne0). The noisy mutual information is therefore not a property of the representation’s shape alone but of its size relative to \sigma, the scale the analyst injected. Read with 1.F.4, both estimators measure geometry at a chosen resolution. Whether that makes mutual information the wrong quantity, or the right one at a stated scale, is not settled by these calculations.

5. By part 1, I(X;T)=I(T;Y)+\tfrac12\log\det(I+W/\sigma^2), so I(X;T)-\beta I(T;Y)=\tfrac12\log\det(I+W/\sigma^2)-(\beta-1)\,I(T;Y). The IB objective penalizes within-class spread in units of \sigma and rewards label information, which by part 2 means a large separation \rho^2=m^\top(W+\sigma^2I)^{-1}m between the class means. Minimizing it pushes toward tight, well-separated class clusters: a clustering pressure, once more a geometric statement whose scale is set by \sigma (part 4). (For \beta\le1 the objective is minimized by W=0, m=0: the trivial representation, as at low \beta in 1.F.1.7.)

1.F.7. Readings, and what the derivations settle

Opening the black box of deep neural networks via information, Ravid Shwartz-Ziv, Naftali Tishby (arXiv, 2017) Importance ●●●●○ Length ●●○○○

The fitting-then-compression account summarized above, with its information-plane figures; it also argues that compression explains the benefit of depth. Read the IB setup and the description of the binning estimator (30 bins, 4096 inputs), then the information-plane figures and the drift-then-diffusion account of SGD. Read it against 1.F.3 and 1.F.4: at each figure, ask what the plotted quantity is for a deterministic network.

On the information bottleneck theory of deep learning, Andrew M. Saxe, Yamini Bansal, Joel Dapello, Madhu Advani, Artemy Kolchinsky, Brendan D. Tracey, David D. Cox (ICLR 2018) Importance ●●●●○ Length ●●●○○

The critique summarized above, with experiments comparing tanh, ReLU and linear networks and full-batch versus stochastic training. Read the introduction and the sections on the nonlinearity (the binning analysis is the one you did in 1.F.4), on compression and generalization, and on batch vs stochastic training. The linear-network analysis, where task-irrelevant information can be compressed while fitting, is optional.

Estimating information flow in deep neural networks, Ziv Goldfeld, Ewout van den Berg, Kristjan Greenewald, Igor Melnyk, Nam Nguyen, Brian Kingsbury, Yury Polyanskiy (ICML 2019) Importance ●●●○○ Length ●●○○○

The noisy-network reading summarized above, with a rigorous estimator for I(X;T) in noisy networks and small worked examples of compression as clustering. Read the introduction and the setup for why deterministic I(X;T) is degenerate and how noise fixes it (1.F.3 and 1.F.5), then the section relating compression to clustering, with its small worked examples. The estimator’s convergence theory can be skipped.

Deep variational information bottleneck, Alexander A. Alemi, Ian Fischer, Joshua V. Dillon, Kevin Murphy (ICLR 2017) Importance ●●○○○ Length ●●○○○

Turns the IB objective of 1.F.2 into a trainable loss for a network with a stochastic (Gaussian) encoder, using variational bounds on I(X;T) and I(T;Y), and reports better generalization and adversarial robustness than deterministic networks with other regularizers. Read §3 for the bounds and §4.1 for the MNIST results; the encoder’s noise plays the role of σ in 1.F.5–1.F.6.

Question. The three accounts of the information plane amount to three positions: (i) compression in mutual information is real and causes generalization; (ii) it is an artefact of saturation and binning, not needed for generalization; (iii) it is real within-class clustering, which binned estimates also track. What does each of 1.F.3, 1.F.4 and 1.F.5–1.F.6 establish or rule out about these positions? Which question does none of them address, and what kind of experiment would?

Solution
  • 1.F.3 rules out the literal reading of (i) for deterministic networks: noise-free I(X;T) is constant while the layer stays injective on a discrete input set (it can fall only through exact collisions), and infinite when the representation is atomless. So “compression in mutual information” must mean compression of some estimate, or of a noisy surrogate.
  • 1.F.4 supports (ii)’s mechanism. Saturation alone produces rise-then-fall in the tanh estimate, and the ReLU estimate grows or stays constant depending on the bins. But it also supports (iii): the estimate is a spread-at-resolution-\Delta statistic, which is geometric, so “artefact” and “measures geometry” are both true.
  • 1.F.5–1.F.6 show that once noise defines a scale, the drop in I(X;T) at fixed label information is exactly within-class shrinkage. That is (iii)’s claim, made exact in a toy model (1.F.6.1). Whether mutual information is the right name for it is now a question about invariances (1.F.6.4). Noise-free mutual information is unchanged by any invertible map of T; a geometric statistic, such as the ratio of within-class to between-class scatter, is not. Two representations with equal estimated I(X;T) and I(T;Y) but different geometry would separate the two.
  • None of 1.F.3–1.F.6 bears on causation (does clustering cause generalization?). That needs interventions: for example, penalize any decrease in within-class spread and see whether test loss suffers. Two observational checks are already in Saxe et al.: ReLU networks generalize without compressing in the binned estimate, and the compression phase also appears under full-batch training, which rules out SGD noise as its cause.

2. Doesn’t some theorem make this impossible? (天无绝人之路)

Three classical theorems seem to say that learning from data should be impossible, or at least unexplained. No free lunch says every learner is at chance on the inputs it has not seen. Universal approximation says networks can represent anything, so why do they learn this function rather than another? Capacity bounds say generalization is guaranteed when the model class is small, and the classes deep networks reach are enormous. Each theorem is true, and none of them bites. This section proves each one in a simple setting and finds the assumption that does the work. Then it reads the experiment that made class-wide bounds untenable for deep learning (Zhang et al.), and introduces PAC-Bayes, which bounds the error of the particular function training found rather than the worst function in the class. 天无绝人之路, “heaven never closes every road”: the way out is a bet on structure in the targets, made implicitly by the architecture and the training procedure.

Notation. A hypothesis h is a predictor, and a hypothesis class \mathcal H is the set a learner chooses from. Examples z=(x,y) are drawn i.i.d. from an unknown distribution, and S=(z_1,\dots,z_n) is the training sample. For a loss \ell(h;z), the population risk is R(h)=\mathbb E_z\,\ell(h;z), the expected loss on a fresh example, and the training risk is \hat R_n(h)=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\ell(h;z_i). For classification the loss is the 0–1 loss \mathbf 1[h(x)\ne y], so R(h) is the error probability.

What a learning guarantee looks like. Most results here have the PAC (“probably approximately correct”) form: with probability at least 1-\delta over the sample S, the learned predictor \hat h has R(\hat h)\le\epsilon(n,\delta), or has a generalization gap R(\hat h)-\hat R_n(\hat h)\le\epsilon(n,\delta). The sample complexity is the n needed to make \epsilon small. A bound is vacuous if \epsilon exceeds the largest possible loss, so that it says nothing.

2.A. No free lunch

The question. Can any learning rule be better than another “in general”? No free lunch says no: averaged over all possible targets, every learner does equally badly on unseen inputs. The exercise proves it, finds the assumption that does the work (a uniform prior over targets), and shows that a bet on simple targets (an Occam bound) escapes it.

Setup. Let the input domain \mathcal X be finite, with |\mathcal X|=N, and the target a binary function f:\mathcal X\to\{0,1\}. A training set consists of inputs x_1,\dots,x_n, chosen independently of f, with labels f(x_i). Assume at least one input remains unseen. A learner is any map, possibly randomized, from the labelled sample to a hypothesis h:\mathcal X\to\{0,1\}. With U the set of unseen inputs, the off-training-set error is \mathrm{err}_{\mathrm{OTS}}(h,f)=\frac1{|U|}\sum_{x\in U}\mathbf 1[h(x)\ne f(x)].

Sources: off-training-set NFL is from Wolpert (1996). Shalev-Shwartz and Ben-David’s Understanding Machine Learning (free PDF; “UML” below) proves a PAC version of NFL in §5.1, the finite-class bound of part 3 in §2.3.1, and the description-length (Occam) version in §7.3.

2.A.1. Prove NFL, then find the bet
  1. Let f be uniform over all 2^N functions. Prove that every learner has expected off-training-set error exactly \tfrac12, conditional on any training inputs.
  2. Conclude that for any two learners A and B, \sum_f\big[\mathrm{err}_{\mathrm{OTS}}(A,f)-\mathrm{err}_{\mathrm{OTS}}(B,f)\big]=0. What does this say about a learner that beats another on some targets?
  3. Now let f lie in a class \mathcal F with |\mathcal F|=M, and let the inputs be i.i.d. from a distribution D on \mathcal X. Show that a learner returning any h\in\mathcal F consistent with the sample (agreeing with f on every training input) satisfies \Pr[\mathrm{err}_D(h)>\epsilon]\le Me^{-n\epsilon}, where \mathrm{err}_D is the error under D. How many samples suffice if \mathcal F is the set of functions computed by programs of at most b bits? What does the bound give for the class of all functions?
