  4. Where in the proof of 1 did the uniform prior enter? Give priors over targets under which the training labels are informative about the unseen ones, and say which kind of learner each favours.
Hint

For 1, condition on the training labels. Under the uniform prior, the values f(x) are independent fair coins, so what is the conditional law of f on U? For 3, a fixed h with error above \epsilon survives n i.i.d. samples with probability at most (1-\epsilon)^n. Then take a union bound.

Solution

1. Under the uniform prior, the values \{f(x)\}_{x\in\mathcal X} are i.i.d. fair coins. Condition on the training inputs and their labels. The values \{f(x)\}_{x\in U} are still i.i.d. fair coins, independent of everything the learner sees. The hypothesis h is a function of the training labels and of independent internal randomness, so h is independent of f|_U. Hence \Pr[h(x)\ne f(x)]=\tfrac12 for each x\in U, and the average over U has expectation \tfrac12.

2. By 1, \sum_f\mathrm{err}_{\mathrm{OTS}}(A,f)=2^N\cdot\tfrac12=\sum_f\mathrm{err}_{\mathrm{OTS}}(B,f), so the difference sums to zero. Whatever advantage A has over B on one set of targets, it gives back exactly on the complement. No learner is better than another “in general”, even the learner that predicts the opposite of the majority label.

3. Fix h with \mathrm{err}_D(h)>\epsilon. Each i.i.d. sample is consistent with it with probability below 1-\epsilon, so \Pr[h\text{ consistent with all }n]\le(1-\epsilon)^n\le e^{-n\epsilon}. There are at most M such h, so by the union bound \Pr[\exists\text{ consistent }h\text{ with }\mathrm{err}_D(h)>\epsilon]\le Me^{-n\epsilon}. Equivalently, with probability at least 1-\delta, every consistent h has \mathrm{err}_D(h)\le(\ln M+\ln(1/\delta))/n. For programs of at most b bits, M<2^{b+1}, so n\approx(b\ln2+\ln(1/\delta))/\epsilon samples suffice. The cost of learning scales with the description length of the target (Occam). For the class of all functions, M=2^N, and the bound needs n\gtrsim N\ln2/\epsilon: you must see a constant fraction of the domain, which is NFL again.

4. It entered at “f|_U is independent of the labels on the training set”. Any prior under which seen labels are informative about unseen ones escapes NFL. Examples:

  • a simplicity (Solomonoff) prior, which favours the shortest consistent program (WB4);
  • a smoothness prior, which favours kernel or nearest-neighbour methods;
  • a symmetry prior, which favours equivariant architectures such as CNNs (WB3).

The question “why does deep learning generalize?” becomes: which prior over targets do the architecture and the optimizer implement, and why does it match the world?

2.B. Universal approximation: existence, not mechanism

The question. Universal approximation theorems (UAT) say that a large enough network can approximate any reasonable function. Does that explain anything about deep learning? The exercise counts how many units are needed in one dimension, shows that depth can buy exponentially many linear pieces, and lists what the theorem does not say.

UAT (quoted). For any continuous f on a compact K\subset\mathbb R^d and any \epsilon>0, a one-hidden-layer network with a continuous non-polynomial activation (for example ReLU) achieves \sup_K|f-g|<\epsilon. Cybenko (1989) and Hornik (1991) proved the sigmoidal versions; the non-polynomial characterization is Leshno, Lin, Pinkus and Schocken (1993). The theorem says nothing about how many units are needed. The problem below counts them in one dimension. Part 4 follows the depth-separation construction of Telgarsky (2015).

2.B.1. How many units for \sin\omega x?
  1. Show that a continuous piecewise-linear function on [0,1] with K pieces is exactly a one-hidden-layer ReLU network with K units plus an output bias.
  2. Upper bound. Show that the piecewise-linear interpolant of \sin(\omega x) at K+1 equally spaced knots has sup error at most \omega^2/(8K^2). Deduce that \lceil\omega/\sqrt{8\epsilon}\,\rceil units suffice for accuracy \epsilon.
  3. Lower bound. Show that a one-hidden-layer ReLU network with m units on \mathbb R is piecewise linear with at most m+1 pieces. Then show that for \epsilon<1 accuracy, m must grow at least like \omega/(2\pi).
  4. Depth. Let t(x)=2\,\mathrm{ReLU}(x)-4\,\mathrm{ReLU}(x-\tfrac12), the tent map on [0,1]. Show that the k-fold composition t^{\circ k} has 2^k linear pieces. Conclude that a depth-k network with 2k units computes a function that any one-hidden-layer network needs at least 2^k-1 units to represent.
  5. List three things UAT does not tell you.
Hint

For 2, the interpolation error on an interval [a,b] is e(x)=\tfrac12f''(\xi)(x-a)(x-b). For 3, the extrema of \sin\omega x alternate between +1 and -1. On a piece where g is affine, can g be positive, negative, positive at three consecutive extrema? For 4, t maps each half of [0,1] monotonically onto [0,1].

Solution

1. Let the breakpoints be 0=t_0<t_1<\dots<t_{K-1}<1, with slopes a_0,\dots,a_{K-1} on the pieces. Then on [0,1]

g(x)=g(0)+a_0\,\mathrm{ReLU}(x-t_0)+\sum_{k=1}^{K-1}(a_k-a_{k-1})\,\mathrm{ReLU}(x-t_k),

since each ReLU switches on the slope change at its breakpoint. That is K units plus a bias.

2. On a knot interval of length h=1/K, the interpolation error is e(x)=\tfrac12f''(\xi)(x-a)(x-b), and |(x-a)(x-b)|\le h^2/4. With |f''|\le\omega^2 this gives |e|\le\omega^2h^2/8=\omega^2/(8K^2). Setting \omega^2/(8K^2)\le\epsilon gives K\ge\omega/\sqrt{8\epsilon}, so m=\lceil\omega/\sqrt{8\epsilon}\rceil units suffice. The count is linear in the frequency.

3. Each unit \mathrm{ReLU}(wx+b) has at most one breakpoint, so the sum has at most m breakpoints and at most m+1 affine pieces. The extrema of \sin\omega x in [0,1] are at x_j=(\pi/2+j\pi)/\omega, with values (-1)^j, and there are J=\lfloor\omega/\pi-\tfrac12\rfloor+1 of them. If \sup|g-\sin\omega x|<1, then g(x_j) has sign (-1)^j. An affine function that is positive at x_j and x_{j+2} is positive at x_{j+1}, which lies between them. So a single piece contains at most two consecutive extrema, and J\le2(m+1), i.e. m\ge J/2-1\approx\omega/(2\pi). Together with 2, the number of units needed is \Theta(\omega) at fixed accuracy.

4. On [0,\tfrac12], t(x)=2x is an increasing bijection onto [0,1]; on [\tfrac12,1], t(x)=2-2x is a decreasing bijection onto [0,1]. So t^{\circ k}=t^{\circ(k-1)}\circ t consists of two reparametrized copies of t^{\circ(k-1)}, one of them reversed, and by induction it has 2\cdot2^{k-1}=2^k pieces. Each layer uses 2 units, so the depth-k network has 2k units. By 3, one hidden layer needs m+1\ge2^k units. Depth buys pieces exponentially.

5. UAT does not tell you:

  • how many units are needed. For generic Lipschitz targets in d dimensions the count grows like \epsilon^{-d}.
  • whether gradient descent finds the approximant.
  • how many samples identify it, or which of the many interpolants of the data training selects.

Polynomials (Weierstrass) and lookup tables are universal approximators too. Expressivity is cheap, so it cannot be what distinguishes networks. UAT rules out “the net cannot represent the target” and nothing else.

2.C. Capacity bounds, and why they are vacuous here

The question. The classical guarantee of generalization: if the hypothesis class is small compared with n, then training risk is close to population risk uniformly over the class, so whatever the learner picks generalizes. How small is “small” for an infinite class (VC dimension, 2.C.1), and what do these bounds say about a real network (2.C.2)?

Uniform convergence. Two quantities measure how well a learned \hat h did: the generalization gap R(\hat h)-\hat R_n(\hat h) (test minus training risk) and the excess risk R(\hat h)-\inf_{h\in\mathcal H}R(h) (how far \hat h is from the best member of the class). For empirical risk minimization (ERM, \hat h\in\arg\min_{\mathcal H}\hat R_n), the excess risk is at most 2\sup_{h\in\mathcal H}|R(h)-\hat R_n(h)| (proved in 1.A.1.3). So it suffices to show that the gap is small for every h in the class at once, whatever the learner picks. For a finite class with losses in [0,1], Hoeffding’s inequality and a union bound give, with probability at least 1-\delta,

\sup_{h\in\mathcal H}|R(h)-\hat R_n(h)|\le\sqrt{\frac{\ln(2|\mathcal H|/\delta)}{2n}} ,

which 2.C.2.1 derives. The size of the class enters only through \ln|\mathcal H|, so the bound is useful when n\gg\ln|\mathcal H|. Classes with real-valued parameters are infinite; for them 2.C.1 replaces \ln|\mathcal H| by a combinatorial size, the VC dimension. 2.C.2 then plugs a real network into both bounds. Finite classes and VC dimension are in chapters 4 and 6 of UML (Shalev-Shwartz and Ben-David, linked in 2.A).

2.C.1. Shattering and VC dimension

Why. The finite-class bound charges \ln|\mathcal H|, which is infinite for any class with a real-valued parameter. What matters on n training points is not how many hypotheses there are but how many different labellings of those points they produce. The VC dimension measures this, and the parts below show it is not the number of parameters.

Let \mathcal H be a class of binary classifiers h:\mathcal X\to\{0,1\}, with risk R(h)=\Pr_{(x,y)}[h(x)\ne y] and training error \hat R_n(h) on n i.i.d. examples. A finite set C=\{x_1,\dots,x_m\}\subset\mathcal X is shattered by \mathcal H if all 2^m labellings of C are produced by some h\in\mathcal H. The growth function \Pi_{\mathcal H}(m) is the largest number of distinct labellings that \mathcal H produces on any m points, so \Pi_{\mathcal H}(m)\le2^m, with equality iff some m-point set is shattered. The VC dimension d_{\mathrm{VC}}(\mathcal H) is the largest m for which some m-point set is shattered (\infty if there is no largest). To prove d_{\mathrm{VC}}=d, exhibit one shattered set of d points and show that no set of d+1 points is shattered.

Sauer–Shelah lemma (quoted; proved in ch. 6 of Shalev-Shwartz and Ben-David’s Understanding Machine Learning). If d_{\mathrm{VC}}(\mathcal H)=d<\infty, then for m\ge d

\Pi_{\mathcal H}(m)\le\sum_{i=0}^{d}\binom mi\le\Big(\frac{em}{d}\Big)^d .

So on n points a class of VC dimension d acts like a finite class of at most (en/d)^d hypotheses, and d\ln(en/d) takes the place of \ln|\mathcal H| in the finite-class bound. One standard form of the resulting VC bound (quoted from Mohri, Rostamizadeh and Talwalkar, Foundations of Machine Learning, 2nd ed., Corollary 3.19; it follows from Rademacher complexity and Massart’s lemma, and other textbooks have other constants): with probability at least 1-\delta, for all h\in\mathcal H,

R(h)\le\hat R_n(h)+\sqrt{\frac{2d\ln(en/d)}{n}}+\sqrt{\frac{\ln(1/\delta)}{2n}} .

It says something only when n\gg d. For ReLU networks with W weights in L layers, the best general upper bound is d=O(WL\log W), and some architectures achieve \Omega(WL\log(W/L)) (Bartlett et al. 2019); at fixed depth this is of order W\log W. 2.C.2 compares that with n.

  1. Thresholds on \mathbb R: h_t(x)=\mathbf 1[x\ge t], t\in\mathbb R. Show d_{\mathrm{VC}}=1 and \Pi(m)=m+1.
  2. Intervals on \mathbb R: h_{a,b}(x)=\mathbf 1[a\le x\le b]. Show d_{\mathrm{VC}}=2, compute \Pi(m), and compare with the Sauer–Shelah bound.
  3. Affine halfspaces in \mathbb R^d: h_{w,b}(x)=\mathbf 1[w^\top x+b\ge0], with d+1 parameters. Show that the d+1 points 0,e_1,\dots,e_d are shattered. Then use Radon’s theorem (any d+2 points in \mathbb R^d can be split into two groups whose convex hulls intersect) to show that no d+2 points are shattered, so d_{\mathrm{VC}}=d+1. Optionally, prove Radon’s theorem.
  4. One parameter, infinite VC dimension. Let h_\omega(x)=\mathbf 1[\sin(\omega x)>0] with \omega>0. Take x_i=2^{-i} for i=1,\dots,m, any labels y_1,\dots,y_m\in\{0,1\}, and \omega=\pi\big(1+\sum_{j=1}^m(1-y_j)2^j\big). Show that h_\omega(x_i)=y_i for all i, so d_{\mathrm{VC}}=\infty. What does this say about counting parameters? What changes if \omega is stored as a 32-bit float?
Hint

For 2, sort the points: which patterns of 0s and 1s can an interval cut out? For 3, the halfspace \{x:w^\top x+b\ge0\} and its complement are both convex. For Radon, solve \sum_i\lambda_ix_i=0, \sum_i\lambda_i=0 and split the points by the sign of \lambda_i. For 4, compute \omega x_i/\pi modulo 2: the terms with j>i are even integers.

Solution

1. One point x is shattered: t\le x labels it 1 and t>x labels it 0. For two points x_1<x_2, the labelling (1,0) needs t\le x_1 and t>x_2, which is impossible. So d_{\mathrm{VC}}=1. On m distinct sorted points the achievable labellings are 0\cdots01\cdots1, fixed by the number k\in\{0,\dots,m\} of zeros. So \Pi(m)=m+1=\binom m0+\binom m1, and Sauer–Shelah holds with equality.

2. Two points x_1<x_2: a long interval gives (1,1), a short interval around one point gives (1,0) or (0,1), and an interval away from both gives (0,0). Three points x_1<x_2<x_3: an interval containing x_1 and x_3 contains x_2, so (1,0,1) is impossible, and d_{\mathrm{VC}}=2. On m sorted points the 1s must form one contiguous block. There is the empty block, plus one block for each choice of first and last point i\le j, which gives

\Pi(m)=1+\frac{m(m+1)}2=\binom m0+\binom m1+\binom m2 ,

again equal to the Sauer–Shelah bound.

3. Lower bound. Write the labels of 0,e_1,\dots,e_d as y_0,\dots,y_d and let s_i=2y_i-1\in\{\pm1\}. Take b=s_0/2 and w_i=s_i-s_0/2. Then w^\top0+b=s_0/2 and w^\top e_i+b=s_i. Each has the sign s_i and none is zero, so every labelling is produced and d_{\mathrm{VC}}\ge d+1.

Upper bound. Take any d+2 points x_1,\dots,x_{d+2}. The system \sum_i\lambda_ix_i=0, \sum_i\lambda_i=0 has d+1 equations and d+2 unknowns, so it has a solution \lambda\ne0. Because the \lambda_i sum to zero, some are positive and some negative. Let I=\{i:\lambda_i>0\}, let J be the rest, and let s=\sum_{i\in I}\lambda_i>0. Then

p=\sum_{i\in I}\frac{\lambda_i}{s}x_i=\sum_{i\in J}\frac{-\lambda_i}{s}x_i

is a convex combination of the I-points and also of the J-points (Radon’s theorem). Label I by 1 and J by 0. A halfspace producing this labelling would have the convex set \{w^\top x+b\ge0\} contain all I-points, hence p. The convex set \{w^\top x+b<0\} would contain all J-points, hence p too. That is a contradiction. So d_{\mathrm{VC}}=d+1, which for this class equals the number of parameters.

4. Write c_j=1-y_j\in\{0,1\}. Then

\frac{\omega x_i}{\pi}=2^{-i}\Big(1+\sum_{j=1}^mc_j2^j\Big)=\underbrace{\sum_{j>i}c_j2^{j-i}}_{\text{even integer}}+c_i+\theta_i,\qquad \theta_i=\sum_{j<i}c_j2^{j-i}+2^{-i}.

The even integer shifts \omega x_i by a multiple of 2\pi and does not change the sine. Also 0<\theta_i\le\sum_{k=1}^{i-1}2^{-k}+2^{-i}=1-2^{-i}<1. So \omega x_i\equiv\pi(c_i+\theta_i)\pmod{2\pi} with \theta_i\in(0,1), and \sin(\omega x_i)>0 iff c_i=0 iff y_i=1. Every labelling of every finite set \{2^{-1},\dots,2^{-m}\} is produced, so d_{\mathrm{VC}}=\infty. No distribution-free VC bound applies to this one-parameter class.

The number of parameters is neither an upper bound on the VC dimension (this example; ReLU networks with W weights in L layers, whose VC dimension can be of order WL\log(W/L)) nor a lower bound (redundant parameters, as in \mathbf 1[(w_1+w_2)x\ge0], add nothing).

If \omega is a 32-bit float, the class has at most 2^{32} members. Shattering m points needs 2^m distinct functions, so d_{\mathrm{VC}}\le32, and the finite-class bound applies with \ln|\mathcal H|\le32\ln2. The construction above needs \omega/\pi to about m+1 significant bits. The infinite VC dimension comes from the infinite precision of one real number, which can hide arbitrarily many bits. This is the difference between counting parameters and counting bits that the float-encoding bound in 2.C.2 relies on.

2.C.2. Plug in a real network

How big are these bounds for a network of realistic size? Use the 0–1 loss (so R is test error and \hat R_n training error) and the finite-class bound \sup_h|R(h)-\hat R_n(h)|\le\sqrt{\ln(2|\mathcal H|/\delta)/(2n)} from the 2.C introduction.

  1. Derive the finite-class bound from Hoeffding’s inequality for one fixed h, \Pr[|\hat R_n(h)-R(h)|>t]\le2e^{-2nt^2}, and a union bound.
  2. A network with W=10^6 float32 weights is a finite class: each weight takes at most 2^{32} values. Evaluate the bound for n=5\times10^4 (the size of the CIFAR-10 training set) and \delta=0.05. Then compare with n the VC-dimension scale d\approx W\ln W (the general upper bound for ReLU networks of fixed depth, quoted in 2.C.1).
  3. Zhang et al. (2.D) show that this kind of architecture, trained by SGD, fits CIFAR-10 images with uniformly random labels to zero training error. Let \mathcal H be the set of functions the architecture and SGD can produce, and write \mathrm{gap}(h)=R(h)-\hat R_n(h). Assume such interpolation succeeds with high probability on i.i.d. samples from a distribution with independent uniform labels and distinct inputs. Show that every bound of the form “for every data distribution P, with probability \ge1-\delta, all h\in\mathcal H have \mathrm{gap}(h)\le\epsilon(n,\delta)” must have \epsilon\ge0.9 (10 classes), up to sampling fluctuation.
Hint

For 3, the bound has to hold for every P. Choose one.

Solution

1. For a fixed h, \Pr[|\hat R_n(h)-R(h)|>t]\le2e^{-2nt^2}. A union bound over |\mathcal H| hypotheses gives \Pr[\sup_h|\cdot|>t]\le2|\mathcal H|e^{-2nt^2}. Setting this equal to \delta and solving gives t=\sqrt{\ln(2|\mathcal H|/\delta)/(2n)}.

2. Using the parameter-encoding upper bound \ln|\mathcal H|\le32W\ln2\approx2.22\times10^7, so the bound is \sqrt{(2.22\times10^7+\ln 40)/10^5}\approx\sqrt{222}\approx14.9. Since the 0–1 loss is at most 1, this parameter-encoding bound says nothing. The rough VC scale is W\ln W\approx1.4\times10^7\gg n=5\times10^4, and the VC bound needs n\gg d, so it is vacuous too. This scale is a generic upper bound; it does not prove that this architecture has that VC dimension. But if the actual VC dimension is at least n, NFL bites as well: the class shatters some n points, and for D uniform on those points the sample misses an expected fraction (1-1/n)^n\approx e^{-1} of them, so by 2.A.1.1 some target forces expected error at least \tfrac12(1-1/n)^n\approx0.18 on any learner.

3. Take P' to be CIFAR-10 images with labels drawn uniformly from the 10 classes, independently of the image. Assume the random-label fitting result extends to i.i.d. samples from this input distribution, with distinct inputs and high probability of interpolation. Then SGD reaches an h\in\mathcal H with \hat R_n(h)=0. Its labels are independent of the input, so R(h)=0.9. So on P', some h\in\mathcal H has gap 0.9 with high probability. A bound that depends only on \mathcal H, n and \delta must hold for P', so \epsilon(n,\delta)\ge0.9-o(1). The same distribution-independent bound is then vacuous for the real labels as well. The experiment on one fixed image collection motivates the interpolation assumption; it does not prove it for every random draw. Vacuous is not wrong, but it cannot be the explanation. 2.D.1.1 asks which explanations survive.

2.D. Reading: networks fit random labels

Why. This is the experiment that made class-wide capacity bounds untenable as an explanation of deep learning. Standard networks, trained the standard way, fit labels that carry no information at all. Whatever class they range over is therefore large enough to make every bound of 2.C vacuous, yet with real labels the same networks generalize.

Understanding deep learning requires rethinking generalization, Chiyuan Zhang, Samy Bengio, Moritz Hardt, Benjamin Recht, Oriol Vinyals (ICLR 2017) Importance ●●●●● Length ●●○○○

Standard image-classification networks trained by SGD fit random labels, and even random pixels, to zero training error, with or without explicit regularization. So capacity measures of the model class cannot explain why the same networks generalize on real labels. Read §1 (the claim and the contributions) and §2 (the randomization tests: true labels, partially and fully random labels, shuffled and random pixels; how training time changes). §3 (the role of explicit and implicit regularization) is short and worth skimming. The finite-sample expressivity construction (§4) and the linear-model discussion (§5) are optional.

2.D.1. Questions on Zhang et al.
  1. List the explanations of generalization that the random-labels experiment rules out, and those it leaves standing.
  2. The paper finds that explicit regularizers (weight decay, dropout, data augmentation) help but are neither necessary nor sufficient. Which of the three views of ridge regularization in 1.D (prior, constraint, spectral filter) does this count against, and which survive? (This uses 1.D.1, 1.D.2 and 1.D.4.)
Solution

1. Ruled out:

  • any explanation through small capacity of the reachable class that holds for every distribution (VC dimension, Rademacher complexity of the class; as the paper notes in §2.2, fitting random labels means the empirical Rademacher complexity is essentially 1);
  • explicit regularization as the main cause, since the nets generalize nearly as well without it;
  • “SGD cannot find memorizing solutions”, since it finds them, only a constant factor more slowly.

Not ruled out:

  • data-dependent accounts, in which the learned function depends on structure in the true labels: an implicit bias toward low-norm, flat or simple solutions when such solutions exist;
  • norm-, margin-, compression- and PAC-Bayes-style bounds evaluated on the learned function (2.E);
  • a prior over functions whose support is everything but whose mass is on structured functions.

The slower fit on random labels is a hint that some simplicity ordering is at work. Such a “soft” bias (the class can fit anything, but training prefers some fits) predicts things a hard capacity restriction does not:

  • the same model at the same n generalizes differently depending on the labels;
  • random labels take longer to fit and end at larger norm or complexity;
  • adding parameters need not hurt, and can help (double descent, section 3), whereas a class-wide capacity upper bound can worsen with class size without predicting that actual risk must worsen;
  • a bound in terms of the prior mass or description length of the found function can be non-vacuous where class-capacity bounds are not.

2. It counts against the constraint view used as an explanation, in which a norm ball is small enough for its capacity to explain generalization. The nets generalize without the constraint, and with the constraint they still fit random labels, so the constrained class is still huge. The prior view survives if the prior is soft and implemented implicitly, by architecture, initialization and SGD. The spectral-filter view also survives as implicit regularization: early stopping and gradient descent’s preference for large-eigenvalue directions, and gradient descent’s convergence to the minimum-norm solution (1.D.4).

2.E. PAC-Bayes: bounds for the function you found

The question. 2.C and 2.D showed that any generalization bound depending only on the hypothesis class, the sample size and the confidence level must be vacuous for networks that can fit random labels. Is there a rigorous bound that can still be small for the network SGD actually found? PAC-Bayes bounds are one answer. Their complexity term measures how far a distribution over weights, built around the trained network after seeing the data, is from a distribution fixed before seeing it. The bound is small only if a distribution close to the prior, and hence broad, still has low training error. It depends on the data and on the solution, not on what else the class could fit. With such a bound, Dziugaite and Roy (2017, reading below) obtained the first nonvacuous bounds for networks with millions of parameters, where VC bounds were vacuous by orders of magnitude. In this subsection you prove a PAC-Bayes bound from scratch and recover the Occam bound as a special case (2.E.1), then see that for Gaussian distributions over weights the complexity term becomes a weight-norm term plus a flatness term, and that the bound is consistent with Zhang et al.’s random-label fits (2.E.2).

Setup. Examples z=(x,y) are drawn i.i.d. from an unknown distribution D, and S=(z_1,\dots,z_n) is the sample. Hypotheses are h\in\mathcal H; for a network, h is a weight vector w\in\mathbb R^p. The loss \ell(h;z) takes values in [0,1], for example the 0–1 loss. The risk is R(h)=\mathbb E_{z\sim D}\,\ell(h;z) and the empirical risk is \hat R_n(h)=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\ell(h;z_i).

  • The prior P is a probability distribution on \mathcal H chosen without looking at S. It may depend on anything independent of the sample, such as the random initialization.
  • The posterior Q is any probability distribution on \mathcal H, and may depend on S in any way. Despite the names, there is no likelihood and no Bayes rule. A typical choice is \mathcal N(w_{\mathrm{SGD}},\sigma^2I), centred on the trained weights.
  • The Gibbs predictor draws a fresh h\sim Q for each prediction. Its risk is \mathbb E_{h\sim Q}R(h), written \mathbb E_QR, and its training risk is \mathbb E_Q\hat R_n.
  • The Kullback–Leibler divergence is \mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)=\mathbb E_{h\sim Q}\ln\frac{dQ}{dP}(h)\ge0: \sum_hQ(h)\ln\frac{Q(h)}{P(h)} for discrete distributions and \int q\ln\frac qp for densities. It is +\infty unless Q gives zero mass to every set to which P gives zero mass.

Theorem (McAllester’s bound with Maurer’s constants; quoted from Alquier’s tutorial, §3.2.2). Fix the prior P and \delta\in(0,1). With probability at least 1-\delta over the sample S, simultaneously for all Q,

\mathbb E_QR\le\mathbb E_Q\hat R_n+\sqrt{\frac{\mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)+\ln(2\sqrt n/\delta)}{2n}} .

  • For all Q simultaneously. You may choose Q after seeing the data, for example by minimizing the right-hand side. Only P must be fixed in advance.
  • What replaced the class. The one-sided finite-class bound, uniform over a class of size |\mathcal H|, reads R(h)\le\hat R_n(h)+\sqrt{(\ln|\mathcal H|+\ln(1/\delta))/(2n)}. Here \ln|\mathcal H|, a property of the class, has become \mathrm{KL}(Q\|P), a property of what you chose after seeing the data. 2.E.1.5 makes this exact.
  • Which predictor. The bound is for the randomized predictor, not for the single network at the centre of Q. Transferring it to that network needs a further argument, for example that its predictions rarely change under the perturbations Q makes. Nothing requires training to sample from Q, or from any Bayesian posterior.

Numerical certificates use the sharper form the theorem is derived from (Alquier, Theorem 3.2): \mathrm{kl}\big(\mathbb E_Q\hat R_n\,\|\,\mathbb E_QR\big)\le\big(\mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)+\ln(2\sqrt n/\delta)\big)/n, where \mathrm{kl}(q\|r)=q\ln\frac qr+(1-q)\ln\frac{1-q}{1-r} is the KL divergence between two coin flips. Pinsker’s inequality \mathrm{kl}(q\|r)\ge2(q-r)^2 gives the displayed theorem; near zero training error the kl form decays like 1/n rather than 1/\sqrt n.

2.E.1. Change of measure, and a PAC-Bayes bound in five steps

The standard PAC-Bayes proofs rest on one inequality, the Donsker–Varadhan (or Gibbs) variational formula of part 1. Parts 2–3 use it to prove a bound due to Catoni (Alquier, Theorem 2.1): for any \lambda>0 fixed before seeing the data, with probability at least 1-\delta, simultaneously for all Q,

\mathbb E_QR\le\mathbb E_Q\hat R_n+\frac{\mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)+\ln(1/\delta)}{\lambda}+\frac{\lambda}{8n} .\qquad(\star)

  1. Donsker–Varadhan. Let f be a bounded function on \mathcal H, and define the Gibbs measure P_f by \frac{dP_f}{dP}=\frac{e^f}{\mathbb E_Pe^f}. Show that for every Q, \mathbb E_Qf\le\mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)+\ln\mathbb E_Pe^f, with equality iff Q=P_f. Equivalently, \ln\mathbb E_Pe^f=\max_Q\big[\mathbb E_Qf-\mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)\big]. What is this for f=-\beta E and P uniform on finitely many states?
  2. One hypothesis. Fix h and \lambda>0. Use Hoeffding’s lemma (if X\in[a,b] and \mathbb EX=0, then \mathbb Ee^{sX}\le e^{s^2(b-a)^2/8}) to show \mathbb E_S\,e^{\lambda(R(h)-\hat R_n(h))}\le e^{\lambda^2/(8n)}.
  3. All posteriors at once. Show that with probability at least 1-\delta over S, \mathbb E_{h\sim P}\,e^{\lambda(R(h)-\hat R_n(h))}\le e^{\lambda^2/(8n)}/\delta. Where do you use that P does not depend on S? On this event, apply part 1 with f=\lambda(R-\hat R_n) to obtain (\star) for all Q.
  4. Choosing \lambda, and the best Q. For fixed Q, minimize the right-hand side of (\star) over \lambda and compare with the theorem above. Why can you not simply plug in the minimizing \lambda, and how does a union bound over a grid of \lambda values fixed in advance repair it? Then, for fixed \lambda, show that the Q minimizing the right-hand side of (\star) is the Gibbs posterior Q_\lambda with \frac{dQ_\lambda}{dP}\propto e^{-\lambda\hat R_n}. When would Q_\lambda be a Bayesian posterior?
  5. Occam. Let \mathcal H be countable and take Q=\delta_h, the point mass on one hypothesis. Show \mathrm{KL}(\delta_h\|P)=\ln(1/P(h)), and write the bound the theorem gives, which holds for all h simultaneously. What does it give for P uniform on a finite class, and for P(h)\propto2^{-L(h)}, where L(h) is the length in bits of h in a prefix-free code? Why is a point mass useless for real-valued weights under a prior with a density?
Hint

For 1, expand \mathrm{KL}(Q\|P_f)\ge0. For 2, write R(h)-\hat R_n(h)=\frac1n\sum_iX_i with X_i=R(h)-\ell(h;z_i): independent, mean zero, each in an interval of length 1. Take s=\lambda/n. For 3, average part 2 over h\sim P, swap \mathbb E_S and \mathbb E_P, and apply Markov’s inequality to the nonnegative random variable \mathbb E_Pe^{\lambda(R-\hat R_n)}. For 4, use the grid \lambda_k=2^k, k=0,\dots,K-1, with confidence \delta/K for each. For 5, Kraft’s inequality says that the code lengths of a prefix-free binary code satisfy \sum_h2^{-L(h)}\le1.

Solution

1. If \mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)=\infty there is nothing to prove. Otherwise write Z=\mathbb E_Pe^f, so \frac{dQ}{dP_f}=\frac{dQ}{dP}\,Ze^{-f} and

0\le\mathrm{KL}(Q\|P_f)=\mathbb E_Q\Big[\ln\frac{dQ}{dP}-f+\ln Z\Big]=\mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)-\mathbb E_Qf+\ln\mathbb E_Pe^f .

Rearranging gives the inequality, and \mathrm{KL}(Q\|P_f)=0 iff Q=P_f, so the maximum is attained exactly at the Gibbs measure. With P uniform on N states, \mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)=\ln N-\mathrm{Ent}(Q), where \mathrm{Ent}(Q)=-\sum Q\ln Q is the entropy. Taking f=-\beta E gives \mathbb E_Pe^f=Z_\beta/N, with Z_\beta=\sum e^{-\beta E} the partition function. The inequality becomes \langle E\rangle_Q-T\,\mathrm{Ent}(Q)\ge-T\ln Z_\beta=F, with equality for the Boltzmann distribution: the Gibbs variational principle for the free energy.

2. The X_i=R(h)-\ell(h;z_i) are i.i.d., have mean zero, and lie in [R(h)-1,R(h)], an interval of length 1. By independence and Hoeffding’s lemma with s=\lambda/n,

\mathbb E_S\,e^{\lambda(R(h)-\hat R_n(h))}=\prod_{i=1}^n\mathbb E\,e^{(\lambda/n)X_i}\le\big(e^{\lambda^2/(8n^2)}\big)^n=e^{\lambda^2/(8n)} .

3. Average part 2 over h\sim P. Since P is fixed and the integrand is nonnegative, Tonelli’s theorem lets you swap the order: \mathbb E_S\big[\mathbb E_{h\sim P}e^{\lambda(R-\hat R_n)}\big]\le e^{\lambda^2/(8n)}. This is where P must not depend on S. If it did, the inner average would be over a different distribution for each sample, and the swap would no longer reduce to part 2. Markov’s inequality applied to Y=\mathbb E_Pe^{\lambda(R-\hat R_n)}\ge0 gives \Pr_S\big[Y>e^{\lambda^2/(8n)}/\delta\big]\le\delta. On the complementary event, part 1 with f=\lambda(R-\hat R_n) (bounded, since the loss is) gives, for every Q,

\lambda\,\mathbb E_Q(R-\hat R_n)\le\mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)+\ln Y\le\mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)+\frac{\lambda^2}{8n}+\ln\frac1\delta .

Dividing by \lambda gives (\star). The event was defined without reference to Q, which is why the bound holds for all Q at once, including Q chosen using S.

4. Let a=\mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)+\ln(1/\delta). Then a/\lambda+\lambda/(8n) is minimized at \lambda^*=\sqrt{8na}, with value \sqrt{a/(2n)}. This would give \mathbb E_QR\le\mathbb E_Q\hat R_n+\sqrt{(\mathrm{KL}+\ln(1/\delta))/(2n)}, the theorem’s form with \ln(1/\delta) in place of \ln(2\sqrt n/\delta). But \lambda^* depends on \mathrm{KL}(Q\|P), and Q depends on S. The event in part 3 was built for a single \lambda chosen in advance, and nothing covers a \lambda chosen after seeing the data.

Repair. Before seeing the data, fix the grid \lambda_k=2^k, k=0,\dots,K-1, with 2^{K-1}\ge4n, so K\approx\log_2(4n)+1. Apply part 3 to each \lambda_k at confidence \delta/K. By the union bound, with probability at least 1-\delta all K bounds hold at once, for all Q, with \ln(K/\delta) in place of \ln(1/\delta), so you may pick the best k after seeing the data. With a=\mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)+\ln(K/\delta) and whenever the optimized bound is below 1, the optimal \lambda^*=\sqrt{8na} lies in [1,4n], hence within a factor \sqrt2 of a grid point; at \lambda=c\lambda^* the value is \frac12(c+1/c)\sqrt{a/(2n)}, at most 1.061\sqrt{a/(2n)} for c\in[1/\sqrt2,\sqrt2]. The cost is that factor and \ln K\sim\ln\ln n extra nats; the theorem pays \ln(2\sqrt n) instead.

Best Q. Only \mathbb E_Q\hat R_n+\mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)/\lambda depends on Q, and by part 1 with f=-\lambda\hat R_n,

\min_Q\Big[\mathbb E_Q\hat R_n+\frac{\mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)}\lambda\Big]=-\frac1\lambda\ln\mathbb E_Pe^{-\lambda\hat R_n},

attained at Q_\lambda\propto e^{-\lambda\hat R_n}P. If the loss were the negative log-likelihood -\ln p(y\mid x,h) and \lambda=n, then e^{-n\hat R_n}=\prod_ip(y_i\mid x_i,h), and Q_\lambda would be exactly the Bayesian posterior. Other values of \lambda give “tempered” posteriors. The log loss is unbounded, so the [0,1] theorem does not cover that case as stated. The Bayesian posterior is one possible Q among many, and the bound certifies whichever Q you choose.

5. \mathrm{KL}(\delta_h\|P)=1\cdot\ln\frac1{P(h)}, since \delta_h puts all its mass on h. With probability at least 1-\delta, for all h simultaneously,

R(h)\le\hat R_n(h)+\sqrt{\frac{\ln(1/P(h))+\ln(2\sqrt n/\delta)}{2n}} .

  • Uniform P on M hypotheses: \ln(1/P(h))=\ln M. This is the finite-class bound, except for \ln(2\sqrt n/\delta) in place of \ln(1/\delta); a direct union bound avoids that extra \ln(2\sqrt n).
  • Prefix code: Kraft’s inequality \sum_h2^{-L(h)}\le1 makes P(h)=2^{-L(h)}/Z a distribution with Z\le1, so \ln(1/P(h))\le L(h)\ln2. The complexity is the description length of the particular h you output, L(h)\ln2 nats, regardless of how large the class is. This is the Occam bound: hypotheses with short descriptions need few samples.
  • Continuous weights: if P has a density, then P(\{w\})=0 and \mathrm{KL}(\delta_w\|P)=\infty, so the bound is vacuous. To pay a finite price for a real-valued w, either store it to finite precision (a code, as above) or spread Q over a neighbourhood of w whose prior mass is not too small. 2.E.2 does the second.

2.E.2. Gaussian posteriors: weight norm and flatness

Take network weights w\in\mathbb R^p, the prior P=\mathcal N(w_0,\sigma_P^2I), with w_0 and \sigma_P fixed before seeing the data (for example w_0=0, or the random initialization), and the posterior Q=\mathcal N(w,\Sigma_Q) centred on the trained weights w. Use Catoni’s bound (\star) from 2.E.1 with \lambda fixed in advance: with probability at least 1-\delta, for all Q, \mathbb E_QR\le\mathbb E_Q\hat R_n+\big(\mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)+\ln(1/\delta)\big)/\lambda+\lambda/(8n). For parts 2–4, treat \hat R_n as smooth near w, with Hessian \hat H=\nabla^2\hat R_n(w). This holds for a bounded smooth surrogate loss; for the 0–1 loss these parts are heuristic.

  1. Show that for Gaussians in \mathbb R^p, \mathrm{KL}\big(\mathcal N(\mu_Q,\Sigma_Q)\,\|\,\mathcal N(\mu_P,\Sigma_P)\big)=\tfrac12\big[\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_P^{-1}\Sigma_Q)+(\mu_Q-\mu_P)^\top\Sigma_P^{-1}(\mu_Q-\mu_P)-p+\ln\frac{\det\Sigma_P}{\det\Sigma_Q}\big]. Deduce that, with s_1,\dots,s_p the eigenvalues of \Sigma_Q, \mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)=\frac{\|w-w_0\|^2}{2\sigma_P^2}+\frac12\sum_{k=1}^p\Big(\frac{s_k}{\sigma_P^2}-1-\ln\frac{s_k}{\sigma_P^2}\Big). Interpret the two terms. What does the second cost for \Sigma_Q=\sigma_Q^2I with \sigma_Q\ll\sigma_P, and how big is that for p=10^6 and \sigma_P/\sigma_Q=10^3?
  2. Show that \mathbb E_Q\hat R_n=\hat R_n(w)+\tfrac12\mathrm{tr}(\hat H\Sigma_Q) up to fourth-order terms in the width of Q, whether or not \nabla\hat R_n(w)=0.
  3. Insert parts 1 and 2 into (\star) and minimize over \Sigma_Q, assuming \hat H\succeq0 and that the quadratic approximation holds over the width of Q. Show that the optimum is \Sigma_Q=(\sigma_P^{-2}I+\lambda\hat H)^{-1} and that the bound becomes \mathbb E_QR\lesssim\hat R_n(w)+\frac1\lambda\Big[\frac{\|w-w_0\|^2}{2\sigma_P^2}+\frac12\ln\det\big(I+\lambda\sigma_P^2\hat H\big)+\ln\frac1\delta\Big]+\frac\lambda{8n}. Which eigendirections of \hat H cost complexity, and how much? Where does weight decay appear?
  4. The prior picks a metric (Dinh et al.’s rescaling). Take the two-layer scalar linear network f(x)=w_2w_1x with \hat R_n(w)=a(w_1w_2-c)^2+\text{const}, a>0, c\ne0: the example of 1.D.7.3, with the training risk in place of the population risk. Its minima form the hyperbola w_1w_2=c; at a minimum \hat H=2a\,vv^\top with v=(w_2,w_1)^\top. The rescaling (w_1,w_2)\to(\alpha w_1,w_2/\alpha) does not change the function; Dinh et al. (1.D.8) use this symmetry to make ReLU minima arbitrarily sharp. (This is illustrative only: the squared loss needs a bounded surrogate, and the quadratic approximation needs \sigma_P\ll|w|.) With w_0=0, show that the norm term and the log-det term of part 3 depend on \alpha only through \rho(\alpha)=\alpha^2w_1^2+w_2^2/\alpha^2, so the bound is smallest at the balanced representative and unbounded as \alpha\to0 or \infty. Is the bound then wrong, or only loose? Show that \mathrm{KL}(Q\|P) is unchanged when the same smooth bijection of weight space is applied to both P and Q. Where did the dependence on the representative come from?
  5. Random labels. Replace the labels by labels drawn uniformly from 10 classes, independently of x, and use the 0–1 loss. Using the theorem at the start of 2.E, show that with probability at least 1-\delta, every Q with \mathbb E_Q\hat R_n\le0.1 has \mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)\ge2n(0.8)^2-\ln(2\sqrt n/\delta). Evaluate this for n=5\times10^4 and \delta=0.05. Why is this consistent with SGD fitting random labels? Why does it not run into the argument of 2.C.2.3, that every bound depending only on the class, n and \delta must be at least 0.9?
Hint

For 1, take the expectation under Q of \ln q-\ln p, using \mathbb E_Q(x-m)^\top A(x-m)=\mathrm{tr}(A\Sigma_Q)+(\mu_Q-m)^\top A(\mu_Q-m). For 2, expand \hat R_n(w+\varepsilon) with \varepsilon\sim\mathcal N(0,\Sigma_Q); odd moments of \varepsilon vanish. For 3, use \partial_\Sigma\,\mathrm{tr}(A\Sigma)=A and \partial_\Sigma\ln\det\Sigma=\Sigma^{-1}, and simplify with \mathrm{tr}\big(\Sigma_Q(\lambda\hat H+\sigma_P^{-2}I)\big)=p. For 5, what is R(h) for any fixed h?

Solution

1. With \Delta=\mu_Q-\mu_P,

\ln\frac{q(x)}{p(x)}=\frac12\ln\frac{\det\Sigma_P}{\det\Sigma_Q}-\frac12(x-\mu_Q)^\top\Sigma_Q^{-1}(x-\mu_Q)+\frac12(x-\mu_P)^\top\Sigma_P^{-1}(x-\mu_P).

Under Q, the first quadratic form has mean \mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_Q^{-1}\Sigma_Q)=p, and the second has mean \mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_P^{-1}\Sigma_Q)+\Delta^\top\Sigma_P^{-1}\Delta, which gives the formula. With \Sigma_P=\sigma_P^2I, \mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_P^{-1}\Sigma_Q)=\sum_ks_k/\sigma_P^2 and \ln\det\Sigma_P/\det\Sigma_Q=\sum_k\ln(\sigma_P^2/s_k).

  • Norm term. \|w-w_0\|^2/(2\sigma_P^2) is an L_2 penalty on the distance travelled from w_0 (see part 3).
  • Width term. Each eigendirection costs \frac12\phi(s_k/\sigma_P^2), with \phi(t)=t-1-\ln t\ge0, which is zero iff t=1. Narrowing Q below the prior width costs complexity. For \Sigma_Q=\sigma_Q^2I with \sigma_Q\ll\sigma_P, the cost is about p\ln(\sigma_P/\sigma_Q): roughly the nats needed to specify each of p coordinates to precision \sigma_Q within a range \sigma_P. For p=10^6 and \sigma_P/\sigma_Q=10^3, it is \frac p2(10^{-6}-1+\ln10^6)\approx6.4\times10^6 nats, hopeless for n=5\times10^4.

So a nonvacuous bound needs Q to be about as wide as the prior in almost every direction. In Dziugaite and Roy’s runs, \mathrm{KL}\approx5\times10^3 nats for about 5\times10^5 parameters, roughly 0.01 nats per parameter.

2. Write \varepsilon=w'-w\sim\mathcal N(0,\Sigma_Q). Then \hat R_n(w+\varepsilon)=\hat R_n(w)+\nabla\hat R_n(w)^\top\varepsilon+\frac12\varepsilon^\top\hat H\varepsilon+O(\|\varepsilon\|^3). The linear term has mean zero, whether or not the gradient vanishes, and \mathbb E\,\varepsilon^\top\hat H\varepsilon=\mathrm{tr}(\hat H\Sigma_Q). The cubic term also has mean zero (odd Gaussian moments), so the error is fourth order, and the formula is exact for a quadratic \hat R_n. The randomized predictor pays for curvature in proportion to how widely it spreads. This is where flatness enters.

3. The \Sigma-dependent part of the bound is

J(\Sigma)=\frac12\mathrm{tr}(\hat H\Sigma)+\frac1{2\lambda}\Big[\frac{\mathrm{tr}\,\Sigma}{\sigma_P^2}-p-\ln\det\Sigma+p\ln\sigma_P^2\Big].

It is convex, since -\ln\det is convex. Setting the gradient to zero gives \frac12\hat H+\frac1{2\lambda}(\sigma_P^{-2}I-\Sigma^{-1})=0, so \Sigma_Q^{-1}=\sigma_P^{-2}I+\lambda\hat H. At the optimum, \mathrm{tr}(\hat H\Sigma_Q)+\mathrm{tr}(\Sigma_Q)/(\lambda\sigma_P^2)=\frac1\lambda\mathrm{tr}\big(\Sigma_Q(\lambda\hat H+\sigma_P^{-2}I)\big)=p/\lambda, so

J=\frac p{2\lambda}-\frac p{2\lambda}+\frac1{2\lambda}\ln\det\big(\sigma_P^2\Sigma_Q^{-1}\big)=\frac1{2\lambda}\ln\det\big(I+\lambda\sigma_P^2\hat H\big).

Adding the norm term, \ln(1/\delta)/\lambda and \lambda/(8n) gives the stated bound.

In the eigenbasis of \hat H, with eigenvalues h_k\ge0, the posterior variance is s_k=\sigma_P^2/(1+\lambda\sigma_P^2h_k), and the direction contributes \frac1{2\lambda}\ln(1+\lambda\sigma_P^2h_k) to the bound (its share of the KL plus its curvature penalty, times 1/\lambda).

  • Flat directions, h_k\ll1/(\lambda\sigma_P^2): the posterior is as wide as the prior and the contribution is about zero, however many such directions there are.
  • Sharp directions: the posterior width is about 1/\sqrt{\lambda h_k} and the contribution about \frac1{2\lambda}\ln(\lambda\sigma_P^2h_k), only logarithmic in the sharpness.

The bound counts the curved directions, not the parameters. This is the PAC-Bayes form of the old minimum-description-length argument that flat minima need fewer bits. Weight decay is the norm term, with w held fixed here. Optimizing w as well trades \hat R_n(w) against \|w-w_0\|^2/(2\lambda\sigma_P^2), which is weight decay towards w_0. Neither \sigma_P nor \lambda may be tuned on the data for free; both need a grid and a union bound, as in 2.E.1.4. (The Laplace approximation to the Bayesian evidence has the same log-determinant, with n in place of \lambda.)

4. Write \rho(\alpha)=\alpha^2w_1^2+w_2^2/\alpha^2. The norm term is \|w\|^2/(2\sigma_P^2)=\rho(\alpha)/(2\sigma_P^2). At the rescaled point \hat H=2a\,v_\alpha v_\alpha^\top with v_\alpha=(w_2/\alpha,\alpha w_1)^\top. This has rank one with nonzero eigenvalue 2a\|v_\alpha\|^2=2a\rho(\alpha), so \ln\det(I+\lambda\sigma_P^2\hat H)=\ln\big(1+2a\lambda\sigma_P^2\rho(\alpha)\big). Both terms increase with \rho. By AM–GM, \rho\ge2|w_1w_2|=2|c|, with equality iff \alpha^2=|w_2/w_1|, the balanced point, and \rho\to\infty as \alpha\to0 or \infty.

Wrong or loose? Every one of these bounds is true, because the theorem holds for all Q at once. They are different numbers for the same function. Since Q may be chosen after seeing the data, you are free to centre it at the balanced representative, so the rescaling cannot make the best available bound worse. Dinh et al.’s construction (sharp minima, same function) shows only that the representative SGD lands on need not be the one that gives the best bound.

Invariance. If \varphi is a smooth bijection and P'=\varphi_\#P, Q'=\varphi_\#Q, both densities pick up the same Jacobian factor, which cancels in q'/p', so \mathrm{KL}(Q'\|P')=\mathrm{KL}(Q\|P). The dependence on the representative came from choosing isotropic Gaussians in the w coordinates: an isotropic Gaussian in rescaled coordinates is a different prior, which favours different representatives. Choosing P is choosing a metric, and so a notion of “flat”, which is the requirement 1.D.7.4 found for any meaningful flatness claim. Because the loss depends on w only through the function f_w, the theorem applies equally to distributions over functions, and by the data-processing inequality their KL is no larger than the weight-space KL. Weight-space KL overcharges by paying to specify which of many equivalent w you hold (Dziugaite and Roy, Appendix B).

5. For any fixed h, \Pr[h(x)\ne y]=0.9, because y is uniform on 10 classes and independent of x, hence of h(x). So \mathbb E_QR=0.9 for every Q. On the theorem’s event, 0.9\le\mathbb E_Q\hat R_n+\sqrt{(\mathrm{KL}+\ln(2\sqrt n/\delta))/(2n)}. If \mathbb E_Q\hat R_n\le0.1, then \mathrm{KL}(Q\|P)\ge2n(0.8)^2-\ln(2\sqrt n/\delta). For n=5\times10^4 and \delta=0.05, \ln(2\sqrt n/\delta)\approx9.1, so \mathrm{KL}\ge6.4\times10^4 nats, about 1.3 nats per training example.

This is consistent with fitting random labels. SGD finds w with \hat R_n(w)=0, but every Q that keeps the Gibbs training error below 0.1 must be far from P: narrow around a sharp memorizing solution, or far from w_0, or both. The theorem predicts its own vacuity on such data. On real labels nothing forbids a small KL. Dziugaite and Roy found Q with \mathrm{KL}\approx5\text{–}9\times10^3 nats at n=55{,}000, and nonvacuous bounds. Their random-label run (two classes) ended at \mathrm{KL}\approx2\times10^5, with a bound of 1.35.

The argument of 2.C.2.3 applies to bounds of the form \epsilon(n,\delta) that depend only on the class, n and \delta. Such a bound must also hold on the random-label distribution, so it is at least 0.9. The PAC-Bayes right-hand side also depends on S through Q and \mathbb E_Q\hat R_n. It can be large on random labels and small on real ones, with the same prior, architecture and n. What the bound does not explain is why SGD on real data lands near wide, low-KL regions. It certifies that when it happens; it does not predict it.

Readings.

Computing nonvacuous generalization bounds for deep (stochastic) neural networks with many more parameters than training data, Gintare Karolina Dziugaite, Daniel M. Roy (UAI 2017) Importance ●●●●● Length ●●●○○

Starting from SGD solutions on a two-class version of MNIST (55,000 training images), the authors optimize a diagonal Gaussian posterior around the trained weights by gradient descent on a PAC-Bayes bound. The prior is centred at the random initialization, and its variance is chosen from a grid via a union bound. For networks with 0.3–2.4 million parameters, the bounds on the error of the stochastic networks are 0.16–0.22, against a measured test error of about 0.03 and VC-dimension bounds that are vacuous by orders of magnitude. On random labels the bound is vacuous. Read §1 (the motivation from Zhang et al. and from flat minima), §2–3 (the kl-form bound, the choice of prior, and the objective: a McAllester-type bound for a diagonal Gaussian posterior, whose KL term is the one in 2.E.2.1) and §5 (results, Table 1). Appendix B, on how network symmetries loosen the bound, is optional.

For reference (books): User-friendly introduction to PAC-Bayes bounds, Pierre Alquier (Foundations and Trends in Machine Learning, 2024), §2.1 (Catoni’s bound), §3.2–3.3.

3. Overfitting and double descent

The question. Classical advice says a model with as many parameters as data points will overfit: it fits the noise in the training set, and its test error explodes. The textbook picture is a U-shaped curve of test error against model size, with a sweet spot in the middle (1.C). Modern networks have far more parameters than training examples, fit their training data exactly (they interpolate), and generalize anyway. The curve that replaces the U, “double descent”, rises to a peak where the number of parameters p matches the number of samples n, then falls again as p grows further. The name is from Belkin et al. (2019).

What you get. All of this already happens in least-squares regression, where it can be computed exactly, and that is what this section does. In the limit p,n\to\infty with p/n\to\gamma:

  • Below the threshold (\gamma<1, 3.A) least squares is unbiased, and its excess risk \sigma^2\gamma/(1-\gamma) blows up as \gamma\to1.
  • Above it (\gamma>1, 3.B) there are infinitely many interpolating solutions. The one gradient descent finds, the minimum-norm interpolant, has risk r^2(1-1/\gamma)+\sigma^2/(\gamma-1). The peak at \gamma=1 is all variance.
  • Regularization removes the peak (3.C): optimally tuned ridge has risk monotone in \gamma. And past the peak, when the signal-to-noise ratio exceeds one, the interpolator can beat predicting zero.
  • Benign overfitting (3.D): when the extra dimensions are many and weak, the interpolator behaves like ridge, and fitting noise exactly costs almost nothing.
  • Which regime are we in? (3.E) A short discussion of where LLMs sit.

The formulas are those of Hastie et al. (reading in 3.F).

Conventions for 3.A–3.C (as in Hastie et al.). The model is y=x^\top\beta+\varepsilon with x,\beta\in\mathbb R^p; there are n training examples, stacked as rows of the design matrix X\in\mathbb R^{n\times p}, and \gamma=p/n. Rows x_i\sim\mathcal N(0,I_p), noise \varepsilon\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma^2), and \|\beta\|^2=r^2, with r,\sigma>0. For an estimate \hat\beta the risk is its squared error on a fresh test input x_0\sim\mathcal N(0,I_p), \mathcal R=\mathbb E\big[(x_0^\top\hat\beta-x_0^\top\beta)^2\big]=\mathbb E\|\hat\beta-\beta\|^2, averaged over the training data. It excludes the irreducible \sigma^2: it is the expected excess risk \mathbb E\,\mathcal E_n of 1.A, \mathcal E_n=R(\hat\beta)-R(\beta), under squared loss. Ridge is \hat\beta_\lambda=(X^\top X+n\lambda I)^{-1}X^\top y. Limits are p,n\to\infty with p/n\to\gamma. The null estimator \hat\beta=0 (“predict zero”) has risk r^2; an estimator that does worse than r^2 is worse than not learning at all.

3.A. Below the threshold

How does the risk of plain least squares grow as the number of parameters approaches the number of samples from below, and why does it blow up?

3.A.1. The left branch

For p<n the least-squares estimator \hat\beta=(X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top y is unique. 1.B.2 computed its exact expected excess risk, \mathbb E\,\mathcal E_n=\sigma^2p/(n-p-1), using the Wishart identity \mathbb E(X^\top X)^{-1}=I_p/(n-p-1).

  1. From 1.B.2 (\mathbb E\,\mathcal E_n=\sigma^2p/(n-p-1)), derive the limit \sigma^2\gamma/(1-\gamma). Explain the divergence in terms of the spectrum of X^\top X/n.
  2. What is the bias of OLS for \gamma<1?
Solution

1. \sigma^2\frac{p}{n-p-1}=\sigma^2\frac{p/n}{1-p/n-1/n}\to\sigma^2\frac{\gamma}{1-\gamma}. The empirical spectrum of X^\top X/n converges to the Marchenko–Pastur law on [(1-\sqrt\gamma)^2,(1+\sqrt\gamma)^2] (quoted). The risk is \frac{\sigma^2}{n}\sum_i1/\lambda_i(X^\top X/n), and as \gamma\to1 the lower edge tends to 0, so the smallest eigenvalues blow up the sum. Consistently, \frac1p\mathrm{Tr}(X^\top X/n)^{-1}\to\frac1{1-\gamma}.

2. Zero: OLS is unbiased whenever X has full column rank (1.B.1). Below the threshold the whole risk is variance.

3.B. Above the threshold: minimum-norm interpolation

With more parameters than samples, least squares no longer has a unique solution: infinitely many coefficient vectors fit the training data exactly. Which one does training pick, and what is its risk? This is the right half of the double-descent curve.

3.B.1. The right branch, bias and variance

For p>n, X has full row rank almost surely, and infinitely many b interpolate the data (Xb=y). X^+ denotes the Moore–Penrose pseudoinverse.

  1. Show that the minimum-norm least-squares solution is \hat\beta=X^+y=X^\top(XX^\top)^{-1}y and that it interpolates. It is also the \lambda\to0^+ limit of ridge, and the limit of gradient descent from 0 (1.D.4.4).
  2. Let P=X^+X be the orthogonal projection onto the row space of X. Show that \hat\beta-\beta=-(I-P)\beta+X^+\varepsilon, with the two terms orthogonal. So \mathcal R=\underbrace{\mathbb E\|(I-P)\beta\|^2}_{\text{conditional bias}^2}+\underbrace{\sigma^2\,\mathbb E\,\mathrm{Tr}(XX^\top)^{-1}}_{\text{variance}}.
  3. Bias. Show that \mathbb E P=(n/p)I, so that \text{bias}^2=r^2(1-n/p)\to r^2(1-1/\gamma).
  4. Variance. XX^\top is an n\times n Wishart matrix with p degrees of freedom. Reuse 1.B.2 with the roles of n and p swapped to get \sigma^2n/(p-n-1)\to\sigma^2/(\gamma-1).
  5. Assemble the full curve for \gamma\in(0,\infty). Describe it near \gamma=1 and as \gamma\to\infty.
Hint

For 3, use rotation invariance: X\to XO for orthogonal O\in\mathbb R^{p\times p}, together with \mathrm{Tr}\,P=n. For 4, XX^\top=\sum_{j=1}^px_{\cdot j}x_{\cdot j}^\top, where the columns x_{\cdot j}\in\mathbb R^n are i.i.d. \mathcal N(0,I_n).

Solution

1. XX^\top is invertible, so X\hat\beta=XX^\top(XX^\top)^{-1}y=y, and \hat\beta interpolates. Any other interpolant is \hat\beta+w with Xw=0. Since \hat\beta\in\mathrm{row}(X)\perp\ker X, we have \|\hat\beta+w\|^2=\|\hat\beta\|^2+\|w\|^2, so \hat\beta is the minimum-norm interpolant.

2. \hat\beta=X^+(X\beta+\varepsilon)=P\beta+X^+\varepsilon, so \hat\beta-\beta=-(I-P)\beta+X^+\varepsilon. The cross term is identically zero: (I-P)\beta lies in the null space and X^+\varepsilon in the row space. Here “bias squared” means the squared bias conditional on X, then averaged over designs, rather than bias after averaging over X. Then \mathbb E\|X^+\varepsilon\|^2=\sigma^2\mathrm{Tr}(X^{+\top}X^+), and X^{+\top}X^+=(XX^\top)^{-1}XX^\top(XX^\top)^{-1}=(XX^\top)^{-1}.

3. For orthogonal O, XO has the law of X, and its row-space projection is O^\top PO. So \mathbb EP=O^\top(\mathbb EP)O for all O, which forces \mathbb EP=cI. Taking traces, \mathrm{Tr}\,P=n gives c=n/p. For any fixed \beta, \mathbb E\|(I-P)\beta\|^2=\beta^\top(I-\mathbb EP)\beta=r^2(1-n/p). The interpolator sees only the n-dimensional projection of \beta onto a random subspace, and it misses the rest.

4. For p>n+1, by 1.B.2.1 with (n,p)\to(p,n): \mathbb E(XX^\top)^{-1}=I_n/(p-n-1), so the variance is \sigma^2n/(p-n-1)\to\sigma^2/(\gamma-1). (Hastie et al. state this branch as part of their Theorem 1. In the isotropic Gaussian case it is a two-line consequence of the same Wishart fact.)

5.

\mathcal R(\gamma)=\begin{cases}\sigma^2\dfrac{\gamma}{1-\gamma},&\gamma<1,\\[2mm] r^2\Big(1-\dfrac1\gamma\Big)+\dfrac{\sigma^2}{\gamma-1},&\gamma>1.\end{cases}

The risk starts at 0, rises, and diverges at \gamma=1 from both sides; the divergence is entirely variance. Past the threshold, the bias grows from 0 toward r^2 while the variance falls from \infty toward 0. As \gamma\to\infty the risk tends to r^2, the null risk: the projection captures a vanishing fraction of \beta, and the noise is spread over so many directions that it does no harm. The “second descent” is the variance falling faster than the bias rises.

3.C. The second descent and the cure

Past the peak the min-norm risk falls again. Does it ever fall below the risk of predicting zero, and is the peak unavoidable, or an artefact of not regularizing?

3.C.1. When does interpolation beat doing nothing, and what removes the peak?

Two facts from §1 are used. Ridge regression, \hat\beta_\lambda=(X^\top X+n\lambda I)^{-1}X^\top y, is the posterior mean of \beta under the Gaussian prior \beta\sim\mathcal N(0,\tau^2I) and Gaussian noise when \lambda=\sigma^2/(n\tau^2) (1.D.1.1); and the posterior mean minimizes the expected squared error averaged over the prior. The minimum-norm interpolant is the \lambda\to0^+ limit of ridge. Write \lambda^*=\sigma^2\gamma/r^2; part 2 shows it is the optimal ridge penalty in this model (“optimally tuned ridge”).

Source for 3: Nakkiran, Venkat, Kakade and Ma (2020) prove that optimally tuned ridge is monotone in sample size and model size in isotropic linear regression.

  1. For \gamma>1, minimize r^2(1-1/\gamma)+\sigma^2/(\gamma-1). Show that if r>\sigma (signal-to-noise ratio r^2/\sigma^2>1), the minimum is at \gamma=r/(r-\sigma), with value 2r\sigma-\sigma^2<r^2. Show that if r\le\sigma, the risk decreases monotonically toward r^2 from above.
  2. Show that \lambda^*=\sigma^2\gamma/r^2 is 1.D.1’s \lambda=\sigma^2/(n\tau^2) for the prior \beta\sim\mathcal N(0,(r^2/p)I), which has \mathbb E\|\beta\|^2=r^2. Hence ridge at \lambda^* is the posterior mean, the Bayes-optimal estimator under squared loss for that prior.
  3. Use 2 to argue that (a) the optimal-ridge risk is at most the min-norm risk at every \gamma, and (b) at fixed p the optimal-ridge risk is non-increasing in n. Deduce that the limiting optimal-ridge curve is non-decreasing in \gamma, so it has no peak.
Hint

For 3(a), both estimators are rotation-equivariant (X\to XO, \beta\to O^\top\beta), so each one’s risk depends on \beta only through \|\beta\|. For 3(b), what can a Bayes estimator with n+1 samples always do that guarantees it is no worse than with n?

Solution

1. \frac{d}{d\gamma}\big[r^2(1-1/\gamma)+\sigma^2/(\gamma-1)\big]=\frac{r^2}{\gamma^2}-\frac{\sigma^2}{(\gamma-1)^2}. This vanishes when \frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma}=\frac\sigma r, i.e. \gamma^\star=\frac{1}{1-\sigma/r}=\frac{r}{r-\sigma}, which requires r>\sigma. There 1-1/\gamma^\star=\sigma/r and \gamma^\star-1=\sigma/(r-\sigma), so

\mathcal R(\gamma^\star)=r\sigma+\sigma(r-\sigma)=2r\sigma-\sigma^2=r^2-(r-\sigma)^2<r^2 .

The derivative is negative before \gamma^\star and positive after, and \mathcal R\to r^2 from below as \gamma\to\infty. So for SNR above one, the interpolator beats the null estimator over a whole range of \gamma. If r\le\sigma, then \frac{\gamma-1}{\gamma}<1\le\frac\sigma r, the derivative is negative everywhere on (1,\infty), and the risk falls toward r^2 without crossing it: the interpolator never beats predicting zero.

In this model the global minimum over all \gamma is still at \gamma\to0, because r^2 is held fixed as p grows, so each added feature carries less signal. Whether adding features can win globally depends on whether they carry new signal. Hastie et al. (§5) study a misspecified variant in which they do, and there, for SNR above one, the global minimum can lie at \gamma>1.

2. With \tau^2=r^2/p: \lambda=\frac{\sigma^2}{n\tau^2}=\frac{\sigma^2p}{nr^2}=\frac{\sigma^2\gamma}{r^2}=\lambda^*. By 1.D.1.1, ridge at \lambda^* is the posterior mean, which minimizes \mathbb E\|b-\beta\|^2 over all estimators under that prior. (This is also 1.C.2.3’s best common penalty n\lambda=\sigma^2/\tau^2.)

3. (a) Ridge and min-norm are both rotation-equivariant, and the design is rotation-invariant, so each one’s risk at fixed \beta depends only on \|\beta\|. Averaged over the prior, ridge(\lambda^*) is no worse than any estimator. For either of these linear estimators, the noise- and design-averaged risk has the form A\|\beta\|^2+B: isotropy makes the squared-bias matrix a scalar multiple of I, and the noise variance is independent of \beta. Thus averaging over a prior with \mathbb E\|\beta\|^2=r^2 gives exactly its risk at any fixed vector of norm r, already at finite p (when the risks are finite). The Bayes comparison therefore gives the fixed-radius comparison too. This does not make ridge Bayes-optimal under the uniform-on-a-sphere prior among all estimators.

(b) A Bayes estimator given n+1 samples can ignore the last one, so its Bayes risk is at most that of the Bayes estimator with n samples. More data never hurts the posterior mean. The limiting curve depends on \gamma alone, and at fixed p increasing n decreases \gamma, so the optimal-ridge risk is non-decreasing in \gamma. There is no peak.

The double-descent peak is therefore a property of the minimum-norm estimator, a variance divergence at p\approx n that properly tuned regularization removes, and not a property of the problem.

3.C.2. The curves at two signal-to-noise ratios

For r^2/\sigma^2=5 and for r^2/\sigma^2=0.5, use 3.C.1.1 to locate the min-norm risk’s minimum on \gamma>1, if it has one, and say whether the curve crosses the null risk r^2. Then sketch both curves against \gamma on a log axis, with r^2 as a horizontal line, the bias and variance contributions shaded, and optimal ridge on the same axes. The interactive figure below draws all of these.

Solution

What a correct sketch shows. For both SNRs, the curve starts at 0, rises as \sigma^2\gamma/(1-\gamma), and has a vertical asymptote at \gamma=1, entirely from variance; the bias is zero for \gamma<1.

  • For SNR 5 (r\approx2.24\sigma): past the peak the curve falls through r^2, has its minimum at \gamma=r/(r-\sigma)\approx1.81 with value 2r\sigma-\sigma^2\approx3.47\sigma^2<r^2=5\sigma^2, and then climbs back to r^2 from below.
  • For SNR 0.5: past the peak the curve falls toward r^2 from above and never crosses it.

Optimal ridge is a smooth, monotone, non-decreasing curve that lies below min-norm everywhere and tends to r^2 as \gamma\to\infty.

▶ Interactive figure: double_descent.html. Turn on the bias/variance decomposition and check which term makes the peak. Toggle optimal ridge λ* = σ²γ/r². Drag the SNR across 1 and watch whether the min-norm curve dips below the null risk r². The dots are finite-n simulations: note how they scatter near γ = 1.

3.D. Benign overfitting: a junk tail acts as ridge

The question. Deep networks can fit their training data exactly, noisy labels included, and still predict well. The usual intuition says that a fit passing through every noisy point has absorbed the noise and must pay for it at test time. In the simplest linear model that intuition is right. Take y=x^\top\beta+\varepsilon with all p features i.i.d. \mathcal N(0,1), noise variance \sigma^2, and n samples with p>n+1. The minimum-norm interpolant then has expected excess risk \|\beta\|^2(1-n/p)+\sigma^2n/(p-n-1) (3.B.1: squared bias plus variance), which diverges as p\downarrow n+1 and tends to the risk of predicting zero as p\to\infty. In that model interpolation can beat predicting zero (3.C.1), but at every fixed ratio p/n>1 its excess risk stays bounded away from zero, however large n is. Benign overfitting is when an interpolant has zero training error on noisy labels and an excess test risk close to the best achievable. When does that happen?

What you get. An exactly solvable model in which you can see the mechanism. Suppose the features beyond the useful ones form a tail: many directions, each with small variance. Then the minimum-norm interpolant, restricted to the useful features, is nearly ridge regression, with the tail supplying the penalty. The noise the tail memorizes costs about (training residual)/m at test time, where m is the tail dimension. Two conditions fall out: the tail must be high-dimensional compared with n, and its total variance must be small compared with n. These are the conditions of Bartlett, Long, Lugosi and Tsigler’s characterization, specialized to this model.

Setup.

  • Features x=(x_s,x_t), with signal x_s\in\mathbb R^k, x_s\sim\mathcal N(0,I_k), and tail x_t\in\mathbb R^m, x_t\sim\mathcal N(0,\nu I_m), independent of x_s. The per-coordinate tail variance \nu is small. The tail’s total variance is c=m\nu=\mathbb E\|x_t\|^2.
  • Labels y=x_s^\top\beta_s+\varepsilon, with noise \varepsilon\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma^2) independent of x. The tail carries no signal. Write r^2=\|\beta_s\|^2.
  • n i.i.d. samples with k<n<m. Stack them as X=[X_s,\,X_t], with X_s\in\mathbb R^{n\times k}, X_t\in\mathbb R^{n\times m}, and labels y\in\mathbb R^n.
  • X has more columns than rows, so many b satisfy Xb=y. The minimum-norm interpolant is \hat\beta=X^\top(XX^\top)^{-1}y (XX^\top is invertible almost surely because m\ge n). It is the \lambda\to0^+ limit of ridge, and the point to which gradient descent from 0 converges. Split it as \hat\beta=(\hat\beta_s,\hat\beta_t).
  • The excess risk of a coefficient vector b=(b_s,b_t) is \mathcal R(b)=\mathbb E\big[(x^\top b-x_s^\top\beta_s)^2\big] over a fresh x; it excludes the irreducible \sigma^2. Because x_s and x_t are independent with covariances I_k and \nu I_m, \mathcal R(b)=\|b_s-\beta_s\|^2+\nu\|b_t\|^2 .
  • For comparison, ridge on the signal features alone is \hat\beta^{\rm R}_\lambda=\arg\min_{b\in\mathbb R^k}\ \tfrac1n\|y-X_sb\|^2+\lambda\|b\|^2=(X_s^\top X_s+n\lambda I_k)^{-1}X_s^\top y .

3.D.1. The tail supplies a ridge penalty
  1. Show that \hat\beta_s=X_s^\top(X_sX_s^\top+X_tX_t^\top)^{-1}y and \hat\beta_t=X_t^\top(X_sX_s^\top+X_tX_t^\top)^{-1}y. Prove the push-through identity X_s^\top(X_sX_s^\top+cI_n)^{-1}=(X_s^\top X_s+cI_k)^{-1}X_s^\top for c>0. Conclude that if X_tX_t^\top were exactly cI_n, then \hat\beta_s would be \hat\beta^{\rm R}_\lambda with n\lambda=c.
  2. Write the columns of X_t as \sqrt\nu\,z_j, j=1,\dots,m, with z_j\sim\mathcal N(0,I_n) i.i.d., so X_tX_t^\top=\nu W with W=\sum_jz_jz_j^\top. Show that \mathbb E\,W/m=I_n, that the diagonal entries of W/m have variance 2/m and the off-diagonal ones 1/m. So X_tX_t^\top\to cI_n entry by entry as m\to\infty at fixed c, by the law of large numbers.
  3. Entry by entry is not enough, because there are n^2 entries. For a fixed unit vector v\in\mathbb R^n, show that \mathbb E\|(W/m-I_n)v\|^2=(n+1)/m. So W/m moves a typical vector by about \sqrt{n/m}, and X_tX_t^\top\approx cI_n needs m\gg n, not just m large.
  4. Quoted (Davidson–Szarek; Vershynin’s notes, Corollary 5.35): with probability at least 1-2e^{-t^2/2}, every singular value of the n\times m matrix [z_1,\dots,z_m] lies in [\sqrt m-\sqrt n-t,\ \sqrt m+\sqrt n+t]. With d=\sqrt{n/m}+t/\sqrt m<1, deduce that every eigenvalue of X_tX_t^\top lies in [c(1-d)^2,\ c(1+d)^2], and that the smallest eigenvalue of XX^\top is at least c(1-d)^2. Why does this lower bound matter, given that the variance of the minimum-norm interpolant involves (XX^\top)^{-1}?
Hint

For 1, split X^\top=\begin{pmatrix}X_s^\top\\ X_t^\top\end{pmatrix} and note XX^\top=X_sX_s^\top+X_tX_t^\top. For the identity, start from (X_s^\top X_s+cI_k)X_s^\top=X_s^\top(X_sX_s^\top+cI_n). For 2, (W/m)_{il}=\frac1m\sum_jz_{ij}z_{lj} is an average of m i.i.d. terms; \mathbb Ez^4=3 for a standard normal. For 3, by rotation invariance take v=e_1, so (W/m)v=\frac1m\sum_jz_jz_{1j}. Expand \mathbb E\|(W/m)v\|^2 as a double sum over j,l and separate j=l from j\ne l. For 4, the eigenvalues of X_tX_t^\top are \nu times the squared singular values; X_sX_s^\top\succeq0.

Solution

1. \hat\beta=X^\top(XX^\top)^{-1}y with X^\top=\begin{pmatrix}X_s^\top\\ X_t^\top\end{pmatrix}, so the two blocks are X_s^\top(XX^\top)^{-1}y and X_t^\top(XX^\top)^{-1}y, and XX^\top=X_sX_s^\top+X_tX_t^\top. For the identity, both sides of (X_s^\top X_s+cI_k)X_s^\top=X_s^\top X_sX_s^\top+cX_s^\top=X_s^\top(X_sX_s^\top+cI_n) are equal. Multiply on the left by (X_s^\top X_s+cI_k)^{-1} and on the right by (X_sX_s^\top+cI_n)^{-1}; both inverses exist because c>0. If X_tX_t^\top=cI_n, then \hat\beta_s=X_s^\top(X_sX_s^\top+cI_n)^{-1}y=(X_s^\top X_s+cI_k)^{-1}X_s^\top y=\hat\beta^{\rm R}_{\lambda},\qquad n\lambda=c . The interpolant never sees a penalty. The tail’s Gram matrix plays the role of n\lambda I_n inside the “kernel” form of ridge.

2. \mathbb E\,z_jz_j^\top=I_n, so \mathbb E W/m=I_n. A diagonal entry \frac1m\sum_jz_{ij}^2 has variance \frac1m\mathrm{Var}(z^2)=\frac1m(3-1)=\frac2m. An off-diagonal entry \frac1m\sum_jz_{ij}z_{lj} (i\ne l) has mean 0 and variance \frac1m\mathbb E z_{ij}^2z_{lj}^2=\frac1m. Each entry is an average of m i.i.d. terms, so it converges to the corresponding entry of I_n, and X_tX_t^\top=\nu W=c\,W/m\to cI_n.

3. With v=e_1, (W/m)v=\frac1m\sum_jz_jz_{1j}, and \mathbb E\|(W/m)v\|^2=\frac1{m^2}\sum_{j,l}\mathbb E\big[z_{1j}z_{1l}\,z_j^\top z_l\big]. For j\ne l the factors are independent and \mathbb E[z_{1j}z_j]=e_1, so each of the m(m-1) terms is e_1^\top e_1=1. For j=l, \mathbb E\big[z_{1j}^2\|z_j\|^2\big]=\mathbb Ez_1^4+(n-1)=n+2, for m terms. So \mathbb E\|(W/m)v\|^2=1+(n+1)/m. Since \mathbb E\,v^\top(W/m)v=1, \mathbb E\|(W/m-I)v\|^2=\mathbb E\|(W/m)v\|^2-2\,\mathbb E\,v^\top(W/m)v+1=\frac{n+1}{m}. Summing over v=e_1,\dots,e_n gives \mathbb E\|W/m-I\|_F^2=n(n+1)/m, which matches 2: n\cdot\frac2m+n(n-1)\cdot\frac1m. The individual entries are O(1/\sqrt m), but their effect on a vector adds up over n coordinates. The operator norm \|W/m-I\| is about 2\sqrt{n/m} (part 4), so the approximation X_tX_t^\top\approx cI_n is controlled by n/m.

4. The eigenvalues of W are the squared singular values, so on the stated event they lie in [(\sqrt m-\sqrt n-t)^2,(\sqrt m+\sqrt n+t)^2]=m[(1-d)^2,(1+d)^2]. Multiplying by \nu and using m\nu=c puts the eigenvalues of X_tX_t^\top in [c(1-d)^2,c(1+d)^2]. Since X_sX_s^\top\succeq0, XX^\top\succeq X_tX_t^\top, and so \lambda_{\min}(XX^\top)\ge c(1-d)^2, equivalently \|(XX^\top)^{-1}\|\le1/\big(c(1-d)^2\big). (With n=100, m=20000 the edges are near (1\pm0.07)^2\approx0.86,\,1.15, and a simulation agrees.)

The noise enters the interpolant as X^\top(XX^\top)^{-1}\varepsilon, so its size is governed by the small eigenvalues of XX^\top. In the isotropic model with p\approx n, XX^\top has eigenvalues near zero, and that is where the divergence of the risk at p=n comes from. A high-dimensional tail puts a floor of about c under every eigenvalue of XX^\top, which is exactly what an explicit ridge penalty n\lambda=c does.

3.D.2. What the memorized noise costs, and when overfitting is benign

Same setup: signal x_s\sim\mathcal N(0,I_k), tail x_t\sim\mathcal N(0,\nu I_m) with c=m\nu, labels y=x_s^\top\beta_s+\varepsilon, minimum-norm interpolant \hat\beta=(\hat\beta_s,\hat\beta_t)=X^\top(XX^\top)^{-1}y, excess risk \mathcal R(\hat\beta)=\|\hat\beta_s-\beta_s\|^2+\nu\|\hat\beta_t\|^2. Write u=(XX^\top)^{-1}y\in\mathbb R^n, so \hat\beta_s=X_s^\top u and \hat\beta_t=X_t^\top u.

  1. Idealized tail. Suppose X_tX_t^\top=cI_n exactly. Show that u=(y-X_s\hat\beta_s)/c. Deduce that on the training inputs the tail’s predictions X_t\hat\beta_t are exactly the ridge residuals y-X_s\hat\beta^{\rm R}_\lambda (n\lambda=c), and that the tail’s contribution to the test risk is \nu\|\hat\beta_t\|^2=\frac{\|y-X_s\hat\beta^{\rm R}_\lambda\|^2}{m}. Why can the tail reproduce residuals of size \sigma at every training point while contributing so little at a fresh point?
  2. Real tail. Let \mu_{\min},\mu_{\max} be the extreme eigenvalues of X_tX_t^\top. Show that \nu\|\hat\beta_t\|^2\le\nu\mu_{\max}\|y\|^2/\mu_{\min}^2. Using 3.D.1.4, conclude that \nu\|\hat\beta_t\|^2\le\frac{\|y\|^2}{m}\cdot\frac{(1+d)^2}{(1-d)^4} with high probability, where d\approx\sqrt{n/m}. With \mathbb E\|y\|^2=n(r^2+\sigma^2), the tail’s cost is O\big((r^2+\sigma^2)\,n/m\big), which tends to 0 as m\to\infty at fixed c.
  3. Pure-noise tail. Take k=0, so y=\varepsilon\sim\mathcal N(0,\sigma^2I_n) and X=X_t. Using the quoted fact \mathbb E\,W^{-1}=I_n/(m-n-1) for W=\sum_{j=1}^mz_jz_j^\top, z_j\sim\mathcal N(0,I_n) i.i.d., m>n+1, show that \mathbb E\,\nu\|\hat\beta_t\|^2=\sigma^2n/(m-n-1). This does not depend on \nu. What happens as m\downarrow n+1? Is it the small per-coordinate variance \nu, or the dimension m, that makes memorizing noise cheap?
  4. Assemble. Argue that as m\to\infty at fixed c, \hat\beta_s\to\hat\beta^{\rm R}_\lambda with n\lambda=c, and hence \mathcal R(\hat\beta) tends to the ridge risk \|\hat\beta^{\rm R}_\lambda-\beta_s\|^2, while the training error stays exactly zero. For n\gg k, use X_s^\top X_s\approx nI_k to show that the ridge risk, averaged over the noise, is approximately \frac{c^2r^2+\sigma^2nk}{(n+c)^2}\approx\frac{\sigma^2k}{n}+\frac{c^2r^2}{n^2}\quad(c\ll n). Combine with 1 to write the total excess risk as roughly \sigma^2k/n+c^2r^2/n^2+\sigma^2(n-k)/m. State in words the two conditions under which interpolation is benign, i.e. the excess risk tends to 0 as n\to\infty.
  5. Contrast with the isotropic model. All p features \mathcal N(0,1) and n<p; the minimum-norm excess risk is \|\beta\|^2(1-n/p)+\sigma^2n/(p-n-1) (quoted, for p>n+1). Think of the features beyond the first few as a tail with \nu=1. Which of the two conditions fails near p\approx n, and which fails for p\gg n? Why can no choice of p satisfy both?
  6. Effective ranks. For a covariance \Sigma with eigenvalues \lambda_1\ge\lambda_2\ge\dots, Bartlett et al. define r_j(\Sigma)=\sum_{i>j}\lambda_i/\lambda_{j+1} and R_j(\Sigma)=\big(\sum_{i>j}\lambda_i\big)^2/\sum_{i>j}\lambda_i^2, and k^*=\min\{j\ge0:r_j(\Sigma)\ge bn\} for a constant b>1. They call a sequence of problems benign when r_0(\Sigma)/n\to0, k^*/n\to0 and n/R_{k^*}(\Sigma)\to0. Compute these for this model (assume \nu<1, k+c<bn\le m) and check that the three conditions are the ones you found in 4.
Hint

For 1, write out (X_sX_s^\top+cI_n)u=y and recognize X_s^\top u. For the tail cost, \|\hat\beta_t\|^2=u^\top X_tX_t^\top u. For 2, \lambda_{\min}(XX^\top)\ge\mu_{\min} because X_sX_s^\top\succeq0, so \|u\|\le\|y\|/\mu_{\min}. For 3, average over \varepsilon first: \mathbb E\,\varepsilon^\top A\varepsilon=\sigma^2\mathrm{Tr}A. For 4, a ridge estimate with X_s^\top X_s=nI_k shrinks each coordinate of the signal-only least-squares estimate (X_s^\top X_s)^{-1}X_s^\top y by n/(n+c); add the squared bias and the variance. For the convergence of \hat\beta_s, write X_tX_t^\top=c(I+\Delta) with \|\Delta\|\to0 and use \|(A+E)^{-1}-A^{-1}\|\le\|A^{-1}\|\,\|E\|\,\|(A+E)^{-1}\|.

Solution

1. u solves (X_sX_s^\top+cI_n)u=y, i.e. cu=y-X_s(X_s^\top u)=y-X_s\hat\beta_s. By 3.D.1.1, \hat\beta_s=\hat\beta^{\rm R}_\lambda with n\lambda=c. On the training inputs the tail predicts X_t\hat\beta_t=X_tX_t^\top u=cu=y-X_s\hat\beta^{\rm R}_\lambda: the signal block makes the ridge fit, and the tail adds exactly the ridge residual, so the total fit interpolates. The tail’s test cost is \nu\|\hat\beta_t\|^2=\nu\,u^\top X_tX_t^\top u=\nu c\|u\|^2=\frac{\nu}{c}\|y-X_s\hat\beta^{\rm R}_\lambda\|^2=\frac{\|y-X_s\hat\beta^{\rm R}_\lambda\|^2}{m}. For n\gg k, the expected squared ridge residual is about \sigma^2(n-k)+c^2r^2/n (noise the signal block cannot fit, plus the shrinkage bias), so for c\ll n the tail costs about \sigma^2(n-k)/m.

Why it is cheap: \hat\beta_t=\sum_iu_i\,x_{t,i} is a combination of the n training tail vectors. These are nearly orthogonal in \mathbb R^m (here exactly orthogonal, each of squared norm c), so x_{t,i}^\top\hat\beta_t=cu_i reproduces residual i at training point i. A fresh x_t is nearly orthogonal to all of them, and x_t^\top\hat\beta_t has variance \nu\|\hat\beta_t\|^2. The fitted function is the ridge fit plus a narrow spike at each training point, and the spikes occupy a vanishing fraction of the m-dimensional tail.

2. \nu\|\hat\beta_t\|^2=\nu\,u^\top X_tX_t^\top u\le\nu\mu_{\max}\|u\|^2. Because XX^\top\succeq X_tX_t^\top, \lambda_{\min}(XX^\top)\ge\mu_{\min}, so \|u\|=\|(XX^\top)^{-1}y\|\le\|y\|/\mu_{\min}. On the event of 3.D.1.4, \mu_{\min}\ge c(1-d)^2 and \mu_{\max}\le c(1+d)^2, so \nu\|\hat\beta_t\|^2\le\frac{\nu c(1+d)^2}{c^2(1-d)^4}\|y\|^2=\frac{\|y\|^2}{m}\cdot\frac{(1+d)^2}{(1-d)^4}. With \|y\|^2\approx n(r^2+\sigma^2) this is O\big((r^2+\sigma^2)n/m\big). The bound uses \|y\|^2 where 1 had the smaller ridge residual. In simulation (k=5, n=100, m=20000, \sigma=1, r^2=5) the tail cost matches \|y-X_s\hat\beta^{\rm R}_\lambda\|^2/m\approx0.005 to within 1%, against a bound of about 0.05.

3. With k=0, \nu\|\hat\beta_t\|^2=\nu\,\varepsilon^\top(X_tX_t^\top)^{-1}X_tX_t^\top(X_tX_t^\top)^{-1}\varepsilon=\nu\,\varepsilon^\top(\nu W)^{-1}\varepsilon=\varepsilon^\top W^{-1}\varepsilon. Averaging over \varepsilon and then over W, \mathbb E\,\nu\|\hat\beta_t\|^2=\sigma^2\,\mathbb E\,\mathrm{Tr}\,W^{-1}=\frac{\sigma^2n}{m-n-1}. The \nu cancels: rescaling the tail rescales \hat\beta_t inversely, and the test cost is scale-free. As m\downarrow n+1 the cost diverges; this is the double-descent peak again, now inside the tail. For m\gg n it is about \sigma^2n/m. So what makes memorization cheap is the dimension of the tail relative to n. A small \nu is needed for a different reason (part 4): to keep the total c=m\nu, and hence the implicit penalty, small.

4. Write X_tX_t^\top=c(I+\Delta), where \|\Delta\|\le(1+d)^2-1\approx2\sqrt{n/m}\to0, and A=X_sX_s^\top+cI_n\succeq cI_n. Then \hat\beta_s-\hat\beta^{\rm R}_\lambda=X_s^\top\big[(A+c\Delta)^{-1}-A^{-1}\big]y, and \|(A+c\Delta)^{-1}-A^{-1}\|\le\frac1c\cdot c\|\Delta\|\cdot\frac1{c(1-\|\Delta\|)}\to0. So \hat\beta_s\to\hat\beta^{\rm R}_\lambda. With 2, \mathcal R(\hat\beta)=\|\hat\beta_s-\beta_s\|^2+\nu\|\hat\beta_t\|^2\to\|\hat\beta^{\rm R}_\lambda-\beta_s\|^2. The training error is 0 for every m, since \hat\beta interpolates.

Ridge risk: if X_s^\top X_s=nI_k, then \hat\beta^{\rm R}_\lambda=\frac{1}{n+c}X_s^\top y=\frac{n}{n+c}\beta_s+\frac1{n+c}X_s^\top\varepsilon. The bias is -\frac{c}{n+c}\beta_s, and the noise term has covariance \frac{\sigma^2n}{(n+c)^2}I_k. Adding squared bias and variance, \mathbb E_\varepsilon\|\hat\beta^{\rm R}_\lambda-\beta_s\|^2=\frac{c^2r^2+\sigma^2nk}{(n+c)^2}. For c\ll n this is \approx\sigma^2k/n+c^2r^2/n^2. (The fluctuations of X_s^\top X_s around nI_k are of relative size \sqrt{k/n} and change this by a factor 1+O(k/n) on the variance term.) In total, \mathcal R(\hat\beta)\approx\underbrace{\frac{\sigma^2k}{n}}_{\text{signal variance}}+\underbrace{\frac{c^2r^2}{n^2}}_{\text{tail-as-ridge bias}}+\underbrace{\frac{\sigma^2(n-k)}{m}}_{\text{memorized noise}} . In simulation with k=5, n=100, m=20000: for c=10, 0.086 against ridge’s 0.082 (and \sigma^2k/n=0.05); for c=50, both \approx0.62, dominated by the bias. At m=200 (still c=10) the risk is about 1.0, of which 0.92 is the tail term: a tail only twice as wide as n is not benign.

Interpolation is benign (\mathcal R\to0 as n\to\infty) when, besides k\ll n:

  • The tail is high-dimensional: m\gg n. The tail then absorbs the noise in nearly orthogonal directions at a cost \approx\sigma^2n/m, and its Gram matrix is close to a multiple of the identity, so it acts as an isotropic ridge.
  • The tail is small in total: c=m\nu\ll n, i.e. the implicit penalty \lambda=c/n is small compared with the signal eigenvalue 1. Otherwise the tail-as-ridge over-shrinks the signal.

Both together require \nu\ll n/m\ll1: very many, very weak directions.

5. In the isotropic model every direction has variance 1, so a tail of m\approx p features has c\approx p>n. Near p\approx n the tail is not high-dimensional: \sigma^2n/(p-n-1) is part 3’s formula, and it diverges. For p\gg n the tail is high-dimensional, but its total variance c\approx p\gg n makes the implicit ridge so strong that the fit captures only the fraction n/p of \beta; the risk tends to \|\beta\|^2(1-n/p)\to\|\beta\|^2, the risk of predicting zero. Because \nu=1 is fixed, m\gg n forces c=m\gg n, so the two conditions cannot hold together. (In the isotropic model the signal is also spread over all directions, so this “tail” is not junk; the analogy is about the variance and shrinkage mechanisms, not an exact match.)

6. The eigenvalues are 1 (k times) then \nu (m times). r_0=(k+m\nu)/1=k+c. For j<k, r_j=k-j+c<bn, and r_k=m\nu/\nu=m\ge bn, so k^*=k. Then R_k=(m\nu)^2/(m\nu^2)=m. The benign conditions become (k+c)/n\to0, k/n\to0 and n/m\to0: small tail total (c\ll n), few signal directions, and a high-dimensional tail (m\gg n). Their variance bound \sigma^2(k^*/n+n/R_{k^*})=\sigma^2(k/n+n/m) matches the first and third terms in 4. For a non-isotropic tail, r_k and R_k are the right notions of “how many dimensions the tail effectively has”.
