The same “the tail acts as ridge” mechanism is why kernel “ridgeless” regression on high-dimensional inputs can generalize: the many small eigenvalues of the kernel matrix act as an implicit ridge penalty (Liang and Rakhlin 2020).

Benign overfitting in linear regression, Peter L. Bartlett, Philip M. Long, Gábor Lugosi, Alexander Tsigler (PNAS, 2020) Importance ●●●●○ Length ●●●○○

Non-asymptotic upper and lower bounds on the excess risk of the minimum-norm interpolant, for any covariance, in terms of two effective ranks of the covariance. Interpolation is near-optimal only if the number of directions unimportant for prediction greatly exceeds n while their total variance stays small compared with n. Read §2–§3: Definition 3 (effective ranks), Theorem 4 (the bounds), and Theorem 6, whose part 2 (a few strong directions plus small isotropic noise in p ≫ n dimensions) is the model of 3.D with decaying rather than equal signal eigenvalues. §4 discusses the relevance to deep networks. The proofs in §5 are long and can be skipped.

3.E. What regime are we in?

The question. Is the proportional regime of this section, p/n=O(1), the one modern models are in? Classical asymptotics (and all of §4) fix p and send n\to\infty; 3.A–3.D let p and n grow together. Chinchilla’s compute-optimal allocation, of roughly 20 training tokens per parameter, puts LLM pretraining at p/n\approx1/20 if a token counted as a sample: neither p\ll n nor p\gg n. Two cautions. Tokens are not i.i.d. regression samples. And pretraining usually runs for about one epoch, so the training loss is never driven to zero, and nothing interpolates. Networks show double-descent-like curves in width, training time and sample size (Nakkiran et al. 2019), typically with label noise, and these are weakened by regularization and early stopping. That phenomenology is consistent with the variance mechanism derived here, but it is not derived from it.

3.E.1. Question

Using 1.C.1, describe a measurement that would tell you whether a network’s double-descent peak is a variance divergence of the kind derived here.

Solution

Train several networks at the same size on independent data draws (or with different seeds and different label noise) near the apparent peak. Decompose the test loss into the loss of the ensemble average (bias plus noise) and the spread across the ensemble (variance), as in 1.C.1. If the peak is a variance divergence, the spread peaks there while the ensemble-average loss does not. Weight decay tuned per size should then flatten the peak. A second check is the spectrum of the relevant kernel or feature covariance at the peak: there should be near-zero eigenvalues.

3.F. Readings

Surprises in high-dimensional ridgeless least squares interpolation, Trevor Hastie, Andrea Montanari, Saharon Rosset, Ryan J. Tibshirani (Annals of Statistics, 2022) Importance ●●●○○ Length ●●●○○

Exact asymptotics, as p/n → γ, for the risk of minimum-norm interpolation and ridge, under isotropic and general covariance, in well-specified and misspecified linear models and a random-features model. Double descent appears in the simplest of these, the peak is a variance divergence, and optimally tuned ridge has no peak. Read §1 (overview and figures) and §3 (isotropic features: Theorem 1 is the formula you derived in 3.B, for general i.i.d. entries rather than only Gaussian ones). §5 on misspecified models shows when the global minimum can sit at γ > 1. §4 (general covariance) and §8 (nonlinear random features) are for those heading to WB5.

4. Bayes, MLE, and generalization measures

The story. Many claims about why networks generalize (“count the parameters”, “flat minima generalize”, “SGD is approximately Bayesian”, “the Bayesian posterior is what matters”) are claims about one classical formula: how much worse a fitted model does on new data than the best model, to leading order in 1/n. This section derives that formula and follows it until it breaks. It is one story in three parts.

  • 4.A. Bayes and MLE generalization error. When there are many more data than parameters, the error of the fitted parameters is approximately Gaussian, and the expected excess risk is \mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C)/2n, built from a curvature matrix H and a gradient-noise matrix C (defined below). For a correctly specified probabilistic model C=H, curvature cancels, and the answer is parameter counting, p/2n: this is the AIC model-selection criterion. The Bayesian side gives BIC, and a comparison of three different things called “the generalization error” (a single fit, the Bayes ensemble, a single posterior draw). The terms are defined just below.
  • 4.B. Does curvature cancel? For trained networks C is not proportional to H. How the two differ, how SGD’s own noise spreads the parameters around a minimum, and whether that spread looks like a Bayesian posterior (it does not, in the measured regime).
  • 4.C. Fantastic generalization measures. A large empirical study of which proposed measures actually predict generalization in networks, read against 4.A and 4.B.

The two matrices. Let \ell(\theta;z) be the loss of parameters \theta\in\mathbb R^p on one example z=(x,y), R(\theta)=\mathbb E_z\ell(\theta;z) the population risk (test loss), \hat R_n(\theta)=\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n\ell(\theta;z_i) the empirical risk (training loss) on n i.i.d. examples, and \theta^* the minimizer of R (as in 1.A).

  • The curvature H=\nabla^2R(\theta^*) is the Hessian of the population loss at the optimum. Its eigenvalues say how fast the loss rises as you move away from \theta^* along each eigenvector: large eigenvalues are sharp directions, small ones flat.
  • The gradient noise C=\mathrm{Cov}_z\big[\nabla_\theta\ell(\theta^*;z)\big] is the covariance of single-example gradients at the optimum. The average gradient there is zero, but individual examples still pull in different directions, and C measures how much. Since the gradient of the training loss at \theta^* is an average of n such terms, it is approximately \mathcal N(0,C/n): this is what makes the fitted parameters wobble from one training set to another.

The link between them and the test loss is the local formula of 1.D.7.1 (a second-order Taylor expansion of R around \theta^*): if a trained parameter is \theta=\theta^*+\delta with spread \Sigma=\mathbb E\,\delta\delta^\top, then, because \nabla R(\theta^*)=0,

\mathbb E\,R(\theta)-R(\theta^*)\approx\tfrac12\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma).

Different learners (ERM, a posterior sample, SGD) differ in their \Sigma, and that is the whole content of 4.A–4.B.

Words used in this section.

  • Log loss, likelihood, MLE. If the model outputs a probability p_\theta(y\mid x), the log loss is \ell=-\log p_\theta(y\mid x), in nats. The log-likelihood of the training set is \log L(\theta)=-n\hat R_n(\theta), so ERM with the log loss is maximum likelihood estimation (MLE). The model is well specified if the data really come from p_{\theta^*} for some \theta^*, and misspecified otherwise.
  • Model-selection criteria score each candidate model by its fitted training loss plus a complexity penalty; you pick the model with the lowest score. AIC (Akaike’s information criterion) is -2\log L(\hat\theta_n)+2p; up to a factor 2n, it estimates the fitted model’s test log loss, assuming the model is well specified (4.A.4). TIC (Takeuchi’s information criterion) replaces p by \mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C) and drops that assumption (4.A.2). BIC (the Bayesian, or Schwarz, information criterion) is -2\log L(\hat\theta_n)+p\log n; it approximates -2\log of the Bayesian evidence and aims to pick the model most likely to be true (4.A.5).
  • Posterior, posterior predictive, temperature. With a prior density \pi(\theta) and the log loss, the Bayesian posterior is \pi_n(\theta)\propto\pi(\theta)\,e^{-n\hat R_n(\theta)} (prior times likelihood). The posterior predictive \bar p_n(y\mid x)=\int p_\theta(y\mid x)\,\pi_n(d\theta) averages the predictions over the posterior: the “Bayes ensemble”. A tempered posterior at temperature T>0 is \pi_T(\theta)\propto\pi(\theta)\,e^{-n\hat R_n(\theta)/T}. T=1 is ordinary Bayes; T>1 widens the posterior, as if there were only n/T data points, and T<1 narrows it. The name comes from statistical physics, where e^{-E/T} is the Boltzmann distribution of energy E at temperature T; here the energy is n\hat R_n.

4.A. Bayes and MLE generalization error

The question. In the classical regime, how large are the excess risk and the gap between test and training loss of the empirical risk minimizer, and how do these compare with what a Bayesian would predict? The answers are the model-selection criteria AIC, TIC and BIC, and the exercises derive each one.

The regular regime. All results in 4.A assume:

  • the number of parameters p is fixed and n\to\infty, and \theta^* is the unique minimizer of R, in the interior of the parameter space;
  • H\succ0 (no flat directions at the optimum), and \hat\theta_n\to\theta^* in probability;
  • enough smoothness and moments for Taylor expansions, the law of large numbers and the central limit theorem.

A model satisfying these is regular. Neural networks are not: they have many more parameters than the regime allows, and their Hessians have flat directions from symmetries. Models whose H is degenerate are called singular; singular learning theory (SLT) is the extension to them, and it appears in 4.A.5–4.A.6 only as a pointer. The value of the regular theory is as an exact reference case.

Notation. The population risk is R(\theta)=\mathbb E_z\ell(\theta;z), with minimizer \theta^*, and ERM is \hat\theta_n=\arg\min\hat R_n, where \hat R_n(\theta)=\frac1n\sum_i\ell(\theta;z_i). The curvature is H=\nabla^2R(\theta^*) and the gradient noise C=\mathrm{Cov}_z[\nabla\ell(\theta^*;z)]. Because \mathbb E\nabla\ell(\theta^*;z)=\nabla R(\theta^*)=0, the gradient noise is also the second moment, C=\mathbb E\big[\nabla\ell(\theta^*;z)\nabla\ell(\theta^*;z)^\top\big]. The optimism of ERM is the expected generalization gap \mathbb E[R(\hat\theta_n)-\hat R_n(\hat\theta_n)] (1.A’s \mathbb E\,\mathrm{gap}_n): how much the training loss flatters the fitted model. The excess risk is \mathbb E\,R(\hat\theta_n)-R(\theta^*): how much worse the fitted model is than the best one in the model.

Sources: the sandwich is due to Huber (1967) and, for misspecified likelihoods, H. White, “Maximum likelihood estimation of misspecified models”, Econometrica (1982). AIC is from Akaike (1974) and BIC from Schwarz (1978); The Elements of Statistical Learning (ESL) §7.4–7.5 and §7.7 derive both in textbook form.

Loss conventions. All excess risks below are in loss units. For squared loss this section uses \ell=\tfrac12(y-f_\theta(x))^2. Its Hessian is \nabla^2\ell=\nabla f\,\nabla f^\top-(y-f)\nabla^2f. The first piece is the Gauss–Newton term (with no factor of 2 in this convention); the second, a residual times a second derivative, has zero population expectation when the model’s mean f_{\theta^*}(x)=\mathbb E[y\mid x] is correct. (1.B used (y-f)^2, which doubles H and quadruples C; \mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C) doubles.) For the log loss \ell=-\log p_\theta(y\mid x), in nats, and a well-specified model, R(\theta)-R(\theta^*)=\mathbb E_x\,\mathrm{KL}\big(p_{\theta^*}(\cdot\mid x)\,\|\,p_\theta(\cdot\mid x)\big), so excess risk is the KL divergence to the truth. (Recall \mathrm{KL}(q\,\|\,p)=\mathbb E_{y\sim q}\log\frac{q(y)}{p(y)}\ge0, with equality iff p=q: the expected extra log loss from predicting with p when the data follow q.)

4.A.1. The sandwich: asymptotic normality of ERM

Why. Everything in 4.A follows from the distribution of the fitted parameters. The result is a Gaussian whose covariance is “sandwiched” between two inverse curvatures, with the gradient noise as the filling.

Starting from the first-order condition \nabla\hat R_n(\hat\theta_n)=0, show that \sqrt n\,(\hat\theta_n-\theta^*)\ \Rightarrow\ \mathcal N\big(0,\ H^{-1}CH^{-1}\big). Here \Rightarrow means convergence in distribution. Classical maximum-likelihood theory instead says that the MLE’s covariance is approximately I^{-1}/n, where the Fisher information I=\mathbb E\big[\nabla\log p_\theta\,\nabla\log p_\theta^\top\big]=-\mathbb E\,\nabla^2\log p_\theta (the two expressions agree for a well-specified model; 4.A.4). Which of H and C does that formula use, and when does it give the wrong answer?

Hint

Taylor-expand \nabla\hat R_n around \theta^*. Apply the law of large numbers to \nabla^2\hat R_n(\theta^*) and the central limit theorem to \sqrt n\,\nabla\hat R_n(\theta^*), then Slutsky’s lemma (if A_n\to A in probability and b_n\Rightarrow b, then A_nb_n\Rightarrow Ab).

Solution

Expanding, 0=\nabla\hat R_n(\hat\theta_n)=\nabla\hat R_n(\theta^*)+\nabla^2\hat R_n(\tilde\theta)\,(\hat\theta_n-\theta^*) for some \tilde\theta between \hat\theta_n and \theta^* (row by row). So \sqrt n(\hat\theta_n-\theta^*)=-\big[\nabla^2\hat R_n(\tilde\theta)\big]^{-1}\sqrt n\,\nabla\hat R_n(\theta^*). \nabla\hat R_n(\theta^*)=\frac1n\sum_i\nabla\ell(\theta^*;z_i) is an average of i.i.d. vectors with mean 0 and covariance C, so by the CLT \sqrt n\,\nabla\hat R_n(\theta^*)\Rightarrow\mathcal N(0,C). By the LLN and consistency, \nabla^2\hat R_n(\tilde\theta)\to H in probability. By Slutsky, the limit is -H^{-1}\mathcal N(0,C)=\mathcal N(0,H^{-1}CH^{-1}). This is the sandwich, with H^{-1} as the bread and C as the filling (Huber; White 1982 for misspecified likelihoods).

The “inverse Fisher information” formula is H^{-1} (or C^{-1}; for the log loss, C is the score form of I and H the Hessian form), which equals the sandwich only when C=H (4.A.4). Misspecification can break this equality, although it need not do so in every model. Example: heteroscedastic noise fitted with a homoscedastic model, as in 4.A.3.

4.A.2. Excess risk, optimism, and a factor of two

Why. Two different numbers summarize how ERM generalizes: its excess risk over the best parameter, and its optimism (how much the training loss flatters it). Both follow from the sandwich, and they differ by exactly a factor of two.

  1. Show that \mathbb E\,R(\hat\theta_n)-R(\theta^*)\approx\frac1{2n}\mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C).
  2. Show that the expected optimism is twice as large: \mathbb E\big[R(\hat\theta_n)-\hat R_n(\hat\theta_n)\big]\approx\frac1n\mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C). This is Takeuchi’s information criterion (TIC): estimate the test loss by \hat R_n(\hat\theta_n)+\frac1n\mathrm{Tr}(\hat H^{-1}\hat C), where \hat H and \hat C are the training-set averages of \nabla^2\ell and of \nabla\ell\,\nabla\ell^\top at \hat\theta_n.
  3. Explain the factor of two in one sentence. Which of the two quantities does a held-out set estimate?
Hint

For 2, expand \hat R_n around its own minimizer \hat\theta_n and evaluate the expansion at \theta^*. Then use \mathbb E\hat R_n(\theta^*)=R(\theta^*).

4.A.3. A concrete misspecified case

Why. When the model is wrong about the noise, “count the parameters” can be off by a large factor; here is a one-parameter example where it is off by 3.

Let x\sim\mathcal N(0,1) and y=\beta x+\varepsilon, where \varepsilon\mid x\sim\mathcal N(0,\tau^2x^2). The noise is heteroscedastic: its variance depends on x. Fit y\approx\theta x with \ell=\tfrac12(y-\theta x)^2.

  1. Compute H, C, the asymptotic variance of \hat\theta_n, the expected excess risk and the expected optimism.
  2. Compare each with what you get by assuming homoscedastic noise of the same average variance, \mathbb E\varepsilon^2=\tau^2, i.e. with C=\tau^2H. By what factor is the naive answer wrong?
Hint

At \theta^*=\beta the per-sample gradient is -\varepsilon x. For a standard normal, \mathbb E x^4=3.

Solution

4.A.2.1. By 4.A.1, \delta=\hat\theta_n-\theta^* has \mathbb E\,\delta\delta^\top\approx\Sigma_n:=H^{-1}CH^{-1}/n. By the 1.D.7 formula, the expected excess risk is \tfrac12\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma_n)=\frac1{2n}\mathrm{Tr}(HH^{-1}CH^{-1})=\frac1{2n}\mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C).

4.A.2.2. Expand \hat R_n around its minimizer, where its gradient vanishes: \hat R_n(\theta^*)\approx\hat R_n(\hat\theta_n)+\tfrac12\delta^\top\hat H\delta, with \hat H=\nabla^2\hat R_n\approx H. Take expectations, using \mathbb E\hat R_n(\theta^*)=R(\theta^*) because \theta^* does not depend on the sample: \mathbb E\hat R_n(\hat\theta_n)\approx R(\theta^*)-\tfrac1{2n}\mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C). Subtracting this from 1 gives \mathbb E[R(\hat\theta_n)-\hat R_n(\hat\theta_n)]\approx\big(\tfrac1{2n}+\tfrac1{2n}\big)\mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C)=\frac1n\mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C).

4.A.2.3. The test loss of \hat\theta_n is above R(\theta^*) by \mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C)/2n, and its training loss is below R(\theta^*) by the same amount, because \hat\theta_n was fitted to that sample’s noise. The optimism spans both. A held-out set estimates R(\hat\theta_n), so held-out minus training loss estimates the optimism. Held-out loss cannot give the excess risk without the unknown R(\theta^*), although differences of excess risk between two models are measurable.

4.A.3.1. \nabla\ell=-(y-\theta x)x, so H=\mathbb E x^2=1. At \theta^*=\beta, \nabla\ell=-\varepsilon x, so C=\mathbb E[\varepsilon^2x^2]=\mathbb E[\tau^2x^2\cdot x^2]=3\tau^2, using \mathbb E x^4=3. Hence:

  • \mathrm{Var}\,\hat\theta_n\approx H^{-1}CH^{-1}/n=3\tau^2/n;
  • excess risk \approx\mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C)/2n=3\tau^2/2n;
  • optimism \approx3\tau^2/n.

4.A.3.2. The homoscedastic formulas use C=\tau^2H, i.e. \mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C)=\tau^2p with p=1. They give variance \tau^2/n, excess risk \tau^2/2n and optimism \tau^2/n: all too small by a factor of 3. The noise is largest exactly where x is large, which is where the data have the most leverage on \hat\theta.

Numerical check (checks/wb2b_check.py): for a model misspecified in both mean and noise (y=\sin2x+(0.3+0.5|x|)\xi fitted linearly with an intercept), \mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C)=3.19 while the naive \sigma^2p=2.01. At n=200, over 2\times10^4 replications, the excess risk is 0.00812 against the formula’s 0.00799, and the optimism 0.01608 against 0.01597, a ratio of 1.98. The same check for a misspecified logistic regression (a non-quadratic loss, n=400) gives a ratio of 1.98.

4.A.4. When C=H: the Fisher identity and AIC

Why. For a probabilistic model trained with the log loss on data it actually generated, gradient noise equals curvature. Then \mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C)=p: every parameter costs the same, whatever the curvature, and the formulas reduce to the classical AIC.

  1. Let \ell(\theta;x,y)=-\log p_\theta(y\mid x), with the data generated by p_{\theta^*} (a well-specified model). Prove that C=H at \theta^* by differentiating \int p_\theta(y\mid x)\,dy=1 twice.
  2. Conclude that the optimism is p/n and the excess risk p/2n. Relate this to AIC =-2\log L(\hat\theta)+2p.
  3. For squared loss \ell=\tfrac12(y-f_\theta(x))^2 with y=f_{\theta^*}(x)+\varepsilon, \varepsilon independent of x and of variance \sigma^2, show that C=\sigma^2H, so \mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C)=\sigma^2p. Check this against the exact OLS result of 1.B.2 (excess risk \sigma^2p/(n-p-1) in the (y-f)^2 convention), minding the factor of two.
Solution

1. Write s=\nabla_\theta\log p_\theta (the score). Differentiating \int p_\theta\,dy=1 once gives 0=\int\nabla p_\theta=\int p_\theta\,s, so \mathbb E_{p_\theta}s=0. Differentiating again gives 0=\int\nabla^2p_\theta=\int p_\theta\big(\nabla^2\log p_\theta+ss^\top\big), using \nabla^2p/p=\nabla^2\log p+ss^\top. So \mathbb E_{p_\theta}[ss^\top]=-\mathbb E_{p_\theta}[\nabla^2\log p_\theta]. At \theta=\theta^* the data come from p_{\theta^*}, so averaging also over x gives C=\mathbb E[ss^\top]=\mathbb E[\nabla^2\ell]=H.

2. \mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C)=\mathrm{Tr}(I_p)=p. So the optimism is p/n and the excess risk, which here is the KL to the truth, is p/2n nats per sample. Estimating the expected test log loss by \hat R_n(\hat\theta_n)+p/n and multiplying by 2n gives -2\log L(\hat\theta)+2p: AIC is TIC with C=H assumed. This is “parameter counting”, and it is exact only for well-specified regular models.

3. \nabla\ell=-r\nabla f with r=y-f_\theta(x), and \nabla^2\ell=\nabla f\nabla f^\top-r\nabla^2f. At \theta^*, r=\varepsilon. So C=\mathbb E[\varepsilon^2\nabla f\nabla f^\top]=\sigma^2\mathbb E[\nabla f\nabla f^\top] by independence, and H=\mathbb E[\nabla f\nabla f^\top]-\mathbb E[\varepsilon\nabla^2f]=\mathbb E[\nabla f\nabla f^\top] since \mathbb E[\varepsilon\mid x]=0. Hence C=\sigma^2H.

For OLS with x\sim\mathcal N(0,I), this predicts excess risk \sigma^2p/2n in the \tfrac12 convention, i.e. \sigma^2p/n in 1.B’s (y-f)^2 convention. That matches the large-n limit of 1.B.2’s exact \sigma^2p/(n-p-1).

4.A.5. The Bayesian side: Laplace and BIC

Why. A Bayesian compares models by their evidence (marginal likelihood): the probability of the data averaged over the prior. To leading order it is the maximized likelihood penalized by \frac p2\log n, the BIC. The penalty counts how fast the posterior volume shrinks, which is also where singular learning theory departs from the regular case.

Take the log loss \ell=-\log p_\theta(y\mid x), so that the likelihood of the training set is L(\theta)=e^{-n\hat R_n(\theta)}; a prior density \pi that is continuous and positive at \theta^*; and the marginal likelihood (evidence) Z_n=\int\pi(\theta)\,e^{-n\hat R_n(\theta)}\,d\theta . The Laplace approximation evaluates such an integral for large n by Taylor-expanding \hat R_n to second order around its minimizer \hat\theta_n, with Hessian \hat H=\nabla^2\hat R_n(\hat\theta_n), and integrating the resulting Gaussian exactly.

  1. Use the Laplace approximation around \hat\theta_n to show that -\log Z_n=n\hat R_n(\hat\theta_n)+\frac p2\log n+\tfrac12\log\det\hat H-\frac p2\log2\pi-\log\pi(\hat\theta_n)+o(1). Hence -2\log Z_n=-2\log L(\hat\theta_n)+p\log n+O(1): BIC.
  2. AIC’s penalty is 2p and BIC’s is p\log n. They answer different questions. Which question does each answer?
Solution

1. Near \hat\theta_n, n\hat R_n(\theta)\approx n\hat R_n(\hat\theta_n)+\frac n2(\theta-\hat\theta_n)^\top\hat H(\theta-\hat\theta_n), and \pi(\theta)\approx\pi(\hat\theta_n) over the region of width O(n^{-1/2}) that dominates the integral. The Gaussian integral gives Z_n\approx\pi(\hat\theta_n)\,e^{-n\hat R_n(\hat\theta_n)}\,(2\pi)^{p/2}\det(n\hat H)^{-1/2}, and \det(n\hat H)=n^p\det\hat H. Taking -\log gives the stated expansion. Every term except the first two is O(1), so -2\log Z_n=-2\log L(\hat\theta_n)+p\log n+O(1).

2. AIC (and TIC) estimate the expected test loss of the plug-in predictor p_{\hat\theta_n}: a prediction question. BIC approximates -2\log of the evidence, so differences of BIC approximate log posterior odds between models: a model-identification question. The evidence penalizes each parameter by \tfrac12\log n because the posterior volume shrinks like n^{-p/2} relative to the prior (the Occam factor; WB4 §2). In singular models this volume scaling changes: \frac p2\log n becomes \lambda\log n with \lambda the real log canonical threshold (RLCT), the central quantity of singular learning theory (SLT). (This \lambda is not a ridge penalty.) In one sentence: with K(\theta)=R(\theta)-R(\theta^*), the prior mass of near-optimal parameters scales as \int_{K(\theta)<\epsilon}\pi(\theta)\,d\theta\propto\epsilon^{\lambda} as \epsilon\to0 (up to a power of \log(1/\epsilon)). In a regular model that set is an ellipsoid \tfrac12\delta^\top H\delta<\epsilon in p dimensions, with mass \propto\epsilon^{p/2}, so \lambda=p/2. Degenerate (flat) directions make the set fatter, which makes \lambda smaller than p/2.

4.A.6. Three estimands: a single fit, the Bayes ensemble, a posterior draw (optional)

Why. “The generalization error” of a Bayesian learner can mean three different things, and claims from Bayesian statistics and singular learning theory are about the second, while a trained network is closer to the first. In the regular case they differ only by constant factors; this exercise derives them.

Use the log loss, a model p_\theta(y\mid x), and a truth q(y\mid x); take the model to be well specified, q=p_{\theta^*}. Let \pi_n(\theta)\propto\pi(\theta)\prod_ip_\theta(y_i\mid x_i) be the posterior, and \bar p_n(y\mid x)=\int p_\theta(y\mid x)\,\pi_n(d\theta) the posterior predictive. With \mathbb E_D over the training sample:

  1. Single-network excess risk: \ \mathcal E=\mathbb E_D\,\mathbb E_x\,\mathrm{KL}\big(q\,\|\,p_{\hat\theta_n}\big)=\mathbb E_D R(\hat\theta_n)-R(\theta^*). One deterministic output of training.
  2. Bayes generalization error: \ B_g=\mathbb E_D\,\mathbb E_x\,\mathrm{KL}\big(q\,\|\,\bar p_n\big). The posterior-predictive ensemble.
  3. Gibbs generalization error: \ G_g=\mathbb E_D\,\mathbb E_{\theta\sim\pi_n}\,\mathbb E_x\,\mathrm{KL}\big(q\,\|\,p_\theta\big). One random draw from the posterior.

Each is an expected population loss minus the entropy R(\theta^*) of the truth, so all three are in nats per sample. “Gibbs” refers to the posterior \propto\pi\,e^{-n\hat R_n} being a Gibbs (Boltzmann) distribution, of which estimand 3 takes one sample. (Watanabe, Mathematical Theory of Bayesian Statistics, 2018, ch. 1–2, defines them without the outer \mathbb E_D, states results for their expectations, and treats the singular case. For the singular Bayes generalization error and WAIC, the “widely applicable” information criterion that estimates it in singular models, see also Watanabe (2010).)

Watanabe’s regular-case results. For a regular, well-specified model with the ordinary posterior (temperature T=1), \mathbb E\,B_g=\frac p{2n}+o(\frac1n) and \mathbb E\,G_g=\frac pn+o(\frac1n). Under the assumptions of singular learning theory (the truth is in the model, and the model is analytic in \theta), \mathbb E\,B_g=\frac\lambda n+o(\frac1n): the real log canonical threshold \lambda\le p/2 of 4.A.5 (the exponent of the prior mass of near-optimal parameters) replaces p/2.

Exercise.

  1. Show that B_g\le G_g always.
  2. In the regular case, approximate the posterior by \mathcal N\big(\hat\theta_n,(nH)^{-1}\big). Show that G_g\approx p/n.
  3. Show that B_g\approx p/2n, the same as \mathcal E to leading order.
  4. Without well-specification, define K^*=\mathbb E_x\mathrm{KL}(q\|p_{\theta^*}). Keep the same Laplace posterior and compute G_g-K^* in terms of \mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C). What does this say about the posterior’s width?
Hint

For 1, \mathrm{KL}(q\|\cdot) is convex in its second argument. For 2, write a posterior draw as \theta=\hat\theta_n+u, with \mathbb E[u\mid D]=0 and \delta=\hat\theta_n-\theta^* fixed given the data. For 3, expand \log\mathbb E_u\,p_{\hat\theta_n+u}(y\mid x) to second order in u, and use the Fisher identity of 4.A.4.

Solution

1. \bar p_n=\mathbb E_{\theta\sim\pi_n}p_\theta, and \mathrm{KL}(q\|\cdot) is convex, so by Jensen \mathrm{KL}(q\|\bar p_n)\le\mathbb E_{\theta\sim\pi_n}\mathrm{KL}(q\|p_\theta). Average over x and D.

2. For a draw \theta=\theta^*+\delta+u, the excess risk is \approx\tfrac12(\delta+u)^\top H(\delta+u). Given the data, u has mean zero, so the cross term vanishes in expectation, and G_g\approx\tfrac12\mathrm{Tr}\Big(H\big(\mathbb E\delta\delta^\top+\mathbb Euu^\top\big)\Big) =\tfrac12\mathrm{Tr}\big(H(H^{-1}/n+H^{-1}/n)\big)=\frac pn, using \mathbb E\delta\delta^\top=H^{-1}CH^{-1}/n=H^{-1}/n (since C=H). The draw pays the ERM’s error once and the posterior’s own spread once more.

3. Write \ell=-\log p_\theta, g=\nabla\ell(\hat\theta_n) and h=\nabla^2\ell(\hat\theta_n) at a given (x,y). Then \log\bar p_n=\log\mathbb E_u\,e^{-\ell(\hat\theta_n+u)} \approx-\ell(\hat\theta_n)+\log\mathbb E_u\,e^{-u^\top g-\frac12u^\top hu} \approx-\ell(\hat\theta_n)+\tfrac12\mathbb E_u\big[u^\top(gg^\top-h)u\big], to order 1/n, since \mathbb E u=0. With \mathbb Euu^\top=(nH)^{-1}, the correction is \frac1{2n}\mathrm{Tr}\big(H^{-1}(gg^\top-h)\big). Averaging over (x,y)\sim q near \theta^*, \mathbb E[gg^\top-h]=C-H=0 by the Fisher identity. So -\mathbb E\log\bar p_n=R(\hat\theta_n)+o(1/n), and B_g\approx\mathcal E\approx p/2n.

In the regular case the ensemble buys nothing at leading order. The three estimands differ by constant factors (\tfrac12,\tfrac12,1 times p/n). In singular models they come apart: B_g\approx\lambda/n, while these regular-case arguments do not determine the MLE’s leading error.

Numerical check (checks/wb2b_check.py), with linear-Gaussian regression, p=5, n=100, known noise and a flat prior, so that the posterior is exactly Gaussian: \mathcal E=0.0268 (exact finite-n value p/2(n-p-1)=0.0266), B_g=0.0258, G_g=0.0526, against p/2n=0.025 and p/n=0.05.

4. Now \mathbb E\delta\delta^\top=H^{-1}CH^{-1}/n while the posterior still has covariance H^{-1}/n, so G_g-K^*\approx\frac1{2n}\big(\mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C)+p\big). The approximation error K^* need not vanish under misspecification. The posterior’s width is set by H alone, but the actual sampling spread of \hat\theta_n is set by the sandwich. A misspecified posterior is overconfident when C\succ H and underconfident when C\prec H. This is one honest motivation for tempering.

4.A.7. Which estimand do practical tools measure?

Why. A claim like “flat minima generalize” or “weight decay is Bayesian” is only checkable once you know which of these quantities it is about.

The three estimands of 4.A.6 are: (1) the excess risk of a single fitted network, (2) the error of the posterior-predictive ensemble, and (3) the error of a single posterior draw.

For each of the following, say which of the three estimands it measures or controls, and in which regime the link is a theorem: held-out loss, early stopping, ridge (weight decay), and flatness \tfrac12\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma).

Solution
  • Held-out loss estimates the population loss of whatever predictor you evaluate: one trained network (estimand 1, up to the unknown constant R(\theta^*)), an ensemble (closer to 2), or a posterior sample (3). It is model-free and needs no regime assumption, which is why it is the practical standard.
  • Early stopping and ridge change the estimator; they do not choose an estimand. They act on the single-network excess risk by trading bias for variance (1.C–1.D). Ridge is MAP (maximum a posteriori: the posterior mode) under a Gaussian prior, which is neither the Bayes ensemble nor a Gibbs draw. (In linear-Gaussian models the posterior mean equals the MAP, so there it coincides with the ensemble’s mean prediction.)
  • Flatness \tfrac12\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma) approximates the mean local excess risk when \Sigma=\mathbb E[(\theta-\theta^*)(\theta-\theta^*)^\top]. This is a second moment, equal to the covariance only for a centered parameter. If \Sigma is the sampling covariance of \hat\theta_n, it is estimand 1 (4.A.2). If \Sigma is the posterior’s covariance, it is the posterior’s contribution to estimand 3. If \Sigma is SGD’s stationary covariance, it is a Gibbs-like error for the “SGD posterior” (4.B.3). Which one is meant has to be stated.
  • None of the regular-case formulas is a theorem for overparametrized networks trained by SGD: H can be singular, and the empirical gradient covariance can vanish at squared-loss interpolation even when the population covariance does not (4.B.1).

4.B. Does curvature cancel? Gradient noise, curvature, and SGD

The question. 4.A’s clean results lean on C\propto H: gradient noise proportional to curvature. Then a sharp direction is pinned down more tightly by the data in exactly the proportion that errors there cost more, so curvature cancels and only the parameter count matters. Does this hold for trained networks? And SGD adds noise of its own: around a minimum it does not converge but keeps jiggling, with a spread set by the learning rate and batch size. Is that spread like a Bayesian posterior, as is sometimes claimed?

What you get.

  • 4.B.1: per example, C and H are built from the same rank-one pieces, weighted differently. Where the network fits its training data (near interpolation), the gradient noise vanishes faster than the curvature.
  • 4.B.2: measured diagonals of real networks follow a power law C_{ii}\propto H_{ii}^\gamma with \gamma roughly 1–1.5 (in 4.B, \gamma is this exponent, unrelated to §3’s p/n; \gamma=1 is C\propto H, and \gamma=0 is noise that ignores curvature); a moment argument makes [1,2] natural, though it does not force a fitted exponent into it.
  • 4.B.3–4.B.5: on a quadratic basin, SGD’s stationary covariance solves a linear matrix equation (a Lyapunov equation). It has the shape of a (tempered) posterior only if the gradient noise is isotropic (\gamma=0), outside the measured range; for \gamma\ge1 SGD spreads at least as much along sharp directions as flat ones, the opposite of a posterior.

Setup, restated. Loss \ell(\theta;z), population risk R, minimizer \theta^* (shifted to 0 where convenient), curvature H=\nabla^2R(\theta^*), and gradient-noise covariance C=\mathrm{Cov}_z[\nabla\ell(\theta;z)] (at \theta^* unless stated). A parameter \theta^*+\delta with \mathbb E\,\delta\delta^\top=\Sigma has excess risk \approx\tfrac12\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma) (1.D.7). For ERM in the regular regime \Sigma\approx H^{-1}CH^{-1}/n (4.A.1). For a well-specified log loss, C=H at \theta^*, and for squared loss with independent noise, C=\sigma^2H. A tempered posterior at temperature T is \pi_T(\theta)\propto\pi(\theta)\,e^{-n\hat R_n(\theta)/T} (T=1 is the ordinary Bayesian posterior for the log loss); near a nondegenerate mode it is approximately Gaussian with covariance \frac TnH^{-1}, long in flat directions and narrow in sharp ones.

4.B.1. Same pieces, different weights

Why. To see when C\propto H can hold, write both matrices as averages over training examples and compare the pieces. In this exercise both are empirical: averages over the n training examples at the current parameter, which is what one can measure on a trained network.

Source: Zhang, Yang and Tu (reading in 4.B.6), §2.1, on why the Fisher approximation C\approx H fails.

  1. Squared loss. Let \ell_i=\tfrac12r_i^2 with residual r_i=f_\theta(x_i)-y_i, and v_i=\nabla f_\theta(x_i). Write the per-sample gradient g_i and the per-sample Gauss–Newton matrix h_i (the Hessian without the r_i\nabla^2f term). Show that h_i has rank at most one, and that over the training set H_{GN}=\frac1n\sum_ih_i,\qquad C=\frac1n\sum_ir_i^2h_i-\bar g\bar g^\top , where \bar g=\frac1n\sum_ig_i is the full-batch gradient and C is the empirical covariance of the g_i.
  2. At an interpolating minimum (r_i=0 for all i), what are C and H_{GN}? Away from interpolation, under what condition is C=\sigma^2H_{GN}?
  3. Logistic loss. With y_i\in\{0,1\}, s_i=\sigma(f_\theta(x_i)), where \sigma(t)=1/(1+e^{-t}) is the logistic sigmoid, and \ell_i=-y_i\log s_i-(1-y_i)\log(1-s_i), show that g_i=(s_i-y_i)v_i and that the per-sample Gauss–Newton matrix is h_i=a_iv_iv_i^\top with a_i=s_i(1-s_i). For a correctly classified example with large margin, compare the weights of v_iv_i^\top in g_ig_i^\top and in h_i. Which goes to zero faster as the margin grows?
Solution

1. g_i=\nabla\ell_i=r_iv_i and \nabla^2\ell_i=v_iv_i^\top+r_i\nabla^2f, so h_i=v_iv_i^\top, which has rank at most one (rank one when v_i\ne0). Averaging gives H_{GN}. The empirical gradient covariance is \frac1n\sum_ig_ig_i^\top-\bar g\bar g^\top=\frac1n\sum_ir_i^2v_iv_i^\top-\bar g\bar g^\top.

2. At interpolation every r_i=0, so every g_i=0 and C=0. But H_{GN}=\frac1n\sum v_iv_i^\top\neq0. So C\propto H fails completely: gradient noise vanishes in every direction while curvature does not. Away from interpolation, at the population optimum with r=-\varepsilon independent of x (hence of v) and \mathbb E\varepsilon=0: \bar g=0 and C=\mathbb E[\varepsilon^2]\,\mathbb E[vv^\top]=\sigma^2H_{GN}. The cancellation needs noise that is independent of where you are in input space, i.e. a well-specified model with homoscedastic noise.

3. \partial\ell_i/\partial f=s_i-y_i, so g_i=(s_i-y_i)v_i, and \partial^2\ell_i/\partial f^2=s_i(1-s_i), giving h_i=a_iv_iv_i^\top. For y_i=1 with margin f\to\infty: g_ig_i^\top has weight (1-s_i)^2\approx e^{-2f}, and h_i has weight a_i\approx e^{-f}. So g_ig_i^\top\approx a_i^2v_iv_i^\top: the noise weight is the square of the curvature weight. Both vanish as the network separates the data, but the noise vanishes faster, so C/H\to0 again. (If y were resampled from the model, \mathbb E_y(s-y)^2=s(1-s)=a and C=H: the Fisher identity. The mismatch comes from using the observed labels, which the model fits.)

4.B.2. The diagonal power law: why between 1 and 2?

Why. 4.B.1 shows C\propto H fails; the next question is what replaces it. The exponent in C_{ii}\propto H_{ii}^\gamma is the input 4.B.3 needs to predict the shape of SGD’s noise cloud.

Zhang, Yang and Tu (2026; reading in 4.B.6) argue that, beyond the Fisher-equals-Hessian setting, C\propto\mathbb E_i[h_i^2], where h_i is the Hessian of the loss on example i and \mathbb E_i averages over training examples. They also report that C and H=\mathbb E_i[h_i] approximately commute (CH\approx HC, i.e. they share eigenvectors) in their setting. Commutation is an additional property, not a general consequence of the second-moment relation. In the fully connected layers they analyse, they fit per-layer power laws C_{ii}\propto H_{ii}^\gamma, with fitted \gamma roughly 1.1–1.5 for cross-entropy and close to 1 for squared loss. Their §5 derives a pair of moment bounds and reads them as confining \gamma to [1,2]. This exercise derives the same kind of bounds in a simplified model and asks what they do and do not imply.

Take rank-one per-sample Hessians h_i=v_iv_i^\top as in 4.B.1.1, and the model C:=\mathbb E_i[h_i^2] (the constant of proportionality does not affect \gamma).

  1. Show that C_{jj}=\mathbb E_i\big[\|v_i\|^2v_{ij}^2\big] and H_{jj}=\mathbb E_i[v_{ij}^2].
  2. Prove the envelope H_{jj}^2\le C_{jj}\le G^2H_{jj}, where G^2=\max_i\|v_i\|^2, and note that H_{jj}\le G^2. Draw it on log–log axes.
  3. Suppose each example’s gradient lies along a single coordinate: v_i=s_{k}e_{k} for examples of “type” k, which occur with frequency \pi_k. Compute H_{kk} and C_{kk}. Show that \gamma=1 if curvature varies across coordinates only through the frequencies \pi_k, and \gamma=2 if only through the magnitudes s_k.
  4. Does the envelope force a fitted \gamma into [1,2]?
Hint

For the lower bound in 2, use \|v_i\|^2\ge v_{ij}^2, then Jensen (or Cauchy–Schwarz) on \mathbb E\,v_{ij}^4. For 3, each example contributes to exactly one coordinate.

Solution

1. h_i^2=v_iv_i^\top v_iv_i^\top=\|v_i\|^2v_iv_i^\top, whose (j,j) entry is \|v_i\|^2v_{ij}^2. Average over i. Similarly (h_i)_{jj}=v_{ij}^2.

2. Upper bound: \|v_i\|^2\le G^2, so C_{jj}\le G^2\,\mathbb E v_{ij}^2=G^2H_{jj}. Lower bound: \|v_i\|^2\ge v_{ij}^2, so C_{jj}\ge\mathbb E v_{ij}^4\ge(\mathbb Ev_{ij}^2)^2=H_{jj}^2 by Cauchy–Schwarz. Also H_{jj}\le\mathbb E\|v_i\|^2\le G^2. On log–log axes the points lie in a wedge between a line of slope 2 (below) and a line of slope 1 (above), and the two lines meet at H_{jj}=G^2.

3. H_{kk}=\pi_ks_k^2 and C_{kk}=\pi_ks_k^4. If s_k\equiv s and only \pi_k varies, C_{kk}=s^2H_{kk}: \gamma=1. If \pi_k\equiv\pi and only s_k varies, C_{kk}=H_{kk}^2/\pi: \gamma=2. Curvature that comes from how often a parameter is used gives \gamma=1; curvature that comes from how strongly it is used on the examples that use it gives \gamma=2. (Note that “all v_i parallel” gives \gamma=1, not 2: scaling every gradient by a per-example factor changes all coordinates proportionally.)

4. No. A cloud that sits on the slope-1 line at small H and on the slope-2 line near H=G^2 can be fitted with a slope below 1, and the reverse gives a slope above 2. The envelope makes [1,2] natural, and 3 shows both ends are attained by simple mechanisms. The measured range is an empirical fact about which mechanism dominates. The checks are in checks/wb2b_check.py.

4.B.3. SGD’s stationary law on a quadratic basin, and the posterior

Why. With a constant learning rate, SGD does not converge to the minimum but settles into a stationary cloud around it. Its covariance \Sigma sets SGD’s excess risk through \tfrac12\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma), and its shape decides whether “SGD samples a posterior” can be true.

Near the minimum (shifted to 0), take R(\theta)\approx\tfrac12\theta^\top H\theta with H\succ0, and SGD with learning rate \eta and batch size B: \theta_{k+1}=\theta_k-\eta\,(H\theta_k+\xi_k),\qquad\mathbb E\,\xi_k=0,\quad\mathrm{Cov}\,\xi_k=C/B, with \xi_k independent across steps and independent of \theta_k, and C fixed (state-independent near the minimum). Here \xi_k is the minibatch gradient’s deviation from the full gradient; averaging B examples divides the per-example covariance C by B. For discrete-time stationarity assume 0<\eta<2/\lambda_{\max}(H). The stationary covariance \Sigma is the covariance of \theta_k after the iteration has run long enough to forget its starting point, so that it no longer changes from step to step.

Two named objects appear. A Lyapunov equation is a linear matrix equation A\Sigma+\Sigma A^\top=Q for an unknown symmetric \Sigma; it is the equation for the stationary covariance of any linear system driven by noise. An Ornstein–Uhlenbeck (OU) process is the continuous-time version of such a system: a particle pulled linearly back toward 0 and kicked by Gaussian white noise, d\theta=-A\theta\,dt+Q^{1/2}dW, with W a standard Brownian motion. Noisy gradient descent on a quadratic basin, with small steps, is one.

Source: this stationary-distribution analysis, and the idea of tuning constant-step SGD so that its stationary law approximates a posterior, are in Mandt, Hoffman and Blei (2017).

  1. Show that the stationary covariance of the recursion satisfies \Sigma_\eta=(I-\eta H)\Sigma_\eta(I-\eta H)+\eta^2C/B, and that to leading order in \eta this is the Lyapunov equation H\Sigma+\Sigma H=\frac\eta B\,C . (It is also the stationary equation of the Ornstein–Uhlenbeck process d\theta=-H\theta\,dt+\sqrt{\eta/B}\,C^{1/2}dW, with t=\eta k.)
  2. Show that the Lyapunov equation has a unique solution for H\succ0. If C and H commute, show that \Sigma=\frac\eta{2B}CH^{-1}. With C=cH^\gamma, this is \Sigma=\frac{\eta c}{2B}H^{\gamma-1}.
  3. Show that \Sigma=\frac TnH^{-1} for some temperature T if and only if C\propto I. Do not assume that C and H commute. Find T.
  4. Show that \tfrac12\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma)=\frac{\eta\,\mathrm{Tr}\,C}{4B} exactly, for any C.
  5. With C=cH^\gamma, write three excess risks: ERM’s \frac1{2n}\mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C) from 4.A.2, SGD’s stationary value, and the tempered posterior’s \tfrac12\mathrm{Tr}(H\cdot\frac TnH^{-1}). What does each count?
  6. Preconditioning. Replace the update by \theta\leftarrow\theta-\eta P(H\theta+\xi) with P\succ0. Write the new Lyapunov equation. Assuming C\succ0, show that P=C^{-1} gives \Sigma=\frac\eta{2B}H^{-1}.
  7. For \gamma\in[1,2], compare the shapes of \Sigma and the posterior: along which directions does SGD spread more than the posterior? What would “SGD is approximately Bayesian” have to mean?
Hint

For 2, \Sigma=\int_0^\infty e^{-Ht}\,\frac\eta BC\,e^{-Ht}dt solves the equation; for uniqueness, work in H’s eigenbasis, where the equation reads (h_j+h_k)\Sigma_{jk}=\frac\eta BC_{jk}. For 3, substitute \Sigma=\kappa H^{-1}. For 4, take the trace.

Solution

1. \theta_k is independent of \xi_k, so \mathrm{Cov}\,\theta_{k+1}=(I-\eta H)\,\mathrm{Cov}\,\theta_k\,(I-\eta H)+\eta^2C/B. At stationarity, expanding gives \Sigma_\eta=\Sigma_\eta-\eta(H\Sigma_\eta+\Sigma_\eta H)+\eta^2H\Sigma_\eta H+\eta^2C/B, i.e. H\Sigma_\eta+\Sigma_\eta H=\frac\eta BC+\eta H\Sigma_\eta H. Since \Sigma_\eta=O(\eta), the last term is O(\eta^2); dropping it gives the Lyapunov equation. (Along an eigendirection of curvature h, the discrete law is wider by a relative O(\eta h), and diverges at \eta h\ge2.)

2. In the eigenbasis of H (eigenvalues h_j>0), the equation reads (h_j+h_k)\Sigma_{jk}=\frac\eta BC_{jk}, which has the unique solution \Sigma_{jk}=\frac\eta B\,C_{jk}/(h_j+h_k). If C commutes with H, C is diagonal in the same basis, so \Sigma_{jj}=\frac\eta{2B}C_{jj}/h_j, i.e. \Sigma=\frac\eta{2B}CH^{-1}. With C=cH^\gamma, \Sigma=\frac{\eta c}{2B}H^{\gamma-1}.

3. If \Sigma=\kappa H^{-1}, then H\Sigma+\Sigma H=2\kappa I, so \frac\eta BC=2\kappa I and C\propto I. Conversely, if C=cI then \Sigma=\frac{\eta c}{2B}H^{-1} solves the equation, and by uniqueness it is the solution. No commutation was assumed. Matching \frac{\eta c}{2B}=\frac Tn gives T=\frac{n\eta c}{2B}.

4. Take the trace of the Lyapunov equation: \mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma)+\mathrm{Tr}(\Sigma H)=2\,\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma)=\frac\eta B\mathrm{Tr}\,C. So \tfrac12\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma)=\frac{\eta\,\mathrm{Tr}\,C}{4B}, for any C, commuting or not.

5. With C=cH^\gamma:

excess risk counts
ERM (4.A.2) \frac c{2n}\mathrm{Tr}\,H^{\gamma-1} sampling noise in \hat\theta_n, each direction weighted by noise over curvature
SGD stationary \frac{\eta c}{4B}\mathrm{Tr}\,H^\gamma total gradient noise per step; curvature does not enter separately
posterior \frac{Tp}{2n} parameter count at temperature T; neither H nor C enters

At \gamma=1 (the Fisher case), ERM gives \frac{cp}{2n}, parameter counting, while SGD gives \frac{\eta c}{4B}\mathrm{Tr}H, which grows with sharpness. For this \gamma=1 comparison at fixed c, the SGD excess-risk value increases with curvature. This local calculation does not by itself determine how SGD selects between basins. Its noise scale is \eta/B, not 1/n.

6. The update is \theta\leftarrow(I-\eta PH)\theta-\eta P\xi. The same calculation gives PH\Sigma+\Sigma HP=\frac\eta BPCP. With P=C^{-1}, try \Sigma=\kappa H^{-1}: the left side is 2\kappa C^{-1} and the right side is \frac\eta BC^{-1}, so \kappa=\frac\eta{2B}. Preconditioning by the inverse noise covariance makes the continuous-time stationary covariance posterior-shaped when C\succ0. With Gaussian diffusion this also matches the Gaussian stationary law; covariance matching alone does not establish a Gaussian law for arbitrary discrete update noise. This is the idea behind using preconditioned SGD as an approximate posterior sampler.

7. With \gamma\in[1,2], \Sigma\propto H^{\gamma-1} ranges from isotropic (\gamma=1) to \propto H (\gamma=2). The posterior \propto H^{-1} is long in flat directions and narrow in sharp ones. SGD’s cloud is at least as wide in sharp directions as in flat ones: the opposite ordering. “SGD samples a (tempered) posterior” is the claim that \gamma\approx0, i.e. isotropic gradient noise, or that one has preconditioned by C^{-1}. Neither holds for plain SGD in the measured regime.

4.B.4. The best temperature, and a symmetry (optional)

Why. If SGD’s cloud \mathcal N(0,\Sigma) is not shaped like the posterior, how close can the best-tempered posterior \mathcal N(0,\frac TnH^{-1}) (the Laplace approximation of 4.B’s setup) get? The Kullback–Leibler divergence \mathrm{KL} measures the mismatch, and its minimum over T isolates the part that no choice of temperature can fix: the shape.

  1. Show that T^*=n\,\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma)/p minimizes \mathrm{KL}\big(\mathcal N(0,\Sigma)\,\|\,\mathcal N(0,\frac TnH^{-1})\big) over T, that the minimum is \mathrm{KL}^*=\tfrac12\big[p\log\big(\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma)/p\big)-\log\det(H\Sigma)\big]\ \ge0, with equality iff H\Sigma\propto I, and that at T=T^* the posterior’s excess risk \frac{T^*p}{2n} equals SGD’s \tfrac12\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma).
  2. In the commuting case with p=2 and H=\mathrm{diag}(1,\kappa), show that \mathrm{KL}^*=\log\cosh\big(\tfrac\gamma2\log\kappa\big), which is symmetric in \gamma\mapsto-\gamma. Is \mathrm{KL}^* symmetric in \gamma for p=3?
Hint

For zero-mean Gaussians, \mathrm{KL}(\mathcal N(0,\Sigma)\,\|\,\mathcal N(0,S))=\tfrac12\big[\mathrm{Tr}(S^{-1}\Sigma)-p+\log\det S-\log\det\Sigma\big]. Minimize over \log T, then write the result in terms of the eigenvalues of H\Sigma and compare their arithmetic and geometric means. For 2, in the commuting case H\Sigma\propto H^\gamma.

Solution

1. For Gaussians with mean zero, \mathrm{KL}(\mathcal N(0,\Sigma)\|\mathcal N(0,S))=\tfrac12[\mathrm{Tr}(S^{-1}\Sigma)-p+\log\det S-\log\det\Sigma]. With S=\frac TnH^{-1}: \mathrm{KL}(T)=\tfrac12\Big[\tfrac nT\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma)-p+p\log\tfrac Tn-\log\det(H\Sigma)\Big]. \partial_T\mathrm{KL}=\tfrac12[-\tfrac n{T^2}\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma)+\tfrac pT]=0 gives T^*=n\,\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma)/p; the function is convex in \log T, so this is the minimum. Substituting gives the stated \mathrm{KL}^*. With \mu_j the eigenvalues of H\Sigma (positive, since H\Sigma is similar to H^{1/2}\Sigma H^{1/2}), \mathrm{KL}^*=\frac p2[\log\bar\mu_{\rm arith}-\log\bar\mu_{\rm geom}]\ge0 by AM–GM, with equality iff all \mu_j are equal, i.e. H\Sigma\propto I. Finally, \frac{T^*p}{2n}=\tfrac12\mathrm{Tr}(H\Sigma). The KL-optimal temperature is also the one that matches excess risks.

2. Commuting case: H\Sigma\propto H^\gamma has eigenvalues \propto(1,\kappa^\gamma). With x=1, y=\kappa^\gamma, \mathrm{KL}^*=\log\frac{(x+y)/2}{\sqrt{xy}}=\log\cosh\big(\tfrac12\log\frac yx\big)=\log\cosh\big(\tfrac\gamma2\log\kappa\big), which is even in \gamma. For p=3 it is not symmetric in general: the arithmetic mean of \mu^\gamma and of \mu^{-\gamma}, each relative to its geometric mean, differ unless the log-spectrum is symmetric. For H with eigenvalues (1,2,30), \mathrm{KL}^*(1.5)=2.97 while \mathrm{KL}^*(-1.5)=1.88 (checks/wb2b_check.py). The interactive figure after 4.B.5 uses p=2, so the symmetry of its \mathrm{KL}^* readout is a feature of p=2.

4.B.5. A two-dimensional example

Why. To see the geometry of 4.B.3 at a glance: which way SGD’s cloud points compared with the posterior’s.

Take H=\mathrm{diag}(1,10): \theta_1 is flat and \theta_2 sharp. With gradient noise C=cH^\gamma, SGD’s stationary covariance is \Sigma=\frac{\eta c}{2B}H^{\gamma-1} (4.B.3.2) and the posterior’s is \frac TnH^{-1}. Use these to find the axes and aspect ratio of SGD’s \Sigma for \gamma=0,1,2, and compare each with the posterior’s \propto H^{-1}. Which way does each SGD ellipse point? What happens if C’s eigenbasis is rotated relative to H’s? The interactive figure below draws these ellipses.

Solution
  • \gamma=0: \Sigma\propto H^{-1}, elongated along the flat \theta_1 with aspect ratio \sqrt{10} in standard deviations. Same shape as the posterior.
  • \gamma=1: \Sigma=\frac{\eta c}{2B}I, a circle.
  • \gamma=2: \Sigma\propto H, elongated along the sharp \theta_2: rotated 90° from the posterior.
  • Rotating C’s eigenbasis tilts \Sigma off the axes. Since \Sigma\propto H^{-1} requires C\propto I (4.B.3.3), no temperature fixes the shape, and \mathrm{KL}^*>0 for every T.
  • The discrete-time ellipse is slightly wider than the continuous one along \theta_2, by a relative O(\eta h_2)=O(10\eta), where h_2=10 is the sharp curvature.

This is as far as WB2 goes. The Training Dynamics materials take up state-dependent noise, edge-of-stability effects and what happens away from a quadratic basin.

▶ Interactive figure: sgd_stationary_vs_posterior.html. Slide γ from 0 to 2 and watch the SGD ellipse rotate from the posterior’s shape through a circle to the opposite orientation. Then rotate the noise (φ) and check that no temperature recovers the posterior shape. The readout panel shows ½Tr(HΣ) = ηTr C/4B, T*, and the discrete-time correction.

4.B.6. Readings and rabbit holes

On the superlinear relationship between SGD noise covariance and loss landscape curvature, Yikuan Zhang, Ning Yang, Yuhai Tu (arXiv, 2026) Importance ●●●●○ Length ●●○○○

The assumption that SGD noise covariance is proportional to the Hessian holds only under restrictive conditions that deep networks typically violate. Using an activity–weight duality, they find C ∝ E_p[h_p²] instead. They also report that C and H approximately commute without coinciding (a separate property; see 4.B.2), and that the diagonals follow per-layer power laws C_ii ∝ H_ii^γ, with fitted γ near 1–1.5 and an argument that 1 ≤ γ ≤ 2. Read §2 (why the Fisher-equals-Hessian argument fails: compare 4.B.1) and skim §3, whose §3.4 derives C ∝ E[h_p²]. Then read §4 (the measured power laws, on MLPs and small CNNs on MNIST and CIFAR-10) and §5 (the moment bounds on γ, which are the envelope of 4.B.2, and why cross-entropy gives larger γ than squared loss). Note which layers the fits cover before carrying γ into 4.B.3.

A circuit prior in NN-Bayes, Kaarel Hänni, Dmitry Vaintrob (LessWrong, 2026) Importance ●○○○○ Length ●●○○○

Bayesian learning with neural networks has a circuit prior: when the target is computed by a small circuit, few samples suffice for good test accuracy, even for arbitrarily overparametrized networks, under particular prior scalings and caveats. A post with slides presenting a simplified version of the result, and open problems at the end. It is a statement about Bayesian learning (4.A.6), not about SGD. The comment thread discusses exactly that gap (some circuit-learnable functions, like sparse parities, are hard for gradient descent). Read it with 4.B.3.7 in mind.

4.C. Fantastic generalization measures

The question. Dozens of quantities have been proposed as explanations of why one network generalizes better than another: parameter norms, margins, flatness (the curvature H of the loss at the trained parameters), PAC-Bayes bounds (2.E), gradient noise (the per-example gradient covariance C). Which of them actually predict the generalization gap when you vary training in controlled ways? This subsection is one reading, plus an exercise connecting its findings to the theory of 1.D.7, 4.A and 4.B: which measures are invariant under reparametrization (changes of parameters that leave the network function unchanged), and why the assumptions behind TIC fail for the networks in the study. TIC, recall, estimates the generalization gap of a fitted model as \frac1n\mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C) (4.A.2), assuming fixed p, n\to\infty and a nondegenerate minimum.

Fantastic generalization measures and where to find them, Yiding Jiang, Behnam Neyshabur, Hossein Mobahi, Dilip Krishnan, Samy Bengio (ICLR 2020) Importance ●●●●● Length ●●●○○

A large controlled study: 2187 CIFAR-10 networks spanning 7 hyperparameters (over 10,000 networks with repeats and SVHN), all trained to the same training cross-entropy, 0.01, and more than 40 published complexity measures ranked by how well they predict the generalization gap. Many norm-based measures correlate negatively with the gap when the optimizer is stochastic, the product of layer spectral norms strongly so. Sharpness-based measures (PAC-Bayes bounds and Keskar et al.’s sharpness) do best overall, and measures of gradient noise and of optimization speed are also predictive. §2 defines the rank correlations, including a ‘granulated’ version that varies one hyperparameter at a time, and a conditional-independence test; §4 has the results; the appendix defines every measure.

4.C.1. Invariance, and the assumptions behind TIC
  1. Let \theta=A\phi with A invertible be a reparametrization that leaves the network function fixed (or the linearization of a smooth one at the minimum). Show that at the minimum H\to A^\top HA and C\to A^\top CA, and that \mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C) is invariant. Which of the parameter \ell_2 norm, the gradient variance \mathrm{Tr}\,C, \mathrm{Tr}\,H and the product of layer spectral norms are invariant under the layer rescaling of 1.D.7.3, and which under a general A?
  2. Jiang et al. train every model to the same cross-entropy. Why? Using 4.B.1, what does this do to the per-sample gradients and to \hat C? Which assumptions behind the derivation of TIC in 4.A.2 fail for these networks?
  3. Jiang et al. score each measure by rank correlation with the gap. Kendall’s \tau over a set of models is the fraction of pairs of models that the measure and the gap order the same way, minus the fraction they order oppositely; it lies in [-1,1], and 1 means the measure ranks the models exactly as the gap does. The pooled \tau uses all pairs of models. The granulated \Psi (their §2.2) computes \tau only over pairs that differ in a single hyperparameter, then averages over hyperparameters. Why might a measure have a high pooled \tau but a low \Psi?
Hint

For 1, per-sample gradients transform as \nabla_\phi\ell=A^\top\nabla_\theta\ell, and at a critical point the Hessian transforms as in 1.D.7.4. For 2, list the assumptions of 4.A’s regular regime (fixed p with n\to\infty, H\succ0, the trained parameter is the ERM) and check each.

Solution

1. Under a reparametrization \theta=A\phi with A invertible (or its linearization, for a smooth one) that leaves the function fixed, gradients transform as \nabla_\phi=A^\top\nabla_\theta, so H\to A^\top HA and C\to A^\top CA at the minimum. Then \mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C)\to\mathrm{Tr}(A^{-1}H^{-1}A^{-\top}A^\top CA)=\mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C): TIC is invariant. (More generally, the eigenvalues of H^{-1}C are.)

  • Not invariant: the parameter \ell_2 norm, the raw gradient variance \mathrm{Tr}\,C, and plain sharpness (\mathrm{Tr}\,H or a worst-case perturbation of fixed size).
  • Invariant to the layer rescaling of 1.D.7.3 but not to general reparametrizations: the product of layer spectral norms, since \|\alpha W_1\|\,\|W_2/\alpha\|=\|W_1\|\,\|W_2\| in a ReLU network.
  • Functions of the function: the margin, and the gap itself.

2. Fixing the training loss removes a trivial predictor: models with higher training loss have a different gap for uninteresting reasons. The price is that every model is near interpolation, where per-sample gradients are small (4.B.1). \hat C is then small and dominated by the hardest examples, and the regular-case theory behind \mathrm{Tr}(H^{-1}C) is furthest from applying. The assumptions that fail:

  • Interpolation (4.B.1): at exact squared-loss interpolation, the empirical \hat C is zero even though population gradient variability and test loss can remain positive. Plug-in TIC then loses its regular-regime justification. Jiang et al. stop at a small positive cross-entropy, not exact interpolation; for cross-entropy both curvature and gradient covariance can shrink, at different rates.
  • Singular H: overparametrized networks have flat directions, so H^{-1} is undefined and damped versions depend on \epsilon. The regular-case p/2 becomes the RLCT (4.A.5–4.A.6), which TIC does not see.
  • SGD is not ERM: the trained parameter’s spread is the stationary law of 4.B.3, set by \eta/B and \mathrm{Tr}\,C, not the sandwich. Learning-rate and batch-size axes move the gap through a channel TIC does not model. (\mathrm{Tr}\,C itself does model it, which is one reading of Jiang et al.’s gradient-variance result.)
  • Wrong loss: TIC is about the training loss. The 0–1 gap can move while the cross-entropy optimism does not, and conversely.
  • Ensembles: TIC is about a single fitted network. If what you report is an ensemble’s error, the leading constant can change (4.A.6).

Where the assumptions do roughly hold (p small compared with n, a nondegenerate minimum, training loss bounded away from zero, a trained parameter close to the ERM), comparisons within one architecture across data size or regularization strength are the natural use of TIC.

3. If one hyperparameter (say, width) moves both the measure and the gap strongly, the pooled \tau is high even if the measure tracks nothing within any fixed setting of the others. \Psi only counts comparisons where one hyperparameter changes, so a measure that just tracks one axis scores well on that axis and near zero on the others. A measure that predicts along one axis and not another is tracking the axis, not the gap.
